## 1.2. Scientific advice

The applicant sought scientific advice from the CHMP in 2018 (EMEA/H/SA/3790/1/2018/SME/II) and 2020 (EMEA/H/SA/3790/1/FU/1/2020/SME/II). These scientific advices are discussed in section 2.2. below.

## 1.3. Eligibility to the centralised procedure

The applicant Zealand Pharma A/S submitted on 28 May 2025 an application for marketing authorisation to the European Medicines Agency (EMA) for Zeydovio (glepaglutide), through the centralised procedure under Article 3 (2) (a) of Regulation (EC) No 726/2004. The eligibility to the

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

centralised procedure was agreed upon by the EMA/CHMP on 29 January 2021.

The applicant applied for the following indication: Treatment of adults with Short Bowel Syndrome (SBS).

## 1.4. Legal basis

## The legal basis for this application refers to:

Article 8.3 of Directive 2001/83/EC - complete and independent application

The application submitted is composed of administrative information, complete quality data, and nonclinical and clinical data based on applicant's own tests and studies and bibliographic literature substituting/supporting certain test(s) or study(ies).

## 1.5. Information on paediatrics

Pursuant to Article 7 of Regulation (EC) No 1901/2006, the application included an EMA decision(s) P/0455/2021 on the agreement of a paediatric investigation plan (PIP).

At the time of submission of the application, the PIP P/0455/2021 had not yet been completed as some measures had been deferred.

The PDCO issued on 15 November 2024 a positive partial compliance check for PIP P/0455/2021, in relation to studies 1 and 2 of the PIP.

Table 1: The compliance status with regards to the agreed PIP

## 1.6. Information on orphan market exclusivity

## 1.6.1. Similarity with authorised orphan medicinal products

Pursuant to Article 8 of Regulation (EC) No 141/2000 and Article 3 of Commission Regulation (EC) No 847/2000, the applicant did not submit a critical report addressing the possible similarity with authorised orphan medicinal products from the start of the procedure because there is no authorised orphan medicinal product for a condition related to the proposed indication.

## 1.7. Applicant's request(s) for consideration

## 1.7.1. New active substance status

The applicant requested the active substance glepaglutide contained in the above medicinal product to be considered as a new active substance, as the applicant claims that it is not a constituent of a medicinal product previously authorised within the European Union.

## 1.7.1.1. CHMP recommendation on new active substance status

Based on the review of available data on the active substance, the CHMP considers that glepaglutide is to be qualified as a new active substance in itself, as it is not a constituent of a medicinal product previously authorised within the European Union.

## 1.10.3. CHMP opinion

Based on the CHMP review of data on quality, safety and efficacy, the CHMP considers by consensus that the benefit-risk balance of Zeydovio is favourable in the following indication(s):

Zeydovio is indicated for the treatment of adults with Short Bowel Syndrome (SBS). Patients should be stable following a period of intestinal adaptation after surgery.

The CHMP, therefore, recommends the granting of the marketing authorisation subject to the conditions described in the following sections.

## 2. Introduction

## 2.1. Therapeutic context

## Condition

Patients with short bowel syndrome (SBS, defined as &lt;200 cm of functional small intestine (Buchman et al. 2003, Pironi et al. 2016, Pironi et al. 2018) are characterized by reduced intestinal absorptive surface area due to extensive surgical bowel resection or congenital diseases (Buchman et al. 2003, Jeppesen 2014, Pironi et al. 2015, Ladefoged et al. 1996). The resulting decreased absorptive capacity of the gut leads to reduced uptake of nutrients, fluids, electrolytes, vitamins and trace elements. This impairs the ability of affected individuals to maintain fluid and metabolic balances when receiving a conventional diet and can lead to dehydration, malnutrition and weight loss if left untreated (O'Keefe et al. 2006, Tee et al. 2011). Many patients with SBS also suffer from severe diarrhoea as a consequence of their impaired absorption, and SBS patients experience a greater frequency of bowel movements compared to healthy individuals with a complete bowel (Olieman et al. 2012). Moreover, patients with more severe malabsorption require a high food/nutrient and fluid intake, which in turn increases their intestinal output (Carllson et al. 2001).

SBS is a rare condition, with a prevalence in the EU well below 100,000 cases. The actual prevalence of SBS has not been fully clarified, primarily due to under-reporting and the absence of reliable patient databases. Attesting to the uncertainty in prevalence estimates, the prevalence of SBS in European countries has been reported within a wide range from 0.4 cases per million citizens in Poland to 40 cases per million citizens in Denmark (Massironi et al. 2020).

SBS is a highly heterogenous disease, in part due to variability with respect to the location and extent of resection as well as the degree of adaption of the residual bowel (Pironi et al. 2016, O'Keefe et al. 2006). Some SBS patients are able to adapt physiologically and compensate for their malabsorption by increasing their oral intake (also referred to as intestinal insufficiency; SBS-II). More severely affected individuals depend upon the safe and well-adjusted provision of parenteral support (PS) consisting of nutrients, fluids, electrolytes, vitamins and trace elements to maintain body function, growth, homeostasis and health (also referred to as intestinal failure; SBS-IF)(Pironi et al. 2016).

## Available therapies and unmet medical need

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Parenteral support (PS) via a central venous catheter is the primary and life-saving nutritional therapy for SBS patients, who are unable to adapt physiologically and compensate for their malabsorption by increasing their oral intake. Complications to parenteral nutrition therapy includes catheter-related blood stream infections and deep vein thrombosis, intestinal failure associated liver disease (IFALD), biliary disorders such as cholecystitis and gallstone formation and impaired quality of life (QoL). (Jeppesen et al. 2014, Pironi et al. 2021). For adult patients with bowel lengths between 60 and 200 cm with an ostomy bag, the amount of required parenteral support may result in up to 10 liters of stoma output per day, with emptying of the ostomy bag up to 15 times per day and 3 times per night (Carssson et al. 2001). Adding to the treatment burden, patients on parenteral support often need frequent follow-up checks at the hospital, and for many there is a need for help from a homecare nurse.

Surgical interventions aim to increase bowel length, potentially leading to bowel autonomy and weaning from PS.

Symptomatic pharmacological therapy for SBS aims at optimising the remnant colon function, and include antisecretory drugs (H2-receptor antagonists and proton pump inhibitors [PPI's]) and antimotility drugs (e.g. loperamide, codeine, diphenoxylate-atropine and opium) which can decrease diarrhoea and the loss of electrolytes (Pironi et al. 2021, Pironi et al. 2023). In addition, occasional antibiotic treatment is recommended for SBS patients who have motility disorders (including those with dilated segments of residual small bowel, blind loop etc.) and who suffer from symptoms of bacterial overgrowth (Cuerda et al 2021). Therapy directed at SBS includes intestinotrophic hormones stimulating intestinal mucosal growth. Two treatments are currently approved for SBS patients: The growth hormone somatotropin (only approved in US) and the GLP-2 analogue teduglutide (marketed as Revestive in the EU and as Gattex in the US).

The introduction of glucagon-like peptide-2 (GLP-2) hormone-based treatment has been a paradigm shift in the treatment of SBS, aiming to reinforce the structural and functional integrity of the remaining intestine. GLP-2 is a specific, endogenous intestinal growth factor that plays a key role in enhancing intestinal mucosal morphology, function, and integrity under normal and pathophysiological conditions. Additional reported effects of GLP-2 include inhibition of gastric motility and gastric acid hypersecretion, stimulation of nutrient absorption, enhancement of intestinal barrier function, and increased intestinal blood flow (Wallis et al. 2007, Hvistendahl et al. 2020, Meier et al. 2006, Wojdemann et al. 1999, Benjamin 2000, Au et al. 2002, Cheeseman 1997, Guan et al. 2003). Degradation of GLP-2 by the enzyme dipeptidyl peptidase IV (DPP IV) occurs rapidly, with subsequent clearance via the kidney (Tavares et al. 2000). The short half-life of 5-7 minutes for circulating native GLP-2 (Hartmann et al. 2000) is a major drawback for its use in a therapeutic setting, which is improved for the currently marketed GLP-2 analogue teduglutide with a half-life in circulation of approximately 2 hours (Kim &amp; Keam 2017). Teduglutide medicinal product Revestive (EU/1/12/787/001-002) is indicated for the treatment of patients 4 months corrected gestational age and above with short bowel syndrome (SBS). It requires daily injections.

Glepaglutide is a potent, long-acting GLP-2 analogue in a stable, aqueous formulation for subcutaneous (SC) administration. The stability in aqueous solution enables glepaglutide to be prepared as a readyto-use, liquid formulation with a shelf life of 30 months at 2-8°C and in-use storage at room temperature (25°C) for up to 2 weeks. The proposed dose is 10 mg twice weekly (TW), injected SC via a ready-to-use, single-dose auto-injector.

## 2.2. Aspects of development

Six different formulations of glepaglutide (formulations A to F) have been used during the clinical development of glepaglutide. The to-be-marketed formulation (formulation D: 20 mg/ml glepaglutide) was used in studies 18131, 19045, 15073 and all Phase 3 studies, whereas a lower glepaglutide formulation strength of 10 mg/ml (formulation F) was used in the Phase 1 PK study 16182.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The glepaglutide clinical development program for the indication of treatment of SBS consists of four phase 1 studies, one phase 2 trial and four phase 3 studies.

## Scientific advice

The glepaglutide clinical development programme has been discussed with CHMP in scientific advice twice.

- CHMP Scientific advice 2018 (EMEA/H/SA/3790/1/2018/SME/II): CHMP advice was provided relating to the pharmacology and clinical development programme. The CHMP agreed on a restricted pharmacology programme. According to the CHMP this is justified by the character of the active substance, and the assumed degradation pathways not being able to be influenced by, or influence the pharmacokinetics and pharmacodynamics of other compounds. With regards to the Phase 3 clinical development programme, several issues were highlighted. According to CHMP, it is unclear whether the selected glepaglutide dosing is acceptable. It was advised to investigate PK data with a population pharmacokinetic approach due to the high inter-individual variability. In addition, due to lack of information regarding PK of twice weekly dosing of 10 mg, it was deemed necessary to take more PK samples than only at trough during the Phase 3 study. According to the CHMP, the clinical meaningfulness of the primary endpoint and other volume related endpoints is unclear. Whereas a 1- or more days free from parenteral support could be regarded as clinically relevant, the 20% reduction of volume is seen with criticism. The CHMP indicated that the applicant could consider the continuous variable \"change from baseline in weekly parenteral support change\" as the primary endpoint.

The proposed algorithm for parenteral support volume reduction includes a single criterion, i.e. daily urine volume of the current visit at least 10% higher than baseline urine volume. According to the CHMP, it is not completely clear what \"daily urine volume of the current visit\" means. Under the assumption that this criterion refers to the 48-hour balance studies (i.e. 48 h collection of urine output with standardized oral liquid intake) the CHMP advised standardising the parenteral liquid intake during the referenced time period (i.e. 48 h), to minimise the bias of the parenteral support input in the patients, not receiving parenteral support daily.

The CHMP advised the applicant to consider inclusion of a teduglutide arm to enable better contextualisation of the long-term efficacy.

- Follow-up CHMP Scientific advice 2020 (EMEA/H/SA/3790/1/FU/1/2020/SME/II): The applicant indicated that the recruitment of the pivotal study ZP1848-17111 halved from 4/month to 2/month during the Covid-19 pandemic since the onset of pandemic-related restrictions. Because of this, the applicant proposes an unblinded interim-analysis after 90 (of the planned 129) study patients have passed the 6 months end-of-study visit of the study. The CHMP did not agree with the applicant's proposal because of the proposed reduction in data quantity, a potential reduction in data quality or damaging of the study integrity.

## 2.3. Description of the product

Zeydovio 10 mg solution for injection in pre-filled pen contains 10 mg of active substance glepaglutide (as acetate) in 0.5 ml clear, colourless to slightly coloured solution.

## Pharmacological Class

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Pharmacotherapeutic group: Other alimentary tract and metabolism products, various alimentary tract and metabolism products.

Proposed ATC code: A16AX26

## Mechanism of action

Glepaglutide is a long-acting analogue of native human GLP-2, a peptide secreted by L-cells. L-cells have the highest concentration per surface area in the distal ileum and colon. GLP-2 possesses intestinotrophic properties and is known to increase intestinal and portal blood flow, inhibit gastric acid secretion, and intestinal motility. GLP-2 binds to the GLP-2 receptors located in intestinal subpopulations of enteroendocrine cells, subepithelial myofibroblasts and enteric neurons of the submucosal and myenteric plexus. Activation of these receptors results in the local release of multiple mediators including insulin-like growth factor - 1 (IGF-1), nitric oxide and keratinocyte growth factor (KGF).

## Proposed Clinical Use

Treatment of adults with Short Bowel Syndrome (SBS).

## 3. Quality aspects

## 3.1. Introduction

The finished product is presented as solution for injection in pre-filled pen containing 10 mg of glepaglutide. The product contains the acetate salt.

Other ingredients are L-histidine, mannitol, water for injections, L-arginine (for pH adjustment), acetic acid (for pH adjustment).

The product is available in a glass (type I glass) pre-filled syringe with attached stainless 29-gauge steel needle, rigid needle shield (polypropylene) and rubber plunger (bromobutyl).

The pre-filled syringe is assembled into a disposable pre-filled pen made of polycarbonate (PC), polyoxymethylene (POM) and acrylonitrile butadiene styrene (ABS), as described in section 6.5 of the SmPC.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Each pre-filled pen contains 0.5 ml of solution for injection.

## 3.2. Active substance

## 3.2.1. General information

Glepaglutide is a synthetic peptide and is isolated as an acetate salt.

The chemical name is L-histidyl-glycyl-L-glutamyl-glycyl-L-threonyl-L-phenylalanyl-L-seryl-L-seryl-L-glutamyl-L-leucyl-L-alanyl-L-threonyl-L-isoleucyl-L-leucyl-L-aspartyl-L-alanyl-L-leucyl-L-alanyl-L-alanyl-L-arginyl-L-aspartyl-L-phenylalanyl-L-isoleucyl-L-alanyl-L-tryptophanyl-L-leucyl-L-isoleucyl-L-alanyl-L-threonyl-L-lysyl-L-isoleucyl-L-threonyl-L-aspartyl-L-lysyl-L-lysyl-L-lysyl-L-lysyl-L-lysyl-L-lysinamide, acetate corresponding to the molecular formula C197H325N53O55 (as free base). It has an average molecular mass of 4316.0 g/mol (as free base) and the following structure:

Figure 1: glepaglutide structure

<!-- image -->

The active substance is a white to off-white powder, very hygroscopic, and soluble in water. The peptide is isolated by lyophilisation, which results in an amorphous powder with no crystalline or polymorphic forms identified to date.

Structural characterisation of glepaglutide was performed using the following methods: sequence of amino acids (by MS/MS), NMR spectroscopy (monoisotopic molecular mass (by MS), amino acid analysis (Ph. Eur. 2.2.56), chiral amino acid analysis (by GC-MS), identity and amount of counter ion (by LC-UV)), confirming the expected chemical structure/primary sequence of glepaglutide.

The structural characterisation of glepaglutide with respect to secondary, tertiary, quaternary structure and the importance of these higher order structures on biological activity was not discussed in the initial submission. Also, the batches used for elucidation of structure were not manufactured by the proposed commercial process which is important for highly complex molecular structures with potential for higher order structures. Therefore, the CHMP raised a major objection (MO1) which was adequately addressed by information on the secondary structure as well as a discussion on potential higher order structures.

The solid-state properties of the active substance were measured by XRPD analysis confirming the amorphous state. Furthermore, data for biological activity were provided, demonstrating comparable results for the clinical phase 3, process validation/primary stability and commercial batches.

Glepaglutide comprises 39 natural L-amino acids. It has a total of 37 stereocenters in the peptide backbone and exists as a single stereoisomer. The chiral purity is controlled in the amino acid starting materials, and diastereoisomeric purity of the active substance is routinely controlled by HPLC.

## 3.2.2. Manufacture, characterisation, and process controls

Detailed information on the manufacturing of the active substance has been provided in the restricted part of the ASMF and it was considered satisfactory.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The active substance is manufactured at one manufacturing site. Satisfactory evidence of GMP compliance has been provided.

Glepaglutide is synthesised using standard solid phase peptide synthesis (SPPS) from well-defined starting materials (protected amino acids and a protected dipeptide) with acceptable specifications. Following the repeated addition of starting materials via SPPS, the crude peptide is cleaved from the resin support with simultaneous deprotection, subjected to purification, desalination, isolated by lyophilisation and finally packaged.

Reprocessing may be applied if a batch does not fulfil the acceptance criteria of the active substance specification, by repeating all or part of the purification stage and lyophilisation stage.

The active substance manufacturing process has been adequately described. Critical process steps and intermediates are defined. Proven acceptable ranges (PARs) have been established for the critical process parameters and the in-process controls along with acceptance criteria are described for each process step. The available development data, the proposed control strategy and batch analysis data from commercial scale batches fully support the proposed PARs.

The specifications and control methods for intermediate products, starting materials and reagents have been presented.

The characterisation of the active substance and its impurities are in accordance with the EU guideline on chemistry of new active substances.

Potential and actual impurities were well discussed with regards to their origin and characterised.

The initial mutagenic impurities risk assessment was incomplete, and the CHMP raised a major objection (MO2) requesting the full report on the potential genotoxic impurities, hazard assessment of those impurities, and justification of control strategy and calculated purge factors for potential mutagenic impurities. In response, the applicant provided a comprehensive risk assessment in line with ICH M7 which included classification of impurities, justification of the option 4 control strategy including purge factor calculations and analytical data. The assessment provided by the applicant was considered adequate, as is the proposed control strategy.

The quality of the active substance used in the various phases of the development is considered to be comparable with that of the proposed commercial process. The only observed relevant difference is a decrease in peptide-related impurities seen during process development, which improves the quality of the active substance.

The active substance is packaged in a primary packaging that complies with EU Commission Regulation (EU) 10/2011, as amended.

## 3.2.3. Specification

The active substance specification includes tests for appearance, appearance of solution (Ph. Eur.), identity (MS, MS/MS, LC-UV), peptide-related impurities (LC-UV), chiral impurities (GC-MS), total impurities (calculation), assay of glepaglutide (LC-UV), glepaglutide content (calculation), sulfated ash (Ph. Eur.), water content (KF), pH (Ph. Eur.), residual solvents (GC), microbial enumeration tests (TAMC, TYMC, Ph. Eur.), bacterial endotoxins (Ph. Eur.).

The active substance specification is appropriately justified. Tests and acceptance criteria are based on the general Ph. Eur. monograph 'Substances for Pharmaceutical Use (2034)' for peptide-related impurities, general Ph. Eur. requirements, ICH Q3C guideline, batch analysis and stability results.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The initially proposed identity tests (MS and LC-UV) were not sufficient to confirm the exact peptide sequence. Therefore, the CHMP raised a major objection (MO3) which was resolved by including a test based on tandem mass spectrometry (MS/MS) which allows confirmation of the expected peptide sequence.

In the initial round the applicant was asked to substantiate the absence of impurities under the main peak of glepaglutide with validated results of peak purity analysis. The additional peak purity investigation revealed an impurity under the main peak. Therefore, the CHMP raised a major objection (MO4) to request the applicant to include a test for this impurity in the active substance specification with a justified acceptance limit. This was adequately addressed by inclusion of the required test in the specification with a justified limit.

During the procedure the acceptance limit for water content was questioned. The applicant made a commitment to re-evaluate the acceptance criterion once additional commercial-scale release data from 30 batches become available. If this assessment supports any changes, the updated acceptance criterion and control strategy will be submitted through an appropriate post-approval variation. This commitment has been agreed and is put forward as a quality recommendation. (REC)

The analytical methods used have been adequately described and (non-compendial methods) appropriately validated in accordance with the ICH guidelines. Satisfactory information regarding the reference standards used for assay and impurities testing has been presented.

The HPLC method for identity, assay and peptide-related impurities was not considered suitably validated for the parameter's accuracy, LOQ and range. Therefore, the CHMP raised a major objection (MO5) which the manufacturer resolved by performing an additional validation study that suitably addressed ICH requirements.

Batch analysis data are provided of the active substance used in the non-clinical and clinical studies, primary stability study, process validation and commercial production. All results were compliant with the specification applicable at the time of testing. A reprocessed batch did not comply with the commercial specification and showed out-of-specification results for impurities. However, the root cause for this was identified and the control strategy was amended accordingly.

## 3.2.4. Stability

Stability data of active substance from the proposed manufacturer stored in a container closure system representative of that intended for the market for up to 60 months under long term conditions (-20 °C ± 5 °C) and for up to 6 months under accelerated conditions (25 °C / 60% RH) according to the ICH guidelines were provided.

The following parameters were tested: appearance, peptide related impurities, assay of glepaglutide, water, microbial enumeration and bacterial endotoxins. The analytical methods used were the same as for release and are stability indicating.

All tested parameters were within the specifications under long-term conditions. An increase in peptide-related impurities and decrease in assay were observed under accelerated conditions, with out-of-specifications for peptide-related impurities in all primary stability batches from three months onwards, and in the reprocessed batch from one month onwards.

Supportive stability data was also provided from batches stored for 60 months under long term conditions (-20 °C ± 5 °C) and for up to 6 months under accelerated conditions (25 °C / 60% RH) according to the ICH guidelines. These batches were tested using the HPLC purity method applicable at the time of testing and showed similar stability profiles to the primary stability batches. Comparison of the analytical methods for purity showed that the proposed HPLC method was at least equivalent to the previous one used.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Results under stressed conditions (heat, humidity, acid, base, oxidation, and light conditions) were also provided on one batch. The results showed that glepaglutide degraded in a manner as expected for each condition tested and in line with known degradation pathways for peptides in general. The active substance is sensitive to light.

The stability results indicate that the active substance manufactured by the proposed supplier is sufficiently stable. The stability results justify the proposed retest period of 48 months when stored at -20 °C ± 5 °C in the proposed container.

## 3.3. Finished medicinal product

## 3.3.1. Description of the product and pharmaceutical development

The finished product is a clear, colourless to slightly coloured, sterile, unpreserved, aqueous solution for subcutaneous administration. It is presented in a 1 mL single-dose pre-filled syringe (PFS) assembled into a single-use autoinjector and contains 10 mg glepaglutide in 0.5 ml solution.

The pharmaceutical development for the finished product contains QbD elements, however no design space was claimed. A quality target product profile (QTPP) was presented to guide the formulation of a product that is suitable for the intended use. Critical quality attributes (CQAs) were identified based on risk assessment and the requirements of the QTPP.

The CQAs identified were identity, content of glepaglutide, extractable volume, pH, tonicity, peptiderelated impurities, non-peptide related impurities, peptide related particles, appearance of solution, sterility, bacterial endotoxins and autoinjector compatibility.

The physicochemical characteristics of the active substance that could impact the finished product quality and manufacturing process were provided. The active substance is soluble in water at the target concentration. Under certain conditions, deamidation, isomerisation, hydrolysis and oxidation could occur in glepaglutide. A histidine buffer was selected to maintain the target pH of the formulation at 7.0 which provides optimal chemical and physical stability.

All excipients are well known pharmaceutical ingredients, and their quality is compliant with Ph. Eur. standards. There are no novel excipients used in the finished product formulation. The list of excipients is included in section 6.1 of the SmPC. The compatibility of the active substance with the selected excipients was demonstrated during development studies and further supported with finished product stability studies.

A bridging study demonstrated pK comparability between subcutaneous administration of 10 mg glepaglutide via autoinjector versus vial/syringe configuration. The formulation and presentation used during phase 3 and bridging studies is the same as that intended for marketing.

The finished product does not contain formula overages. However, an overfill is required to ensure that the nominal extractable volume is correctly discharged from the autoinjector containing the syringe.

The manufacturing process is a common process for aqueous sterile finished products which cannot be terminally sterilised. The proposed process consists of compounding of the bulk solution, sterile filtration, aseptic filling, stoppering and visual inspection. Subsequently the PFS is assembled into the autoinjector.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The choice of sterile filtration with aseptic filling into pre-sterilised container closure system has been adequately justified since glepaglutide was shown to be susceptible to heat degradation in a steam sterilisation cycle with an F0 lethality input that was significantly below the minimum EU requirements for steam sterilisation.

The formulation and manufacturing development have been evaluated through the use of risk assessment to identify the critical process steps and process parameters that may have an influence on the finished product quality attributes. The risk identification was based on lab-scale experiments, prior knowledge, process design and scale-up studies. PARs have been established for critical process parameters during compounding, sterile filtration and filling. The available development data, the proposed control strategy and batch analysis data from commercial scale batches fully support the proposed PARs.

The primary container closure system is a PFS constituted of a 1 mL syringe barrel made of clear type I glass, with a staked stainless-steel needle (29 G, 12.7 mm) protected with a rigid needle shield, which is sealed with a grey bromobutyl and silicon oil coated rubber plunger. The materials comply with Ph. Eur. requirements. The PFS is assembled into a single-use autoinjector.

The choice of the container closure system has been adequately justified with information on safety, compatibility, extractables/leachables, container closure integrity, autoinjector performance and stability data.

The suitability of the staked needle and its length was questioned since a risk for intramuscular injection was identified. In addition, no information was provided on potential aggregation and degradation of the peptide in the needle during injection. Therefore, the CHMP raised a major objection (MO6) and the applicant adequately resolved these points by 1) submitting sufficient analytical data demonstrating that quality of the finished product is not impacted by expelling it through the needle, 2) clarifying that the needle protrusion upon activation of the autoinjector carries no risk of intramuscular injection, and the device performance test for needle extension was included as a routine test in the finished product specification.

The components of the primary packaging are supplied ready-to-use since they are sterilised by the suppliers. Initially, the documentation on the sterilisation of the primary container closure components was not complete. The CHMP raised a major objection (MO7) since the choice for radiation instead of heat sterilisation for the plunger was not justified, a description of the sterilisation cycle and validation data were not provided for the syringe and plunger, and a sterility test was not included in the specification of the plunger. In response to this MO, the sterilisation method for the plunger was changed from gamma irradiation to steam sterilisation and a test for sterility was included in the specification. For the plunger, the applied sterilisation cycle exceeds the reference conditions of the Ph. Eur., hence, no validation data was required to be included in the dossier. The syringes are sterilised using ethylene oxide and sufficient validation data have been provided. Based on the updated documentation the MO was considered resolved.

The finished product falls under the second subparagraph of Article 1(9) of the Medical Devices Regulation (EU) 2017/745 (MDR). The medical device (parts) and the medicinal product form an integral product which is intended exclusively for use in the given combination, and which is not reusable. In accordance with Article 117 of the MDR, a Notified Body Opinion has been provided which confirms conformity of the autoinjector device parts with the relevant GSPRs set out in Annex I of the Regulation (EU) 2017/745.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 3.3.2. Manufacture of the product and process controls

Satisfactory evidence of GMP compliance has been provided for the manufacturing sites and batch release site.

The batch formula for the defined batch size was provided.

The manufacturing process consists of the following main steps: compounding of the bulk solution, sterile filtration, aseptic filling in glass syringe and plunger insertion, visual inspection, storage, final assembly of the autoinjector unit, labelling and packaging. The process is considered to be a nonstandard manufacturing process due to the aseptic processing.

The critical steps defined in the manufacturing process are compounding, sterile filtration, filling and visual inspection. The in-process controls are adequate for this type of manufacturing process and pharmaceutical form. The critical process times and holding times have been established and these were justified with development and process validation studies.

The manufacturing process has been validated on three consecutive full scale process validation batches of the prefilled syringe (PFS) and the autoinjector. Filter validation of the sterilising filters is reported in line with relevant EMA guidance. It has been demonstrated that the manufacturing process is capable of producing the finished product of intended quality in a reproducible manner.

## 3.3.3. Product specification

The finished product release and shelf-life specifications include appropriate tests for this kind of dosage form: appearance of solution (Ph. Eur.), visible particles (Ph. Eur.), identity (HPLC), assay (HPLC), impurities (HPLC), pH (Ph. Eur.), osmolality (Ph. Eur.), uniformity of dosage units (Ph. Eur.), sub-visible particles (Ph. Eur.), sterility (Ph. Eur.), container closure integrity (USP, dye ingress test), bacterial endotoxins (Ph. Eur.), dose accuracy (ISO), cap removal force (ISO), activation force (ISO), needle extension (ISO), injection time (ISO).

The finished product release and shelf-life specification is justified with reference to relevant ICH guidelines (ICHQ6A), Ph. Eur./USP requirements, ISO standards, batch release and stability data.

Peptide-related impurities present above the qualification threshold according to the general Ph. Eur. monograph 'Substances for Pharmaceutical Use (2034)', were qualified by toxicological and clinical studies and appropriate acceptance criteria have been set.

In the first round, CHMP raised a major objection (MO8) since the proposed shelf-life limits for glepaglutide content and impurities were not sufficiently justified. The applicant resolved this MO by adequately justifying the limits for product-related impurities based on linear regression trend analysis and taking variability into account, and 2) by justifying the lower content limit during shelf-life based on release and stability data.

Omission of a routine test for biological activity was justified.

The potential presence of elemental impurities in the finished product has been assessed following a risk-based approach in line with the ICH Q3D Guideline for Elemental Impurities. Batch analysis data using a validated ICP-MS or ICP-OES method was provided, demonstrating that each relevant elemental impurity was not detected above 30% of the respective PDE. Based on the risk assessment and the presented batch data it can be concluded that it is not necessary to include any elemental impurity controls. The information on the control of elemental impurities is satisfactory.

A risk assessment concerning the potential presence of nitrosamine impurities in the finished product has been performed considering all suspected and actual root causes in line with the \"Questions and answers for marketing authorisation holders/applicants on the CHMP Opinion for the Article 5(3) of Regulation (EC) No 726/2004 referral on nitrosamine impurities in human medicinal products\" (EMA/409815/2020) and the \"Assessment report- Procedure under Article 5(3) of Regulation EC (No) 726/2004- Nitrosamine impurities in human medicinal products\" (EMA/369136/2020). Based on the information provided, it is accepted that there is no risk of nitrosamine impurities in the active substance or the related finished product. Therefore, no specific control measures are deemed necessary.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The analytical methods used have been adequately described and appropriately validated in accordance with the ICH guidelines. Satisfactory information regarding the reference standards used for assay and impurities testing has been presented.

Batch analysis results are provided for full scale batches confirming the consistency of the manufacturing process and its ability to manufacture to the intended product specification.

## 3.3.4. Stability of the product

Stability data is presented from commercial scale batches manufactured at the commercial manufacturing site, including:

- Stability data from commercial scale batches in PFS stored for 36 months under long term conditions (5 °C ± 3 °C) and for 6 months under accelerated conditions (25 °C / 60% RH) according to the ICH guidelines. Dual storage conditions of 5 °C ± 3 °C for 30 months, followed by subsequent storage at 25 °C / 60% RH for two weeks (samples were moved to 25 °C after 30 months providing a total storage period of 30.5 months) were also investigated and reported completed. The batches of medicinal product are identical to those proposed for marketing and were packed in the primary packaging proposed for marketing.
- Stability data from commercial batches of PFS assembled in autoinjectors stored for up to 36 months under long term conditions (5 °C ± 3 °C) and for 6 months under accelerated conditions (25 °C / 60% RH) according to the ICH guidelines. The batches of medicinal product are identical to those proposed for marketing and were packed in the primary packaging proposed for marketing (PFS) and in an autoinjector design representative to that proposed for marketing.
- Stability data from commercial scale PPQ batches in PFS stored for 18 months under long term conditions (5 °C ± 3 °C) and for 6 months under accelerated conditions (25 °C / 60% RH) according to the ICH guidelines.

Samples were tested for: appearance of solution, visible particles, content of glepaglutide, impurities, pH, osmolality, extractable volume, sub-visible particles, sterility, bacterial endotoxins, container closure integrity, functionality testing of autoinjector (dose accuracy, cap removal force, activation force, needle extension, injection time). The analytical procedures used are stability indicating.

All tested parameters were within the specifications. Long-term and accelerated stability results show a decreasing trend for content of glepaglutide and an increasing trend for impurities (specified, largest unspecified, total).

All tested parameters were within the specifications. Changes were observed for content of glepaglutide and impurities but remained acceptably within the proposed specifications until 24 months and are not likely to have a significant effect on efficacy and safety of the product when used according to the directions in the SmPC.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The stability results for the autoinjector and PFS are similar, and dose accuracy did not change during storage. The applicant's conclusion that the assembly of the PFS into the autoinjector has no impact on the stability of the finished product was endorsed.

For the dual storage conditions, it was observed that the 2-weeks storage at 25 °C after 30 months storage at 5 °C, causes a slight increase in impurities. This has no impact since dual storage conditions or storage at 25 °C is not included in the proposed shelf-life claim and SmPC.

In addition, commercial batch samples of finished product in PFS and autoinjector assembly were exposed to light as defined in the ICH Guideline on Photostability Testing of New Drug Substances and Products. The photostability study shows that the finished product in the PFS is sensitive to light degradation and this is not completely mitigated by the autoinjector. Hence, the recommendation to store the finished protected from light (in the original carton) is justified.

Based on available stability data, the proposed shelf-life of 24 months and storage conditions 'Store in a refrigerator (2 °C - 8 °C). Do not freeze. Store in the original package in order to protect from light\" as stated in the SmPC (sections 6.3 and 6.4) are acceptable.

## 3.3.5. Post approval change management protocol(s)

Not applicable

## 3.3.6. Adventitious agents

No excipients derived from animal or human origin have been used except for the rigid needle shield. The elastomer of the rigid needle shield contains ingredient(s) of animal origin in the form of tallow derivate(s). The tallow derivates have been treated in compliance with the requirements described in section 6.4 of the EMA \"Note for guidance on minimising the risk of transmitting animal spongiform encephalopathy agents via human and veterinary medicinal products\" (EMA/410/01 rev.3). Therefore, the risk for TSE/BSE and adventitious viruses is negligible. This has been assessed in the context of the Notified Body Opinion.

## 3.4. Discussion and conclusions on chemical, pharmaceutical and biological aspects

Information on development, manufacture and control of the active substance and finished product has been presented in a satisfactory manner.

The applicant has applied QbD principles in the development of the active substance and/or finished product and their manufacturing process. However, no design spaces were claimed for the manufacturing process of the active substance, nor for the finished product.

During the procedure 8 MOs were raised by the CHMP related to 1) the incomplete structural characterisation of the active substance, 2) incomplete mutagenic impurities risk assessment and control strategy justification, 3) peak purity analysis revealing a new identified active substance impurity, 4) proposed identity tests that were insufficient to confirm the peptide sequence, 5) incomplete validation of the HPLC method for identity, assay and peptide-related impurities of the active substance, 6) suitability of the staked needle with regard to the risk for intramuscular injection, 7) incomplete documentation on the sterilisation of the primary container closure components, 8) inadequate justification of the proposed shelf-life limits for glepaglutide content and impurities.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

All MOs were satisfactorily resolved by 1) presenting satisfactory information on the presence of secondary structure and absence of higher order structures using appropriate analytical techniques, 2) providing updated documentation on the ICH M7 risk assessment and control strategy with underlying data on (Q)SAR assessment and purge factor calculations where appropriate, 3) including a test for an identified impurity in the active substance specification with a justified limit, 4) including a test based on tandem mass spectrometry (MS/MS) which allows confirmation of the expected peptide sequence, 5) additional validation of the HPLC method for identity, assay and peptide-related impurities as per ICH requirements, 6) demonstrating that quality of the finished product is not impacted by expelling it through the needle, justifying absence of risk of intramuscular injection and including a test for needle extension in the finished product specification, 7) submitting complete documentation on sterilisation of the primary packaging components, 8) adequate justification of shelf life limits for glepaglutide content and product-related impurities with finished product release and stability data.

At the time of the CHMP opinion, there was a minor unresolved quality issue having no impact on the Benefit/Risk ratio of the product, which pertains to the water content acceptance limit in the active substance. This point is put forward and agreed as a recommendation for future quality development. The results of tests carried out indicate consistency and uniformity of important product quality characteristics, and these in turn lead to the conclusion that the product should have a satisfactory and uniform performance in clinical use.

The quality of this product is considered to be acceptable when used in accordance with the conditions defined in the SmPC. Physicochemical and biological aspects relevant to the uniform clinical performance of the product have been investigated and are controlled in a satisfactory way. Data has been presented to give reassurance on TSE safety.

## 3.5. Recommendation for future quality development

In the context of the obligation of the MAHs to take due account of technical and scientific progress, the CHMP recommends the following points for investigation:

The applicant should re-evaluate the acceptance criterion for water content upon availability of 30 commercial scale batch release data (REC).

## 4. Non-clinical aspects

## 4.1. Introduction

The nonclinical safety program for glepaglutide has been designed in accordance with the ICH M3 (R2) guideline to support a chronic indication. All pivotal nonclinical safety studies (safety pharmacology, repeat-dose toxicity, genotoxicity, carcinogenicity and DART) were conducted according to Good Laboratory Practice (GLP).

The pharmacodynamic profile of glepaglutide was characterized by using in vitro and in vivo studies. Potential off-target effects were assessed in vitro, investigating the selectivity of glepaglutide against various receptors and transporters and the activity on related peptide G protein-coupled receptors. A core battery of safety pharmacology studies was conducted including in vitro assays for effects on cardiac ion channels, an Irwin test in rats for assessment of effects on the central nervous system (CNS), a respiratory test in rats for assessment of effects on the respiratory system, and telemetry studies in dogs and rabbits to assess effects on the cardiovascular system following intravenous (IV) administration.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Pharmacokinetic studies with glepaglutide were conducted in rats. Toxicokinetic assessments were included in the toxicity studies with glepaglutide in agreement with the ICH S3A guideline. 8 No in vivo studies to assess pharmacokinetic drug interactions were performed.

Repeat dose toxicity studies were performed with dosing for up to 13 weeks in mice, 26 weeks in rats, and 39 weeks in dogs. Assessment of local tolerance and development of anti-drug antibodies (ADA) was investigated in the toxicology studies. In vitro and in vivo genotoxicity studies, carcinogenicity studies in mice and rats, a fertility study in rats, embryo-fetal development studies in rats and rabbits, a pre- and post-natal toxicity study in rats and a juvenile toxicity study in rats were also completed.

## 4.2. Analytical methods

Different bioanalytical assays using LC/MS/MS have been used during the development program, initially, measuring the parent compound, and later, including the main metabolites. The assays used for detection of anti-glepaglutide antibodies in serum all used the ELISA format and have similarly been updated through the development program. All methods used to document exposure/antibody development in the non-clinical safety and toxicity studies have been validated according to guidelines in effect at the time of validation.

## 4.3. Pharmacology

## 4.3.1. Pharmacodynamics

## 4.3.1.1. Primary pharmacodynamics

Glepaglutide is a high affinity GLP-2R agonist. The potency of glepaglutide and its two main metabolites ZP18481-35 and ZP18481-34 for inducing cAMP production were determined in terms of EC50, in two GLP-2 receptor functional assays. The first study (06-195 and 08-230) used a Baby Hamster Kidney cell line (BHK21) transfected with human GLP-2R. The second study (15-150) used Human embryonic kidney cells (HEK293) transfected with human, mouse, rat, rabbit and dog GLP-2R. Both studies showed that glepaglutide is a potent agonist of the GLP-2R in human and all species used in toxicology testing, with EC50 values in the pM range. The two main metabolites are more potent than glepaglutide in all species, and all three are more potent than GLP-2, the natural ligand of GLP-2R.

Exogenous GLP-2 induces significant growth of the small intestinal mucosal epithelium via stimulation of stem cell proliferation in the crypts and via inhibition of apoptosis on the villi. Additional reported effects of GLP-2 include inhibition of gastric motility and gastric acid secretion, stimulation of nutrient absorption, enhancement of intestinal barrier function, and increased intestinal blood flow.

No animal models of disease were used in the pharmacology studies performed by the applicant. Small intestinal mass increased in mice and rats after treatment of 3 days, 1 week and 2 weeks (studies 04-076 05-008, 08-061, 07-203, 20-091). This coincided with increases in citrulline plasma levels, a marker for intestinal absorption. Intestinal mass and citrulline increases remained for up to 2 weeks after cessation of daily dosing at the highest dose tested of 405 nmol/kg (1.75 mg/kg). In a similar experiment in rats, small and large intestinal mass was measured, as well as citrulline levels, after one week of treatment with glepaglutide (study 10-176). The small intestine appears more susceptible to glepaglutide, as increased mass was seen from the lowest dose of 22 nmol/kg (0.095 mg/kg), whereas the large intestine responded from doses of 225 nmol/kg (0.97 mg/kg), and showed a lesser degree of enlargement (100% for small intestine, 20% for large intestine). For increased in citrulline, the dose between 225 and 899 nmol/kg (0.97 to 3.9 mg/kg) appeared to be the optimum, as higher doses up to 2247 nmol/kg (9.7 mg/kg) resulted in less effect.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In a further rat study (11-129), the effect of less frequent dosing was evaluated. It was shown that a reduction from daily dosing (for 2 weeks, after which there was an increase in intestinal mass) to weekly or twice weekly dosing (for 4 weeks), resulted in a reduction of small intestinal mass. After a total of 6 weeks, the mass was still increased as compared to vehicle control in the high dose group (200 nmol/kg = 0.86 mg/kg), but no longer in the low dose group (50 nmol/kg = 0.22 mg/kg), which is likely to be a more clinically relevant dose. Further, it is likely that after a longer period of weekly or twice weekly dosing, the mass would be equal to control also in the high dose group.

Overall, the primary pharmacology effects evaluated in vivo were limited to increases in small and large intestinal mass, and enhanced intestinal absorption as determined by plasma citrulline levels. Although limited, the proof of concept of glepaglutide for the treatment of small bowel syndrome has been sufficiently shown in rats, when treated daily with 1-4 mg/kg of glepaglutide.

## 4.3.1.2. Secondary pharmacodynamics

The secondary pharmacology screen shows (study 05-056) no significant binding of glepaglutide to other targets. Although the number of secondary targets is somewhat limited, it is considered sufficient considering the nature of the product. The maximum tested concentration of 1 µM is sufficient, considering the total Cmax (glegaplutide and its metabolites) in humans is only 4.73 nM.

In a more targeted analysis (study 11-028), agonist activity at related G-protein coupled receptors (GPCR), including GLP-1R, was investigated using Millipore profiling. In this assay no agonist activity was seen at any other GPCR except GLP-2R, at a concentration of 0.125 µM. This is in contrast to a cellular assay with overexpression of GLP-1R (study 16-011), where EC50 values for GLP-1R agonism ranged from 2.5 nM for glepaglutide, to as low as 1.1 nM for the main metabolites ZP18481-34. In this assay there was an 8.3-fold difference in potency between GLP-2R and GLP-1R for glepaglutide, and a 21-fold difference between the two receptors for the main metabolite. This indicated that some agonistic activity at the GLP-1R is possible. This is confirmed by an in vivo study in mice on glucose tolerance (study 20-078). Glepaglutide demonstrated an effect more similar to GLP-1R agonist compounds than to other GLP-2R agonist compounds, evidenced as anti-glycemic actions, although the effect of glepaglutide was less pronounced. A further mouse study (21-013) showed that although there was no effect of glepaglutide on gastric emptying, intestinal transit was inhibited, albeit to a lesser degree than the GLP-1R agonist reference compound. These data suggest a moderate agonistic effect of glepaglutide and its main metabolite on the GLP-1R.

## 4.3.1.3. Pharmacodynamic drug interactions

No non-clinical pharmacodynamic interaction studies were performed with glepaglutide. This is agreed as it is further discussed on a clinical level.

## 4.3.2. Pharmacokinetics

## 4.3.2.1. Absorption

Pharmacokinetics of glepaglutide after IV administration was investigated in male rats (1 dose only; study 09-067) and male and female dogs (3 dose levels; study 08-021). In both species, on day 1, ZP1848 had a short plasma half-life (0.8h in rats and 1.8-6h in dogs) and low volume of distribution (0.06L/kg in rats and 0.07-0.51 L/kg in dogs). In dogs, distribution volume and terminal half-life increased with increasing dose and was increased after multiple doses, up to a T1/2 of 11h and a Vss of 1.32 L/kg. In rats, ZP1848, ZP18481-34 and ZP18481-35 represented 95%, 4% and 1% of the total measured exposure, respectively. Both metabolites peaked 1 h after administration. In dogs, only the parent compound was analysed.

Toxicokinetics following SC administration was investigated in several studies in mice (14 days-103 weeks), rats (7 days-103 weeks), rabbits (14d) and dogs (14d-39 weeks) as well as in pregnant rats and rabbits and juvenile rats.

In all species, glepaglutide was retained in the subcutaneous compartment. After a single exposure, ZP1848, represented approx. 5% of the total exposure, which increased to 20-30% of the total exposure after multiple exposure in rats, rabbits and dogs (increase was less in mice). Exposure of ZP18481-34 was higher than the parent compound in all species (50-90% of total exposure) ZP18481-35 represented 1-10% of the total exposure. Maximum concentration of the parent compound was reached after 0.5-2h, whereas Tmax of ZP18481-34 and ZP18481-35 was 4-12 and 1-12h. The halflives of ZP1848 and the main metabolites were longer after SC administration than after IV administration, indicating the PK/TK was absorption rate limited.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In general, exposure to the parent and main metabolites increased with dose, varying from less than dose proportional to more than dose proportional. Based on the provided information, this claim is not supported for rabbits in which exposure to the parent compound seemed relatively constant at 25-30% regardless of dosing duration.

Accumulation of ZP1848 and ZP18481-34 was observed in mice and rats up to approximately week 12/13 (sometimes even up to &gt;10x), but decreased again at later time points, as measured in the carcinogenicity studies to levels ≤ day 1. No relevant accumulation was observed in dogs and no relevant changes in exposure were observed for ZP18481-35. Since plasma concentrations versus time profiles were highly variable across dose levels and sampling occasions, conclusions on accumulation should be taken with caution.

Overall, no consistent gender differences were observed.

Exposure in the juvenile rat study (study 19-067) seems to be somewhat lower when compared to the other rat studies, whereas exposure in pregnant rats and rabbits seems to be comparable to nonpregnant animals.

Anti-drug antibodies were observed in all species and in all treatment groups, but also in the control groups (not in pre-treatment groups). This was observed in all studies analyzing ADA's. Antibody titres in control animals were lower than in treatment groups, but no further explanation has been provided. Overall, ADA incidences were highest in the lower dose groups, probably due to interference at higher dose levels. Presence of antibodies in the treated animals did not seem to affect exposure, indicating that ADA's were not clearing. In addition, no impact on the pharmacodynamic (PD) effect of ZP1848 was observed, indicating that ADA's were also not neutralizing.

## 4.3.2.2. Distribution

After IV administration, for both glepaglutide and the major metabolites (ZP18481-34 and ZP18481-35), a low distribution volume was observed in rat and dog (0.06L/kg in rats and 0.07-0.51 L/kg in dogs after a single dose), indicating distribution did not exceed the extracellular space. In dogs, data indicate that after repeated administration the test compound is distributed to a higher extent than after a single dose.

The distribution ZP1848 has been studied after single SC administration of [3H-Phe22]-ZP1848 to male Sprague Dawley rats (study 16-117). Radioactivity from [3H-Phe22]-ZP1848 was rapidly and widely distributed throughout the body (exception of white fat). Highest tissue: blood ratios were observed in organs reported to express the GLP-2 receptor (pancreas, small intestine wall). Relatively high levels of radioactivity were also observed in organs with high peptide or protein synthesis, such as bone marrow, various glands, and seminal vesicles, probably due to de-novo protein synthesis. Blood: plasma ratios were 1:7.7 at 2 and 10 h and 1:3.0 at 24h. Binding to melanin or accumulation in pigmented tissues was not analysed (only non-pigmented animals were used). Placental transfer and excretion to milk have not been analysed. However, glepaglutide and its metabolites could not be detected in rat pups on PND4.

## 4.3.2.3. Metabolism

The in vivo metabolism of glepaglutide following SC administration was investigated by targeted analysis of rat, dog and human plasma (studies 08-200, 08-125 and 08-216) using an immunoaffinity capturing approach (IA), and analysis by IA-LC-MS/(MS). The metabolite profiles from plasma were similar across species and gender. All metabolites observed in human plasma were also observed in rat and dog plasma. All identified metabolites were C-terminal truncated analogs of ZP1848, except for one N-terminal truncated metabolite which was only detected in the rat. All C-terminal analogs were active metabolites. ZP18481-34 was the major metabolite in all species, followed by ZP18481-35.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In another study in rats (study 16-117), the metabolite profile in plasma and urine was analysed following SC administration. In urine, the following components were detected: phenylalanine, tyrosine, hippuric acid and phenylacetylglycine.

In a third study in rats (study 09-067), metabolism following administration by the IV and SC routes in rats was compared. The results suggest that ZP1848 is mainly metabolized to ZP18481-35 and ZP18481-34 in the subcutis, whereas in plasma only 3-4% of ZP1848 was metabolized to ZP18481-35 or ZP18481-34. Approximately 25% (after a single dose to 75% (after multiple doses) of ZP18481-35 is metabolized to ZP18481-34 in plasma. Following multiple SC administration, ZP18481-34 accounted for ≥68% of the systemic exposure.

Together, the available data indicate that ZP1848 is gradually metabolized by proteolytic enzymes as for other endogenous peptides and enters the normal pool of amino acids.

## 4.3.2.4. Excretion

Subcutaneous administration of [3H-Phe22]ZP1848 to male Sprague Dawley rats at a target dose level of 50 mg/kg (study 16-117) resulted in limited excretion of radioactive material: after 168h, around 8% was excreted via urine, 9.5% via feces and 9% via expired air. More than 70% remained in the carcass and was probably incorporated into endogenous proteins via normal anabolic mechanisms.

## 4.3.2.5. Pharmacokinetic drug interactions

The pharmacokinetic drug interactions are assessed in the clinical section.

Repeat-dose toxicity studies with daily SC administration were performed in mice (up to 13 weeks), rats (up to 6 months) and dogs (up to 9 months). All glepaglutide-treated groups from the lowest dose onwards (≥0.25-2.5 mg/kg) showed a clear PD effect on the small intestine, including increased weight/length, villous hypertrophy and mucosal hyperplasia in the duodenum, ileum and jejunum. In rodents, there were also PD effects on the large intestine and cecum, as evidenced by increased colon weight/length and distension, respectively. Associated microscopic changes only occurred in higher dose groups. Expected PD effects were also seen in the gall bladders of mice (all doses) and dogs (high dose only), including dilatation and epithelial hyperplasia. The effects on the large intestine and gall bladder completely recovered after 6 weeks, whereas the effects on the small intestine were only partially reversible. Gall bladder disease is included in the RMP and events of cholecystitis, cholangitis and cholelithiasis have been reported in clinical studies with glepaglutide.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In mice, a non-GLP dose range finding study (15-186) found that 20 mg/kg caused adverse injection site reactions (bruising). A GLP-compliant 13-week study was subsequently done (16-026), to determine a suitable dose range for the carcinogenicity study. In both sexes, there were slight changes in plasma biochemistry and blood haematology. In males only, there were injection site reactions and liver findings. All changes up to the high dose (10 mg/kg) were minor and non-adverse, but because of mortality at the low dose (1 mg/kg, 44-fold exposure margin) that was potentially glepalutide-related, given the gall bladder findings, no NOAEL was established in mice.

Repeated dosing was performed in two rat strains. In Sprague Dawley rats, a non-GLP 3-day dose range finding study (07-172) as well as a GLP-compliant 14-day study (08-032) showed that treatment with 125 mg/kg glepaglutide resulted in decreased food consumption and body weight loss. A 56-day GLP-compliant study (10-108) did not show adverse systemic effects up to 50 mg/kg. GLP-compliant studies were also performed with Han Wistar rats. Treatment with glepaglutide had no adverse systemic effects up to 50 mg/kg for 14 days (15-185) and up to 10 mg/kg for 6 months (16-024). Only 6-month treatment resulted in mesenteric lymph node findings (dilation, pigmented macrophages) and reduced plasma lipids from the low dose onwards. In both strains, injection site reactions were observed as well as similar non-adverse findings related to haematology, liver and kidney function, which were partially reversible after 6 weeks. The systemic NOAEL of the long-term toxicity study in rats was 10 mg/kg/day (100-fold exposure margin).

In dogs, repeated daily IV administration of glepaglutide up to 15 mg/kg was tested in a 4-day nonGLP study (07-174) as well as a GLP-compliant 14-day study (08-021). Both studies showed a decrease in food consumption and body weight (from 5 mg/kg), injection site reactions (from 1 mg/kg), and transient post-dosing clinical observations (pale gums at all doses, hypoactivity from 15 mg/kg). Treatment for 14 days also caused lower heart and thymus weights (with mild lymphoid depletion) at 5 and 15 mg/kg, respectively. The systemic NOAEL of the 14-day IV study in dogs was 1 mg/kg. Full TK analysis including metabolites was not done (only parent compound measured), so exposure margins could not be calculated. Nevertheless, the clinical relevance of the conducted IV studies is limited given the intended SC administration route.

Repeat-dose studies using daily SC administration were also performed in dogs. In a 3-day non-GLP study (07-173), 25 mg/kg glepaglutide reduced food consumption/body weight and increased neutrophils. GLP-compliant studies were done for 14 days (08-033), 56 days (10-109) and 9 months (16-009). From the lowest dose onwards (0.25 mg/kg) in the 56-day and 9-month studies, cholesterol levels were reduced, similar to observations in the 6-month rat study. Partially reversible injection site inflammatory reactions with associated changes in hematology (increased white blood cells, decreased red blood cells) as well as liver and kidney findings associated with slight changes in plasma biochemistry and urinalysis were also observed in most glepaglutide-treated groups. All SC studies in dogs noted a reversible decrease in thymus weight at doses ≥2.5 mg/kg (≥88-fold exposure margin), which may be due to stress. Decreased food consumption/body weight was observed at even higher doses and recovered after 6 weeks. The NOAEL of the 56-day and 9-month studies in dogs was 1 mg/kg/day (52-fold exposure margin).

Glepaglutide was negative in standard GLP-compliant in vitro (bacterial and mammalian) and in vivo genotoxicity studies. According to the ICH S6 guidance, proteins are considered to be non-genotoxic.

Carcinogenicity studies have been conducted in mice (17-119) and rats (17-118). Glepaglutide was administered SC daily for two years. The doses selected for both rodent carcinogenicity studies (up to 1 mg/kg in mouse and 2 mg/kg in rat) seem low given that in the 13-week mouse toxicity study the Applicant concluded that doses up to 10 mg/kg/day were appropriate for administration in a carcinogenicity study and given that the NOAEL established in the 6-month rat toxicity study was 10 mg/kg. Nevertheless, the highest dose in each study is pharmacologically active based on the in vivo PD studies and was expected to represent a more than 25-fold exposure margin as compared to the clinical AUC. TK analysis showed that, in mice as well as rats, exposure peaked at 12 weeks (reaching sufficient margins) but declined thereafter for unknown reason. Especially in mice, the exposure was very low after 12 weeks, leading to a very small endpoint safety margin (2-fold at the high dose). The relevance of the mouse carcinogenicity study is therefore questionable.

Non-neoplastic changes were in line with what was observed in the repeat-dose toxicity studies, including PD-related hypertrophy in the GI tract and gall bladder hyperplasia, which were observed in both species from the lowest doses (0.1 mg/kg in mouse and 0.2 mg/kg in rat). In rats, there were also non-neoplastic findings in the bile duct, kidneys, spleen and in the mesenteric lymph nodes from the lowest dose onwards.

No gastrointestinal neoplastic changes were seen in the mouse study, however, neoplastic changes in the duodenum (adenoma and adenocarcinoma) were observed in the rat study, though only in males (4/56 or 7.1% in high dose group and 1/56 or 1.8% in low dose group versus none in the control group). Such tumors are rare (historical control 0.13% in males and 0% in females), though the finding was not unexpected given the pharmacology of glepaglutide. Considering these glepaglutiderelated intestinal tumors, no NOAEL could be established in the rat study (lowest dose corresponded to a 4-fold exposure margin at endpoint). As such, the potential carcinogenic risk can best be evaluated by clinical monitoring and post-marketing clinical surveillance.

At the injection sites, neoplastic skin findings consisting of cutaneous (fibro)sarcomas were found in both animal studies. The Applicant claims they were likely caused by the vehicle (5 mM L-arginine, 15 mM L-histidine and 230 mM D-mannitol, PH 7.0 in water, similar to the clinical formulation) since these tumors were also found in the control groups. In female rats, the incidences of skin fibrosarcomas were

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

low and comparable between the control group (1/56 or 1.8%) and glepaglutide-treated groups (0-2/56 or 0-3.6%). In female mice, only the low dose group showed a slightly higher incidence as compared to the control group (3/56 or 5.4% vs 1/56 or 1.8%) and historical control data (3%, nonSC injection). In male mice, the incidence of pleomorphic sarcomas was higher than in females (up to 12/56 or 21%) but comparable between the control group and glepaglutide-treated groups, supporting a vehicle effect as compared to historical control data (3.6%, non-SC injection). However, in male rats the incidence of pleomorphic fibrosarcomas clearly increased dose-dependently (1/56 or 1.8% in control and low dose group, 9/56 or 16% in mid dose group and 13/56 or 23% in high dose group). Furthermore, 1 mid dose male developed a malignant sarcoma versus none in the control group. These neoplastic skin findings were a frequent contributing factor to death in male rats, including the control group. Historical control data were not shown. Literature indicates that fibrosarcomas are commonly induced by repeated subcutaneous injection of non-carcinogenic compounds in rodents and are thought to result from chronic inflammatory processes. It was not discussed by the Applicant whether the inflammatory injection site reactions caused by glepaglutide correlated with the observed skin malignancies, especially in male rats (from mid dose, 11-fold exposure margin at endpoint). Nevertheless, injection site fibrosarcomas in rodents are considered to have low relevance for human risk assessment. Any risk is further reduced by the less frequent dosing schedule that is proposed for patients (twice weekly rather than daily).

Cardiomyopathy and atrial thrombosis were observed in mice, predominantly males, administered ≥0.4 mg/kg/day, which resulted in a slight increase in male mortality (7 deaths versus 3 in the control/low dose groups, 4 in the mid dose group, and 0 in all female mice). Such findings were not observed in the preliminary mouse studies of shorter duration or in any other tox species. It was explained that rodent progressive cardiomyopathy (RPCM) is a spontaneous, chronic and progressive disease in laboratory rodents, which may be exacerbated by drug treatment, and which does not occur in human patients. This finding is therefore not considered clinically relevant.

In a rat fertility study (16-028), glepaglutide up to 10 mg/kg/day SC had no effect on sperm count, mating performance, the number of corpora lutea, implantations, resorptions, live embryos and the extent of the pre- and post-implantation loss. Glepaglutide treatment did cause a shift in female estrous cycles (4/5 or 5 days instead of 4 days) and a decreased number of copulation plugs produced by some of the males. These findings were not considered adverse given that reproduction was not affected. The NOAEL of this study was 10 mg/kg/day (estimated exposure margin at least 50-fold).

EFD studies were conducted in two species. In rats, a preliminary EFD study showed that dose levels ≥25 mg/kg/day SC were not tolerated, causing premature maternal death due to GI tract abnormalities. In the pivotal rat study (16-027), the highest dose of 10 mg/kg also led to premature deaths as well as litter deaths. Embryo-foetal survival, placental weight, foetal weight and litter size were unaffected at lower dose levels.

At 5 and 10 mg/kg, there was a non-dose-related increase in the incidence of bent scapulae (1.4% and 0.7%, respectively) and short/thickened long bones (1.8% and 0.7%, respectively) as compared to the control group (0%) and the historical control range (0-0.5%). These findings can be considered skeletal variations that are likely secondary to maternal toxicity.

Other findings that were treatment-related (observed at all doses) and exceeded the historical control range were a delay in ossification (cranial centres: 13-18% versus 5% in control group) and medially thickened/kinked ribs (9-15% versus 0.9% in control group). These findings are generally regarded as transient developmental variations in rats and can be considered non-adverse. The embryo-foetal NOAEL of the rat EFD study is therefore 2 mg/kg/day (10-fold exposure margin).

In rabbits, a preliminary EFD study showed that dose levels from 20 mg/kg/day SC were not tolerated, causing premature maternal death due to injection site reactions and body weight loss. In the pivotal rabbit study (15-188), injection site reactions were the predominant findings in the pregnant females. Glepaglutide up to the highest dose of 10 mg/kg did not cause any effect on litters or foetal pathology. The embryo-foetal NOAEL of the rabbit EFD study is 10 mg/kg/day (46-fold exposure margin).

The effect of glepaglutide on prenatal and postnatal development was assessed in rats. A preliminary study indicated that dose levels up to 2 mg/kg/day SC were well-tolerated. The pivotal PPND study (19-165) showed pharmacological effects on the small and large intestines in the F0 females from the mid dose (0.7 mg/kg). At the high dose (2 mg/kg) these animals also had higher body weights on Day 21 as compared to the control group. Glepaglutide or its metabolites were not detected in the blood of the F1 pups on Day 4 of age. Apart from a slight increase in body weight gain after weaning, there were no findings in the litters from glepaglutide-treated rats. Mating and fertility were unaffected in the F1 animals and there were no subsequent findings in the F2 litters.

In conclusion, daily glepaglutide treatment of rats up to 2 mg/kg/day (NOAEL) from Day 6 after mating to Day 10 of lactation had no adverse effects on pre- or post-natal development. There were no macroscopic findings in the rat PPND study, supporting that the skeletal observations (delayed ossification of cranial centers and medially thickened/kinked ribs and) observed in the rat EFD study at the same dose level were transient and without functional consequence.

Since glepaglutide is intended for adult SBS patients, potential juvenile toxicity is not relevant for the current MAA.

Daily subcutaneous administration of glepaglutide was tested in neonatal rats from Day 10 of age, which corresponds to the developmental period of human infants (1-24 months). A preliminary study indicated that dose levels up to 5 mg/kg were well-tolerated. In the pivotal 8-week study (19-070), there were minor reversible changes in haematological and urinary parameters from 1 mg/kg, without a dose-response or histopathological correlates. Non-adverse changes in liver weight (increase with partial recovery) and some biomarkers of liver function were likely due to physiological adaptations to the metabolism and excretion of glepaglutide and were considered non-adverse. Furthermore, there were expected pharmacological effects in the small and large intestine (showing partial and complete recovery, respectively) and local irritation (showing complete recovery) at the injection sites at all dose levels.

Exposure of glepaglutide and its metabolites increased with increasing dose. The increases were not fully dose-proportional on Day 10, but approximately dose-proportional on Day 62. Glepaglutide did not accumulate. The metabolites showed accumulation, which may be due to change in metabolism throughout the maturation.

In conclusion, similar glepaglutide treatment effects as well as pharmacokinetics were observed in the neonatal and adult rat studies. Glepaglutide did not cause adverse effects on the developing GI tract. The NOAEL of the 8-week neonatal rat study was 5 mg/kg/day (35-fold exposure margin).
