Pharmacokinetic and toxicokinetic data is available for mice, rats, dogs, rabbits. Overall, the SC exposure profiles were similar across all evaluated species. No consistent sex-related differences were observed in any species.

Glepaglutide is extensively metabolised in the subcutaneous depot in both animal species and humans. This results in a relatively low systemic exposure to parent compound, but higher exposure to the active metabolites ZP18481-35 and especially ZP18481-34.

Dose proportionality of exposure varied across studies and species. Although increases in exposure (Cmax and AUC) were generally observed with rising doses in all species, the proportion was inconsistent.

Following intravenous injections of the parent drug, both parent and main metabolites have a low volume of distribution.

Also, metabolism is similar in animal species and humans, with glepaglutide being metabolized by proteolytic enzymes to (C-terminal) truncated analogs of ZP1848 and finally in single amino acids, which are subsequently incorporated into endogenous proteins.

The main route of elimination in animals as well as humans is via proteolytic metabolism. After 168h, only 8% was excreted via urine and 9.5% via feces. In rats, more than 70% remained in the carcass and was probably incorporated into endogenous proteins via normal anabolic mechanisms.

Exposure margins have been calculated based on total exposure to ZP1848, ZP18481-34 and ZP18481-35. Since parent and main metabolites are all active, this is acceptable. In addition, exposure values at the last treatment day are used for the calculations, even though in several studies, exposure levels are higher at earlier time points. Nevertheless, even with this conservative approach, MOEs are considered sufficient, being above 10, for most studies. Only in the carcinogenicity studies the margins

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

are low. In mice, endpoint exposure was absent/minimal (2-fold margin at high dose) and the relevance of this study is therefore questionable. In rats, the endpoint exposures were still adequate for the whole dose range, though there was no NOAEL for the neoplastic changes in the duodenum (&lt;4-fold MOE).

## 4.4.2. Other toxicity studies

Not applicable

## 4.4.3. Local tolerance

A separate local tolerance study (15-183) was conducted in rats to compare local effects between glepaglutide, metabolites (ZP18481-35, ZP18481-34 and ZP18481-33) and Teduglutide. Compounds were administered SC at 10 or 20 mg/kg for 14 consecutive days. Glepaglutide and Teduglutide both caused massive inflammation, though Teduglutide caused a more severe histopathological reaction (granulomatous inclusions). ZP18481-33 caused slighty more edema than glepaglutide. ZP18481-35 and ZP18481-34 were formulated in a different vehicle which caused adverse reactions on its own, therefore a comparison between glepaglutide and its main metabolites could not be made, limiting the relevance of this study.

In conclusion, similar to what was observed in repeat-dose toxicology studies, glepaglutide caused injection site reactions in the local tolerance study from the lowest dose onwards. Given the lack of NOAEL, injection site reactions are expected to occur at clinically relevant doses, though the less frequent dosing schedule that is proposed for patients (twice weekly rather than daily) may reduce the severity. In the conducted clinical studies with glepaglutide, injection site reactions were the most frequently reported adverse events and are labelled in 4.8 of the SmPC.

## 4.4.4. Ecotoxicity/environmental risk assessment

The active substance is a natural substance, the use of which will not alter the concentration or distribution of the substance in the environment. Therefore, glepaglutide is not expected to pose a risk to the environment.

## 4.5. Overall discussion and conclusions on non-clinical aspects

## 4.5.1. Discussion

## Pharmacodynamics

The in vitro effects of glepaglutide and its two main metabolites ZP18481-35 and ZP18481-34 was investigated in two GLP-2 receptor functional assays, which showed that all three compounds are high affinity GLP-2R agonists, with ZP18481-34 being the most relevant pharmacologically active compound. No studies on cell proliferation or inhibition of apoptosis were presented while this is the proposed mechanism of action. However, other functions of GLP-2 are difficult to evaluate due to complex signalling pathways. No further functional studies were submitted. This is agreed, since there is sufficient literature on the function of GLP-2. In rats, increases in small intestinal mass, large intestinal mass, and plasma citrulline levels were seen between doses of 22-2247, 225-2247, and 56-899 nmol/kg/day, respectively. No exposure was measured in these PD studies, but when extrapolated from pharmacokinetics and repeated dose studies, it appears that the exposure needed for an optimum effect, based on plasma citrulline levels, in rats is much higher than the exposure reached in the clinic. Further discussion on dose and clinical efficacy is provided in the clinical part of this assessment report. Rat intestinal effects were dose-dependent, but plasma citrulline peaked at mid-dose and declined at higher doses, questioning its usability as biomarker. However, the applicant adequately justified that the absence of a clear dose-response relationship is likely attributable to limitations of the animal model (i.e. healthy animals, short treatment duration, and high dose levels) and is not considered reflective of the clinical situation. Furthermore, it was discussed that citrulline concentrations alone are not considered a reliable surrogate for intestinal adaptation, absorptive capacity, or reduction in parenteral support in SBS patients. From a non-clinical perspective, the use of citrulline levels as supportive evidence for glepaglutide's anticipated intestinotrophic effect on the intestinal epithelium is therefore considered adequately justified. In rats, when switching to less than daily dosing (once or twice weekly), the effect on intestinal mass and citrulline levels reduced to near baseline levels after 6 weeks. It is therefore anticipated that daily dosing is required to maintain the effect on rats. Further discussion on the choice of dosing in the clinical setting is provided in the clinical AR. Overall, the primary pharmacology effects evaluated in vivo were limited to increases in small and large intestinal mass, and enhanced intestinal absorption as determined by plasma citrulline levels. Although limited, the proof of concept of glepaglutide for the treatment of small bowel syndrome has been sufficiently shown in rats, when treated daily with 1-4 mg/kg of glepaglutide. No secondary pharmacology targets were identified, apart from GLP-1R. EC50 values for GLP-1R agonism ranged from 2.5 nM for glepaglutide, to as low as 0.84 and 1.1 nM for the main metabolites ZP18481-35 and ZP18481-34, respectively. In vivo, anti-glycemic actions and a reduction in intestinal transit time, which could be consistent with GLP-1R receptor activation were evident in mice at exposures (combined for the parent compound and the two main metabolites) 8.5-fold higher than the expected exposure in humans at MRHD, although to a lesser degree than the GLP-1R agonist reference compound. These data suggest a moderate agonistic effect of glepaglutide and its main metabolites on the GLP-1R. Furthermore, glepaglutide's GLP-1R agonistic properties were confirmed in repeat-dose toxicity studies, where a decrease in food consumption and/or body weight gain (mainly in higher dose groups) and a reduction in cholesterol (mainly after long-term treatment) observed in several toxicology studies could be consistent with off-target GLP-1 agonism. The applicant justified the omission of GLP-1 receptor activity from section 5.1 of the SmPC on the basis that glepaglutide and its metabolites display substantially lower in vitro agonist activity at the GLP-1 receptor than at the GLP-2 receptor. Accordingly, GLP-1 receptor-mediated effects are not considered clinically relevant at therapeutic exposure levels. Although effects such as improved glucose tolerance and reduced intestinal transit may occur in vivo, these were observed at toxicological dose levels considerably exceeding the clinical dose range. Glepaglutide showed some effects in safety pharmacology studies. In the in vivo dog study with conscious male radiotelemetry-instrumented Beagle dogs, there were changes in heart rate, blood pressure (systolic, diastolic, mean arterial and pulse), and ECG parameters at the high dose of 15 mg/kg. The NOAEL for cardiovascular effects is the mid dose. In the in vivo cardiovascular study in rabbits, no effects on cardiovascular parameters were seen at doses up to 36 mg/kg. From the mid-dose (18 mg/kg) onward, there was a decrease in body temperature, and a single mortality at the high dose. Although this is likely treatment-related, there is still a large safety margin of at least 122-fold for glepaglutide and 18-fold for ZP18481-34 at the NOEAL of 6 mg/kg based on Cmax. These values are likely largely underestimated as there is no PK data in rabbits after IV dosing, and therefore the safety margins are based on SC dosing, where the Cmax is much lower.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

After a single high IV dose in rats, immediate effects on body temperature and reduced activity was seen, in females from the mid-dose, and males only at the high dose. Although it is argued by the applicant that the effects seen are not centrally mediated CNS effects, they could be due to an a-specific effect of high doses of peptide, or a secondary effect caused by an acute drop in blood pressure, as seen in the cardiovascular studies. These clinical signs are therefore considered treatment-related. However, clinical relevance is unlikely since the NOEAL of 5 mg/kg, still results in a safety margin of 1190-fold, based on Cmax, or 73-fold based on AUC (extrapolated from another rat study with the same dose).

In a respiratory study in rats, there was an immediate effect on respiratory frequency (males only), and on tidal and minute volume, which were all reduced. This was apparent at all doses and is likely treatment-related. A similar effect on respiration was seen in a single dose IV rat study, also with high doses. In repeated dose rat SC studies resulting in lower exposures and therefore more relevant for the human situation, no such effects were observed. It is therefore concluded that the respiratory findings in the safety pharmacology study at exposure 1190-fold the clinical exposure, are not relevant for humans.

## Pharmacokinetics

Method validation studies to support bioanalyses/toxicokinetics of glepaglutide, ZP18481-35, ZP18481-34 and anti-glepaglutide antibodies in the pivotal toxicology studies were GLP-compliant and considered validated and fit-for-purpose according to applicable guidelines. Incurred sample reanalysis of glepaglutide, ZP18481-35 and ZP18481-34 was investigated in the pivotal 13-, 26- and 39-week repeatdose in mouse, rat and dog, respectively, and embryo-foetal development studies in rat and rabbit.

Absorption of glepaglutide was evaluated in a comprehensive number of studies and when compared to information in the clinical dossier, pharmacokinetics of glepaglutide appear in general to be comparable.

Across species, subcutaneous glepaglutide was greatly metabolised and released to the systemic circulation in the form of two main pharmacologically active metabolites, ZP18481-34 and ZP18481-35. Therefore, the overall pharmacological effect of glepaglutide should be seen as the combined effect of the parent compound and the two main metabolites. Of the three, ZP18481-34 was the major exposure responsible compound across species. Overall, systemic absorption of glepaglutide was low, with the compound largely retained within the subcutaneous compartment. Release from the injection site appeared to be the rate-limiting step for systemic exposure. Only 30 (following a single dose) to 40% (after multiple doses) of the administered parent compound was detected in the systemic circulation as one of the three analytes. In most species, systemic exposure to the parent compound increased with increasing treatment duration.

Across non-clinical species, the proportional exposure of the parent and metabolite compounds changes over time. In general, overall exposure increased with dose, varying from less than dose proportional to more than dose proportional. Based on information in the clinical dossier, the distribution of analytes in humans did not appear to significantly change from first dose to steady state when evaluated following once weekly dosing. Despite the apparent differences in proportional exposure of the three analytes between non-clinical species and humans - which should be acknowledged when extrapolating nonclinical findings to humans - these are not considered to introduce a particular clinical safety concern as AUC-based exposure margins of the three analytes and total glepaglutide (sum of glepaglutide, ZP18481-35 and ZP18481-34), based on the determined NOAELs in the pivotal repeat-dose and embryo-foetal toxicology studies, were sufficiently in excess (&gt;10) of the predicted adult human exposure at the clinical dose of 10 mg administered twice weekly.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

According to the applicant, the metabolites exhibit hGLP-2 receptor potency comparable to the parent compound. However, results from primary pharmacodynamic in vitro studies indicate that the potency of the two metabolites was significantly higher than that of glepaglutide, with ZP18481-34 being the most potent (see section 5.3.1.1). ZP18481-34 - accounting for the highest systemic exposure and demonstrating the greatest receptor potency - also demonstrated the longest T½ of the three pharmacologically active compounds. The enhanced potency and prolonged half-life of ZP18481-34 may suggest improved therapeutic effects and sustained receptor activation. No safety concerns however are caused by the higher proportion of ZP18481-34 in humans, since the exposure margins for ZP18481-34 exceeded ten at the NOAEL, which is considered sufficient.

Parent compound tmax was between 0.5 to 2 hours which for the metabolites, ZP18481-35 and ZP18481-34, ranged from 1˗12 and 4˗12 hours, respectively.

In long-term toxicology studies (rats, rabbits, dogs), a 1.8 to 10-fold accumulation of parent compound and metabolites was observed relative to Day 1. Accumulation is also observed in the clinical setting and reflected in 5.2 of the SmPC. Clinically, a modelled mean T½ for glepaglutide in SBS patients appears to be significantly longer than the T½ observed across non-clinical species. To account for these interspecies differences in plasma half-life, and to ensure sustained and adequate systemic exposure in relation to the anticipated clinical exposure profile, animals were dosed once daily.

Both in rats and mice carcinogenicity studies, it is observed that exposure of glepaglutide and ZP18481-34 increased significantly up to around week 12 but decreased thereafter to levels ≤ day 1. The cause for this decrease is unknown. Although high incidences of ADAs were observed, there was no clear or consistent relationship between ADA formation and exposure levels in these studies. Nevertheless, a similar decrease in exposure has not been observed in the clinical studies (up to 30 months of treatment) and the long-term efficacy of glepaglutide in humans is not considered to be compromised.

Glepaglutide and metabolites are peptides without unnatural modifications and lack albumin/Fc-binding motifs, show rapid clearance following IV administration and have no anticipated PPB-mediated interactions. A tissue distribution study using quantitative whole-body autoradiography following a single s.c. dose of [ 3 H-Phe 22 ]-glepaglutide in male Sprague Dawley rats indicated that the tissues with the highest radioactivity exposures in rats were bone marrow, pituitary gland, prostate and small intestine. Apart from tissue with known GLP-2 receptor activity, relatively high levels of radioactivity were also observed in organs with high peptide or protein synthesis, such as bone marrow, various glands, and seminal vesicles. This observation is likely due to reuse of 3 H-Phe in de novo peptide or protein synthesis. No placental transfer and milk excretion studies of glepaglutide and its metabolites have been conducted. Glepaglutide and its metabolites are large molecules, and it is expected that only a very small fraction will pass the placental barrier. As a risk to the breast-fed infant cannot be fully excluded, it is preferable to avoid the use of glepaglutide during breast-feeding which is reflected in the SmPC.

In accordance ICH guideline S6(R1) no traditional metabolism and excretion studies are warranted. Nevertheless, metabolite profiles of glepaglutide were determined in rat, dog and human plasma and no disproportionate drug metabolites were identified.

No pharmacokinetic drug interactions with glepaglutide are expected.

## Toxicology

A comprehensive toxicology package was provided in line with ICH guideline M3(R2) even though ICH guideline S6(R1) is also applicable. Glepaglutide was administered by daily subcutaneous injection in mice (up to 20 mg/kg), rats (up to 125 mg/kg), rabbits (EFD study only, up to 10 mg/kg) and dogs

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

(up to 25 mg/kg). PD studies showed that glepaglutide is pharmacologically active in all chosen toxicological species.

In all repeat-dose studies, glepaglutide-treated groups from the lowest dose onwards (~15-fold exposure margin) showed a clear PD effect on the small intestine, including increased weight/length, villous hypertrophy and mucosal hyperplasia in the duodenum, ileum and jejunum. In rodents, there were also PD effects on the large intestine and cecum, as evidenced by increased colon weight/length and distension, respectively. Associated microscopic changes only occurred in higher dose groups. Expected PD effects were also seen in gall bladders of mice (all doses) and dogs (high dose only), including dilatation and epithelial hyperplasia. The effects on the large intestine and gall bladder completely recovered after 6 weeks, whereas the effects on the small intestine were only partially reversible. Gall bladder disease is included in the RMP and events of cholecystitis, cholangitis and cholelithiasis have been reported in clinical studies with glepaglutide and are labelled adverse reactions in 4.8 of the SmPC.

A decrease in food consumption and/or body weight gain (mainly in higher dose groups) and reduced cholesterol (mainly after long-term treatment) were observed in several toxicology studies. These effects could be caused by off-target GLP-1 agonism by glepaglutide.

Repeat-dose studies also showed minor dose-dependent effects of glepaglutide on the liver and kidney, which were not completely reversible after 6 weeks. Besides slight increases of liver and kidney weight, there were changes in blood chemistry and urinalysis parameters, but there were no corresponding histopathological findings. The effects on liver and kidney function are likely due to the metabolism and excretion of glepaglutide and can be considered of no toxicological significance.

Injection site reactions consisting of inflammation, sometimes with fibrosis and degeneration, were dose-dependently observed in all studies from the lowest dose onwards and are therefore clinically relevant, though the dosing regimen of glepaglutide in patients is less frequent (twice weekly rather than daily). 4.8 of the SmPC includes injection side reactions as a very common adverse reaction. Reactions were associated with minor haematological changes (mainly increased white blood cells) and partially recovered after 6 weeks.

Taken together, in GLP-compliant repeat-dose toxicity studies up to 6 months in rats and up to 9 months in dogs, the systemic NOAELs were 10 mg/kg/day (100-fold exposure margin) and 1 mg/kg/day (52-fold exposure margin), respectively. Due to injection site reactions at all dose levels, there was no local NOAEL established.

According to the ICH S6 guidance, the evaluation of genotoxicity of biotechnology products is not mandatory as proteins are considered to be non-genotoxic. Glepaglutide was negative in standard GLP-compliant in vitro (bacterial and mammalian) and in vivo (rat) genotoxicity studies. Section 5.3 of the SmPC with regard to genotoxicity is adequately reflected.

The carcinogenicity studies were conducted in mice (0.1-1 mg/kg/day) and rats (0.2-2 mg/kg/day). Due to reduced exposure after 12 weeks in both studies, the exposure margins are uncertain. In rats, the endpoint exposures were still adequate for the whole dose range (4 to 23-fold exposure margin), though in mice, endpoint exposure was absent/minimal (2-fold margin at high dose). Despite the questionable TK profile, PD-related non-neoplastic changes were seen from the lowest dose onwards in both species, in line with what was observed in the repeat-dose toxicity studies.

Neoplastic changes were observed in the duodenum (adenoma and adenocarcinoma), though only in male rats. Such tumors are rare, though the finding was not unexpected given the pharmacology of glepaglutide. There was no NOAEL for glepaglutide-related intestinal tumors in the rat study (lowest dose corresponded to a 4-fold exposure margin at endpoint). The SmPC recommends Section 4.4. colonoscopy of patients before treatment starts and then in regular intervals.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Both carcinogenicity studies showed an increased incidence of neoplastic skin findings at the SC injection site, consisting of (fibro)sarcomas. The applicant claims that the sarcomas were likely caused by the vehicle since these tumors were also found in the control mice and rats. However, in male rats the incidence clearly increased dose-dependently (from mid dose, 11-fold exposure margin at endpoint). Literature indicates that fibrosarcomas are commonly induced by repeated SC injection of compounds in rodents and are thought to result from chronic inflammatory processes. A potential correlation with the inflammatory injection site reactions caused by glepaglutide was not discussed. Nevertheless, injection site fibrosarcomas in rodents are considered to have low relevance for human risk assessment. Any risk is further reduced by the less frequent dosing schedule that is proposed for patients. Section 5.3 of the SmPC with regard to carcinogenicity is adequately reflected.

Developmental and reproductive toxicity studies were conducted in rats and rabbits. In a rat fertility study, glepaglutide had no effect on reproduction and the NOAEL was considered 10 mg/kg/day (estimated exposure margin at least 50-fold). An EFD study in rat showed skeletal variations (bent scapulae, short/thickened long bones) that were likely secondary to maternal toxicity. Treatment at 10 mg/kg was associated with four mortalities with signs of GI tract abnormalities. Embryo-foetal toxicity in terms of litter death was also observed at this dose. The embryo-fetal NOAEL of this study (2 mg/kg/day) had a 10-fold exposure margin. Glepaglutide did not affect pre- or postnatal development in rats (NOAEL 2 mg/kg/day, estimated 10-fold exposure margin). In the rabbit EFD study, there were no effects on litters or fetal pathology (NOAEL 10 mg/kg/day, 46-fold exposure margin).

Glepaglutide was also SC administered to neonatal rats (PND10-65, 1-5 mg/kg/day). Similar glepaglutide treatment effects as well as pharmacokinetics were observed as compared to the repeatdose toxicity studies in adult rat. Glepaglutide did not cause adverse effects on the developing GI tract or other organs (NOAEL 5 mg/kg/day, 35-fold exposure margin).

There are no concerns regarding antigenicity, immunotoxicity, dependence, metabolites, impurities, excipients or phototoxicity.

## ERA

The active substance is a natural substance, the use of which will not alter the concentration or distribution of the substance in the environment. Therefore, glepaglutide is not expected to pose a risk to the environment.

## 4.5.2. Conclusions

Glepaglutide and its main metabolites are high affinity agonists of the -2R. The proof of concept of the primary pharmacology of glepaglutide has been proven in rats but was limited to increases in small and large intestinal mass, and enhanced intestinal absorption as determined by plasma citrulline levels. Daily dosing is needed in rats to maintain the pharmacological effect. Glepaglutide and its main metabolite are moderate agonists of the GLP-1R.

The pharmacokinetics of glepaglutide have been sufficiently summarized. Overall, the pharmacokinetics and toxicokinetics from the pivotal non-clinical animal species is similar to the kinetics of glepaglutide in humans. Metabolism between animal species and humans is comparable. The rat and dog are acceptable species as pivotal toxicity species.

The toxicology of glepaglutide has been sufficiently described. Any adverse effects were observed at sufficiently high exposure multiples, thus the relevance of these toxicities for the clinic is considered limited. Most systemic findings could be attributed to the pharmacology of glepaglutide, including the increased incidence of neoplastic changes in the duodenum, for which no safety margin could be established. This is appropriately labelled in 5.1 of the SmPC, and the large bowel will be followed up in patient at treatment start and in regular intervals thereafter as described in 4.4 of the SmPC.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 5. Clinical aspects

## 5.1. Introduction

## 5.1.1. Good Clinical Practice (GCP) aspects

The clinical studies were performed in accordance with GCP as claimed by the applicant.

The applicant has provided a statement to the effect that clinical studies conducted outside the community were carried out in accordance with the ethical standards of Directive 2001/20/EC.

Two GCP inspections have been conducted. One was conducted by the Danish Medicines Agency (2020), another one was conducted by the FDA (2024).

## GCP inspection Danish Medicines Agency 2020

From 4 until 7 February 2020 the Danish Medicines Agency conducted a GCP inspection at the sponsor site of study ZP1848-17111 (EASE-1). The inspectors concluded based on random checks that the investigator site generally performed the study in accordance with the protocol and current legislation. The inspectors observed major deviations regarding the audit trial in the Healthcare Platform, source data and monitors' direct access to source data. However, no consistent differences between active treatment groups and placebo were observed. It is concluded that the DKMA inspection findings do not hamper interpretation of the study results of the EASE-1 study (see section 3.4.3).

## GCP inspection Food and Drug Administration 2024

The Food and Drug Administration (FDA) of the United States of America inspected 4 clinical investigators () and the study sponsor (Zealand Pharma A/S in Denmark) for studies ZP1848-17111 (EASE-1), ZP1848-17127 (EASE-2) and ZP1848-20110 (EASE-3).

The inspection at found two unreported serious adverse events (acute kidney injury and hypomagnesemia) and several non-serious adverse events including one adverse event of special interest (elevated AST/ALT).

The FDA concluded that apart from the inspection findings at, based on the overall inspection results, the clinical data generated by the inspected sites and submitted by the sponsor are acceptable.

Based on the review of clinical data and the above-mentioned GCP inspections, the CHMP did not identify the need for a further triggered GCP inspection of the clinical studies included in this dossier (see section 3.4.3).

## 5.2. Clinical pharmacology

It is important to note that throughout the submitted dossier and this document, glepaglutide is used interchangeably to refer to the to-be-marketed drug product, the parent drug glepaglutide (ZP1848), and to the combined total of the parent drug and its main metabolites M1 (ZP2711) and M2 (ZP2469). Where possible, clarification is provided by specifying 'parent' when referring solely to the parent compound.

## 5.2.1. Methods

## 5.2.1.1. Bioanalytical methods

Analysis of glepaglutide parent, and M1 and M2 in plasma

The determination of parent drug glepaglutide (ZP1848), and the main metabolites M1 (ZP2711) and M2 (ZP2469) in human plasma were based on a validated immunoaffinity extraction followed by liquid chromatography coupled to tandem mass spectrometry. The mean metabolite profile of glepaglutide parent was obtained by immunoaffinity liquid chromatography mass spectrometry analysis of pooled plasma samples (limit of detection: 0.3-1.0 nM) obtained from the first-in-human study 08216.

During initiation of the assay preparing for analysis of samples from the phase 3 study, EASE-1, unacceptable carry-over was observed; the resolution for this carry-over issue involved application of a new analytical column and new chromatographic conditions. A partial validation (study no. 305163, date: 09/06/2023) included evaluation of assay selectivity, matrix factor, assay carry-over, assay linearity, and intra- and inter batch assay accuracy and precision. The long-term stability has been established for 392 days at -80°C to -20°C, since the samples in EASE-1 and EASE-2 were stored for a maximum of 743 days and 683 days, respectively, not all samples were analysed within established stability. The hybrid ligand-binding method showed 83.0% of ISR values for ZP1848 within ±30%.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Immunogenicity

A tiered assay approach has been implemented for the assessment of glepaglutide immunogenicity. The binding antibody assays are ELISA-type assays using plates coated with parent drug for capturing potential ADA and detecting using labelled protein A/G. \"Screened positive\" (Tier 1) results are confirmed by immunodepletion (Tier 2) with an excess amount of drug. The parent drug contains also the epitopes represented in the metabolites and has therefore been maintained as capturing agent in the screening assay. The detected antibodies constitute the total pool of anti-glepaglutide antibodies or \"anti-drug antibodies\" (ADA). Confirmed ADA-positive samples are further characterized (Tier 3): Binding to the predominant active metabolite, M2, is investigated, and the in vitro glepaglutideneutralizing potential of the ADA is investigated in a cell-based activity assay using parent (ZP1848) as stimulatory agent.

The level of the detected drift (mean drift, M2 Bab LPC = 22.7% (Study 20-053), mean drift, GLP-2 CR LPC = 16.8% (Study 20-055)) did not appear to impact the assay sensitivity, as illustrated by the LPC failure rate during validation (0/70 for GLP-2 CR and 2/30 for M2 BAb).

The anti-glepaglutide antibody screening assay cut point was calculated to give a 5% false positive rate. The overall false positive rate (FPR) in the clinical studies was calculated using the study baseline samples and was 12.2%, and thus it is considered unlikely that true positive samples have been missed.

The overall LPC failure rate of 2.2% confirms the reliability of the assay sensitivity. For all clinical study sample analysis, the screening cut-points were equal to or lower than the validation cut-points.

## 5.2.1.2. Evaluation and qualification of models

## 5.2.1.2.1. Population pharmacokinetics

The population PK analysis was conducted using the NONMEM software (version 7.5.1) for nonlinear mixed-effects models running under Perl-speaks-NONMEM (PsN; version 5.3.1). Model estimation was performed using the first-order conditional estimation with interaction (FOCE INTER) method. Data management was performed in SAS (version 9.4). Graphical explorations, analysis of results, and model-based simulations were performed using R (version 4.2.3).

The population PK model was based on PK rich data observations of healthy volunteers (ZP08-216, ZP1848-16182, ZP1848-18131, ZP1848-19045, and ZP1848-15073) and sparse PK data sampling SBS patients (ZP1848-17111 (EASE-1), ZP1848-17127 (EASE-2), and ZP1848-20060 (EASE-4)). The analysis dataset included 28460 observations from 363 subjects, with respectively a total of 9485, 9489, and 9486 PK samples for glepaglutide parent (P), M1, and M2. Observations that were below the limit of quantification (BLQ) were respectively 66.0%, 28.7%, and 12.9% of the total number of observations for P, M1, and M2. All BLQ data was handled per M1 BLQ method. The analysis population consisted of 155 females (43.2%) and 204 males (56.8%) with a median (range) age of 44 years (18 to 82 years), and a median (range) baseline body weight of 72.1 kg (42.4 to 107 kg). The majority of participants identified as white (77.2%), classified as normal renal function (71.9%) and as normal hepatic function (82.7%).

The final model was identified as a joint parent-metabolite model with administration of glepaglutide into the parent drug subcutaneous (SC) depot compartment, dose-dependent conversion of parent drug to M1 in the SC depot, and absorption from the 2 depot compartments into circulation. Once in circulation, the PK of parent drug and M1 was described by 2-compartment models, while M2 was described by 1-compartment kinetics. Covariate evaluation identified total injection volume and injection site (thigh versus other) as having an effect on the absorption rate of M1 into the central compartment (KAM1) and the rate of parent drug to M1 conversion in the depot compartment (KPTOM1), respectively. The final model further included the effects of renal impairment versus normal (or unknown) renal function, Black versus non-Black race, male versus female sex, and the effect of time-dependent ADA presence on M2 clearance.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 5.2.2. Pharmacokinetics

Since the metabolites have similar GLP-2 receptor potency to that of the parent drug, the PK/PD properties of glepaglutide are viewed as a combined effect of unmetabolized parent and its two main metabolites. Glepaglutide is referring to the conceptual analyte: the sum of parent drug + M1 + M2, unless stated otherwise.

## 5.2.2.1. Absorption

Mean steady state plasma concentration-time profiles for the glepaglutide parent and the two main metabolites (M1 and M2) are shown for healthy subjects receiving glepaglutide 10 mg OW (Figure 2). Plasma concentrations of parent drug peaked first after injection, followed by M1 and then M2.

Notes: Open markers: all values below the lower limit of quantification. Data presented for 10 mg (Group D). Parent: parent compound. M1: ZP1848 (1-35) (metabolite of glepaglutide). M2: ZP1848 (1-34) (metabolite of glepaglutide).

Figure 2. Mean concentration-time profiles for parent, M1, M2 and glepaglutide following OW dosing of 10 mg in healthy subjects - Trial 16182.

## 5.2.2.1.1. Bioequivalence

Six different formulations of glepaglutide (formulations A to F) have been used during the clinical development of glepaglutide. The to-be-marketed formulation (Formulation D: 20 mg/mL glepaglutide) was used in studies 18131, 19045, and all phase 3 studies, whereas a glepaglutide formulation strength of 10 mg/mL (Formulation F) was used in the phase 1 PK study 16182. The shape of the concentration-time profiles was similar for the two formulation strengths and there was substantial overlap in both individual subject concentration-time profiles and AUC0-∞ and Cmax values across three

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

recent studies (16182, 18131, and 19045). Overall, the glepaglutide exposure appeared to be similar for formulation strengths of 10 and 20 mg/mL when the same dose is applied.

## 5.2.2.1.2. Injection site

The overall population PK model showed that injection site had an impact on the conversion of parent to M1 in the SC depot. Higher glepaglutide exposure following injection in the abdomen vs. thigh in SBS patients was observed.

## 5.2.2.2. Distribution

Following IV dosing of healthy subjects (study 16182, group E), glepaglutide parent has a low to moderate volume of distribution at steady state (Vss = 14.0 L) indicating that distribution is largely limited to the extracellular body volume.

## 5.2.2.3. Metabolism

Clinical and non-clinical studies have shown that following SC injection, two main glepaglutide metabolites (M1 and M2) are formed via C-terminal proteolytic cleavage. Both active metabolites together with a smaller proportion of unmetabolized parent drug enter the systemic circulation, with the protracted release of M1 and M2 from the SC depot being the rate limiting step that governs overall plasma exposure and effective half-life. Metabolism continues in the circulation where M2 is the predominant metabolite at steady state. This is contrary to what is observed after IV administration where the parent drug is the main circulating compound indicating that the majority of the metabolism occurs in a SC compartment, suggesting that the majority of metabolism occurs within a SC depot compartment. The main route of elimination is considered to be via this proteolytic metabolism. The parent drug appears also to be eliminated by renal filtration, whereas renal clearance seems to be a minor elimination pathway of the metabolites.

## 5.2.2.4. Elimination

At steady state, plasma concentrations of glepaglutide decline in at least a tri-phasic manner, with a terminal elimination half-life of &gt;200 hours following multiple dosing in healthy subjects. However, because only a small fraction of the drug is eliminated during the terminal phase, effective half-life (t½, eff) is used as a measure of the steady state elimination kinetics of glepaglutide. For patients with SBS, the modelled mean t½, eff (range) is 125.6 (82.2; 176.3) hours for glepaglutide 10 mg OW and 68.2 (47.8; 105.1) hours for glepaglutide 10 mg twice-weekly.

Glepaglutide clearance in healthy subjects was 5.72 L/h, while the clearance in SBS patients was not determined.

## 5.2.2.5. Dose proportionality and time dependency

Dose proportionality at steady state was evaluated based on glepaglutide exposure (AUC0-τ) following once weekly doses of 5 mg and 10 mg glepaglutide in healthy subjects (study 16182). The estimated slope with 95% CI, from a linear model for log-transformed AUC0-τ including log transformed dose as covariate, was 1.24 [0.53; 1.95]. The variability of AUC0-τ between subjects was high, as reflected in the width of the confidence intervals. The geometric least square mean percentage ratios for AUC0-t comparing 10 mg vs. 5 mg showing values of 267% on day 1 and 221% on day 36. The other analyzed metrics (AUC0-inf, AUC0-last, and Cmax) show a similar relation with ratios between 213% and 277% (all with p-values &lt;0.005). The metrics thus suggest that the relationship is not strictly doseproportional between 10 mg and 5 mg.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Based on trough glepaglutide values (Ctrough), steady state was reached after 2 to 3 weeks of OW dosing. Pharmacokinetic parameters for glepaglutide are presented in Table 15. With OW dosing, glepaglutide had an accumulation ratio of 1.37 based on AUC0-168h.

## 5.2.2.6. Intra- and inter-individual variability

The best measure of glepaglutide intra- and inter-subject variability is provided by the 3-period crossover trial 19045 where ~70 healthy subjects received 3 single SC doses of glepaglutide 10 mg, with each dose separated by at least 7 weeks. In this trial, intra-subject variability (CV%) for AUC0-t and Cmax was 28.2% and 25.5%, respectively. The corresponding values for inter subject variability (CV%) were 36.4% and 39.7%, respectively. The intrasubject variability was predicted to be increased in patient studies compared to healthy volunteers' studies. However, it is not fully understood why this variability differs so markedly between healthy volunteers and patients. The source of this variability is unclear and may involve disease-related factors, comorbidities, concomitant medications, or differences in administration such as injection site, needle length, or technique. As a result, certain populations may experience subtherapeutic exposures, potentially impacting efficacy and safety.

## 5.2.2.7. Pharmacokinetics in the target population

The PK in healthy volunteers was primarily characterised in study 16182. The PK in patients with SBS was based on samples taken during the phase 3 studies (EASE-1 and EASE-2). In both healthy volunteers and in patients with SBS, the plasma concentrations of parent peaked first after dosing followed by M1 and then M2. The pharmacokinetics parameters are presented in Table 16.

For healthy subjects, mean t½, eff [95% CI] for glepaglutide 10 mg OW is 88.3 [31; 146] hours based on the observed increase in AUC0-τ by a factor of 1.37 at steady state compared with the first weekly dose of glepaglutide. For patients with SBS, the modelled mean t½, eff (range) is 125.6 (82.2; 176.3) hours for glepaglutide 10 mg OW and 68.2 (47.8; 105.1) hours for glepaglutide 10 mg TW.

Non-compartmental analysis (NCA) was used for the estimation of PK parameters for parent, the two main active metabolites (M1 and M2) and a calculated conceptual analyte, glepaglutide (the sum of parent, M1 and M2). For glepaglutide, the concentration-time profile was constructed as the sum of concentrations of the parent, M1, and M2 for the individual measuring time points. The shape of the PK profiles was similar for once-weekly (OW) and twice-weekly (TW) dosing up until patients on a TW regimen received their second dose (Figure 3). Thereafter, AUC tended to be higher with TW dosing for the remainder of the sampling timepoints because patients on TW received a second dose of glepaglutide. This is reflected by the ~60% higher observed mean value for AUC0-168h with TW vs. OW dosing (Table 17). Mean glepaglutide Cmax was ~20% higher with OW vs. TW. However, it should be noted that there was substantial between-patient variability across dosing and that Cmax was derived from a single measurement.

Absolute bioavailability has been calculated for glepaglutide parent based on the mean AUC0-∞ with IV dosing and following the initial SC dose of glepaglutide 10 mg OW. Due to extensive metabolism within the subcutaneous depot, the absolute bioavailability of parent drug is concluded to be low at 0.5%. However, as substantial amounts of M1 and M2 enter the plasma from the subcutaneous depot, the pharmacologically relevant bioavailability of glepaglutide is substantially higher.

## 5.2.2.8. Special populations

Simulations were conducted to evaluate the impact of covariates included in the final population PK model on glepaglutide exposure at steady state relative to exposures in a typical subject representative of the EASE-1 and EASE-2 studies. The reference scenario was defined as 10 mg glepaglutide administered TW into the abdomen/stomach of a non-Black male subject with no renal impairment and no ADA.

Glepaglutide exposure (AUC0-t and Cmax) decreases with increasing body weight in both healthy volunteers and SBS patients, based on both clinical data and the population PK analysis. The population PK model also predicted approximately 25% higher glepaglutide exposure in ADA-positive subjects, as a result of decreased clearance of M2. In the glepaglutide long-term phase 3 studies, ADA developed in approximately 90% of patients.

Based on the popPK analysis, the effect of sex and race on the glepaglutide PK were considered not clinically meaningful, and thus, no dose adjustments were proposed. In addition, no consistent association was found between renal or hepatic impairment and glepaglutide exposure in patients with SBS. Besides the population PK analysis, dedicated studies were performed in subjects with renal (study 18131) and hepatic (hepatic indocyanine green clearance study) impairment. Liver function categories were based on NCI-ODWG criteria. In accordance with the population PK analysis, glepaglutide exposures between SBS patients with impaired hepatic or renal function and normal hepatic or renal function were considered sufficiently comparable. Thus, no dose adjustments were proposed on the basis of hepatic or renal function. However, it should be noted that the evaluation for patients with moderate hepatic impairment is based on a low number of observations.

## 5.2.2.9. Pharmacokinetic interaction studies

Glepaglutide was evaluated in three in vitro studies conducted with human biomaterial (study 19-013, 19-014, and 19-155). All of these studies were conducted for the predominant metabolite of glepaglutide (M2), which constitutes approximately 90% of overall exposure at steady state.

Based on study 19013, M2 was determined to not be a reversible inhibitor of the cytochrome P450 isoforms CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6 and CYP3A4 human liver microsomes based on all IC50 values were above 5.

In study 19155, the potential of M2 to be an inducer of CYP1A2, CYP2B6 and CYP3A4 using both catalytic and mRNA endpoints across three donors in cryopreserved human hepatocytes was tested.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Only in donor 1 using an mRNA endpoint, a fold induction of 3.02 was observed for CYP3A4 at 0.01 μM.

Under the current assay conditions with the in vitro cell test systems utilized in study 19014, ZP2469 (ZP18481-34) has been determined not to be an inhibitor of transport mediated via OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OAT1, OAT3, OCT2, OCT1, MATE1, MATE2-K and BSEP, as there is no inhibition at IC50 (μM) for all included transporters.

## 5.2.3. Pharmacodynamics

## 5.2.3.1. Mechanism of action

Human GLP-2 is a 33-amino-acid intestinal growth factor secreted in response to nutrient intake.

Glepaglutide is a long-acting analogue of native human glucagon-like peptide 2 (GLP-2) that has been developed for the treatment of short bowel syndrome (SBS).

## Mechanism of action

GLP-2 is a specific, endogenous intestinal growth factor that plays a key role in enhancing intestinal mucosal morphology, function, and integrity under normal and pathophysiological conditions. Endogenous GLP-2 peptide is secreted in response to nutrient intake by L cells located all over the intestine with an increasing density towards the distal ileum and proximal colon, which are sections of the bowel that are frequently removed in patients with SBS (Drucker 1999). GLP-2 binds to and acts via a single G-protein-coupled receptor (Munroe et al. 1999), which has been localized in the small intestine, colon, stomach, and - to a much lesser extent - brain (Sinclair &amp; Drucker 2005).

Exogenous GLP-2 induces significant growth of the small intestinal mucosal epithelium via stimulation of stem cell proliferation in the crypts and via inhibition of apoptosis on the villi (Drucker et al. 1996). This trophic effect of GLP-2 has been observed in numerous species including humans (Wallis et al. 2007). Additional reported effects of GLP-2 include inhibition of gastric motility and gastric acid secretion, stimulation of nutrient absorption, enhancement of intestinal barrier function, and increased intestinal blood flow (Wallis et al. 2007).

Consistent with the mechanism of action of GLP-2, glepaglutide is postulated to improve intestinal absorptive capacity as measured by reductions in fecal wet weight output and increases in urine output and the absorption of wet weight/fluids, energy, and electrolytes. In addition, glepaglutide increases plasma citrulline levels (a biomarker of enterocyte mass).

## 5.2.3.2. Primary and secondary pharmacology

## Primary pharmacology

The pharmacodynamic effects of glepaglutide for SBS have been evaluated in Phase 2 study 15073 and Phase 3 study 20060 (EASE-4).

## 5.2.3.2.1. Study ZP1848-15073

## Study design

Study ZP184815073 is a Phase 2, single-centre (Copenhagen, Denmark), proof-of-concept, dosefinding, randomized, two-period cross-over, double-blind, fixed-dose study to evaluate efficacy and safety of three different once daily subcutaneous glepaglutide doses (0.1, 1 or 10 mg) in 18 SBS patients with intestinal insufficiency or intestinal failure. The pharmacodynamic outcomes were evaluated during 2 balance study periods (baseline and end of treatment) of 72 hours' duration (Figure 4).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Figure 4: Design study 15073

<!-- image -->

## Main inclusion criteria

SBS Patients with intestinal insufficiency or intestinal failure were included, if they met all of the following criteria:

- Following receipt of verbal and written information about the trial, the patient had to provide signed informed consent before any trial related activity was carried out
- Age ≥18 years and ≤90 years
- Stable SBS patients with intestinal insufficiency or failure, where the last surgical resection of gut tissue was performed at least 1 year ago
- A stable parenteral support (PS) volume (&lt;25% change in volume or energy content) for four weeks prior to randomization for patients requiring parenteral support

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

- Wet weight of faecal excretion ≥1500 g/day demonstrated during a hospital stay prior to screening or during at least one day of the first baseline balance study
- Stable body weight (&lt;5% weight deviance in the three months prior to screening)

## Test product, dose and mode of administration

The test product in this study was glepaglutide in three different doses; 0.1, 1 and 10 mg. Each patient received two different doses of glepaglutide in two 3-week treatment periods separated by a 4-week washout period.

Glepaglutide was self-administered by the patients by subcutaneous injection of 0.5 ml (with either 0.2, 2 or 20 mg glepaglutide/ml) once daily.

Batch number B2234 01 was used for all patients during the entire study.

## Sample size

As planned, 18 patients were randomized in the study. Of these, 16 patients provided post-dose data and were included in the efficacy analyses.

## Demography of the study population

The mean age was 62.4 years, ranging from 52 to 76 years. All 16 patients in the full analysis set were white, and the same number of women and men were enrolled. The patients had a mean BMI of 24.6 kg/m 2 , ranging from the lower end of normal weight (19.1 kg/m 2 ) to obesity class I (32.1 kg/m 2 ).

Eleven patients (68.8%), including the two patients with colon in continuity had intestinal failure, while five patients had intestinal insufficiency.

Overall, parenteral support was used by 68.8% of the patients in the full analysis set, and the average scheduled parenteral support volume was 2.9 l/day, ranging from 0.9 l/day to 6.1 l/day.

## Efficacy results

## Primary endpoint

Absolute change from baseline to the end of the 3-week treatment period of wet weight of ostomy output or diarhoea

The average wet weight output at baseline was approximately 3.4 kg/day in the 1 mg and 10 mg groups and 2.6 kg/day in the 0.1 mg group (Table 18).

## Primary analysis

In the primary analysis, statistically significant reductions in wet weight output were found for the combined 1 mg and 10 mg doses of glepaglutide (covariate adjusted mean -713 g/day, p&lt;0.0001), as well as for the 10 mg (-833 g/day, p=0.0002) and the 1 mg doses (-592 g/day, p=0.0021) separately (Table 19). No statistically significant effect of the 0.1 mg dose was observed (173 g/day, p=0.2743).

Similar results were obtained in sensitivity analyses and secondary analyses of the primary endpoint.

## Secondary endpoints

## Absolute and relative change of urine weight

The mean baseline urine weight (g/day) of the 0.1 (n=10), 1 (n= 11), and 10 mg/day (n=11) glepaglutide treatment arms were respectively 1482, 1675, and 1227 g/day. The covariate adjusted mean absolute changes in urine weight for the 0.1, 1, and 10 mg/day glepaglutide treatment arms were respectively +90.4 (95% CI -208.1; 388.8; p= 0.52), +530.1 (95% CI 244.5; 815.7; p&lt; 0.01), and +367.9 (95% CI 82.2; 653.5; p= 0.02) g/day. Similar trends were observed for the relative change of urine weight in the different treatment groups.

## Absolute and relative change in wet weight absorption (i.e. oral intake minus faecal/stoma output)

The mean baseline balance wet weight absorption (g/day) of the 0.1 (n=10), 1 (n= 11), and 10 mg/day (n=11) glepaglutide treatment arms were respectively 524.7, 537.9, and 287.8 g/day. The covariate adjusted mean absolute changes in wet weight absorption for the 0.1, 1, and 10 mg/day glepaglutide treatment arms were respectively -211.4 (95% CI -487.1; 64.3; p= 0.12), +650.0 (95%

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

CI 385.4; 914.5; p&lt; 0.01), and +785.8 (95% CI 523.0; 1048.6; p&lt; 0.01) g/day. Small relative changes in wet weight absorption were observed (p&gt; 0.05 for all treatment arms).

## Absolute and relative change in urine weight minus oral intake

The mean baseline balance urine weight minus oral intake (g/day) of the 0.1 (n=10), 1 (n= 11), and 10 mg/day (n=11) glepaglutide treatment arms were respectively -1644, -2294, and -2447 g/day. The covariate adjusted mean baseline balance urine weight minus oral intake for the 0.1, 1, and 10 mg/day glepaglutide treatment arms were respectively +117.6 (95% CI -244.3; 479.4; p= 0.49), +486.7 (95% CI 140.5; 833.0; p= 0.01), and +411.5 (95% CI 65.1; 757.8; p= 0.02) g/day. Similar trends were observed for the relative change of urine weight minus oral intake in the different treatment groups.

## Absolute and relative change in intestinal absorption of electrolytes

Increases in intestinal absorption of some electrolytes (sodium and potassium) were observed during treatment with 1 mg and 10 mg glepaglutide per day, while no changes were found for other electrolytes (calcium and magnesium).

## Energy and macronutrients

The observed mean absolute changes in intestinal energy absorption as measured by bomb calorimetry were 391.0 (1374.9) kJ/day in the 1 mg group, and 643.9 kJ/day in the 10 mg group. The calculated intestinal energy absorption increased in the combined 1 mg and 10 mg dose group (covariate adjusted mean 822 kJ/day; p=0.01) and in the 1 mg dose group (1099 kJ/day; p=0.02).

Overall increases in intestinal absorption of macronutrients (protein, carbohydrates, lipids) were observed in the 1 mg and/or 10 mg dose groups. No additional benefit was seen in macronutrient absorption when increasing the glepaglutide dose from 1 mg/day to 10 mg/day.

## Citrulline levels

The mean baseline citrulline levels (µmol/l), a biomarker of enterocyte mass, of the 0.1 (n=10), 1 (n= 11), and 10 mg/day (n=11) glepaglutide treatment arms were respectively 43.9, 46.5, and 42.6 g/day. The covariate adjusted mean absolute change in plasma citrulline levels for the 0.1, 1, and 10 mg/day glepaglutide treatment arms were respectively -1.4 (95% CI -9.3; 6.5; p= 0.69), +15.3 (95% CI 7.7; 22.9; p&lt; 0.01), and +15.6 (95% CI 8.2; 23.0; p&lt; 0.01) g/day. Hence, the increase in enterocyte mass tended to be larger for higher glepaglutide dosages.

## Quality of life

Small improvements in general quality of life as assessed by SF-36 were observed in the 0.1 mg and 1 mg dose groups, while a slight impairment in general quality of life was observed in the 10 mg dose group.

Based on explorative visual analogue scale on appetite and thirst, the patients' appetite was unchanged in the 0.1 mg group and decreased in the 1 mg and 10 mg groups from baseline to the end of the 3-week treatment periods. Thirst decreased from baseline to the end of the 3-week treatment periods in all dose groups.

For most of the 17 items of the SBS quality of life there was a small improvement in health-related quality of life in the 0.1 mg and 1 mg groups from baseline to the end of the 3-week treatment period (up to -2.2 points) and an impairment in health-related quality of life in the 10 mg group (up to +2.3 points).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 5.2.3.2.2. Study ZP1848-20060 (EASE-4)

## Study design

Study ZP1848-20060 is a Phase 3b single-centre, open-label study to evaluate the clinical effects of glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly during one year on intestinal absorption and nutritional status as well as long-term safety in 10 patients with stable SBS with or without the need for parenteral support.

Study patients received glepaglutide 10 mg once-weekly for 52 weeks, with a subsequent 4-week follow-up period (Figure 5).

Figure 5: Overall design study 20060

<!-- image -->

The study consisted of a screening period, a treatment period, and a follow-up period.

## Diagnosis and main criteria for inclusion

Patients with SBS with intestinal insufficiency (SBS-II) or intestinal failure (SBS-IF) who fulfilled the following main inclusion criteria were eligible for inclusion:

- Age ≥18 years and ≤90 years at screening.
- Stable condition of SBS either with intestinal failure or intestinal insufficiency. For patients with SBS-IF, a stable condition was defined as &lt;25% change in parenteral support (PS) volume or energy content for 4 weeks prior to screening.
- Stable body weight (&lt;5% change in weight in the 3 months prior to screening).
- Wet weight of faecal excretion ≥1,500 g/day demonstrated during a hospital stay prior to screening.

## Methodology

After signing informed consent, patients were screened for initial confirmation of eligibility. The screening procedures were performed within 3 weeks prior to visit 1 (baseline).

At visit 1, eligible patients completed a 48-hour in-house (admission to the hospital, at site) baseline metabolic balance study as well as other baseline assessments, after which they started treatment with glepaglutide 10 mg once-weekly for 52 weeks. The treatment period covered 12 visits.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

All patients had in-house stays for 48-hour metabolic balance studies at baseline and at week 24 (visit 9). During the in-house periods, they followed an individual drinking menu but could vary the food intake according to their own choice.

All patients had a 48-hour period scheduled before each visit (except visit 1 and 9 where balance studies were done), during which they recorded their fluid intake and urine output.

## Fixed drinking menu

During the screening phase, each patient constructed an individual fixed drinking menu to be kept during all 48-hour \"pre-visit\" periods until visit 9. The individual drinking menu was to be followed during both scheduled in-house 48-hour metabolic balance studies (at visit 1 and 9). After visit 9, the patients were no longer restricted to a fixed drinking menu during the 48-hour periods, but they had to instead drink an amount that reflected their preferred or habitual fluid intake. They could only drink the same fluid items as in the drinking menu and had to record their intake as well as their urine output. Patients receiving parenteral support (patients with SBS and intestinal failure) had their parenteral support adjusted based on an algorithm which was used at each visit.

## Parenteral support volume

During the study, parenteral support volume and content were regulated according to changes in urine output, weight change and clinical symptoms of fluid overload. During the second part of the trial, this was based on a 48-hour measurement without a fixed drinking menu. Changes from baseline to week 12 (visit 6), week 24 (visit 9) and week 52 (visit 12) were measured.

## Requirement for parenteral support volume reduction:

IF: daily urine volume of the current visit was at least 10% higher than baseline urine volume.

THEN: new parenteral support volume (weekly) = current parenteral support volume (weekly) - 7 x absolute increase in daily urine volume.

Parenteral support volume and content could be changed for safety reasons, e.g., if clinical signs of laboratory values supported this or in case of clinical signs of fluid overload, as judged by the investigator.

In cases where a patient could not be fully compliant with the adherence to the prescribed parenteral support volume, the drinking menu, and/or may have missed a urine measurement (e.g., in case the patient had missed to report a urine measurement during the 48-hour measurement period), the investigator could still perform adjustments to the new parenteral support volume at the investigators discretion, in order to best match the needs of the patient and in adherence with the algorithm stated above. In that case, the reason for the change was documented in the eCRF.

The patients were instructed to contact the clinical site in case urine production fell below normal and if their clinical status changed.

## Increasing parenteral support volume:

Increasing the parenteral support volume was considered if laboratory parameters indicated safety concerns. If the average urine production fell below 1 l per day, the investigator considered increasing parenteral support volume.

If parenteral support was not adjusted according to the algorithm (within ± 200ml/day), a reason for not adhering to the algorithm was provided in the eCRF.

A follow-up visit was performed 4 weeks after end of treatment.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

For each patient, the maximum study period was 59 weeks (3 weeks screening, 52 weeks treatment period, 4 weeks follow-up).

## Patient disposition

A minimum of 6 and a maximum of 16 patients with SBS intestinal failure (SBS-IF) or SBS intestinal insufficiency (SBS-II) patients were planned to be enrolled in the study.

A total of 12 patients were screened for enrolment in the study. Of these, 1 patient was a screen failure, and 1 patient was diagnosed with metastatic colorectal cancer and was withdrawn from the study before receiving investigational product; the patient subsequently passed away. Of the 10 patients who received the investigational product, 9 completed the full treatment phase of the trial (SBS-IF: N=9; SBS-II: N=2) and 1 patient had investigational product permanently discontinued (due to a serious adverse event of multiple metastasis of possible colorectal origin reported at week 40) and subsequently passed away.

The safety analysis set (SAS) equalled the full analysis set (FAS) and included all 10 treated patients.

Figure 6: Patient disposition study 20060

<!-- image -->

## Demographic and other baseline characteristics

For the total population (n=10), the mean age was 54.6 years, ranging from 27 to 78 years. All patients were white, non-Hispanic/Latino, and 50% were female. Patients had a mean BMI of 24.1 kg/m2, ranging from 18 to 27 kg/m2.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Most patients had intestinal failure (SBS-IF) (8 patients). The most common reason for SBS was Crohn's disease (5 patients), followed by mesenteric vascular disease (2 patients), other cause (2 patients) and surgical complications (1 patient). All patients with SBS-IF had SBS severity type III (chronic IF). The mean time since last resection was 81.0 months.

## Treatment compliance

Study patients were generally compliant with study medication. The mean (SD) drug exposure was 99.3 (1.6) %. There were 51.7 (4.9) administrations per patient out of the planned total of 52 administrations per patient up to visit 12 (week 52).

## Results

Primary endpoint change from baseline to week 24 in absorption of wet weight/fluids assessed by 48hour metabolic balance studies

For the total study population (n=10), mean absorption of wet weight/fluids increased from 841.6 g/day at baseline to 1240 g/day at week 24. This increase of 398.4 g/day was not statistically significant (minimum -606, maximum 1417; p=0.059).

Increases in the absorption of wet weight/fluids from baseline to week 24 were observed for patients (ranging from 42 to 1417 g/day), whereas decreases were noted for 2 patients (ranging from 121 to 606 g/day). The changes in wet weight/fluid absorption were similar for patients with SBS-IF and SBSII.

## Post-hoc analysis of the primary endpoint

A post-hoc analysis of the primary endpoint was performed using a linear mixed model adjusting for baseline parenteral support volume. Adjustment for baseline parenteral support volume was done since higher baseline parenteral support volumes have been shown to be associated with great reductions in absolute parenteral support volume requirements (i.e., greater improvements in intestinal absorption) (Jeppesen et al. 2018).

With adjustment for baseline parenteral support volume, the absorption of wet weight or fluids from baseline to week 24 upon glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly was statistically significant (LS means 398.4 (95% CI 29.0; 767.8); p= 0.04).

## Key secondary endpoint

## Change from baseline to week 24 in absorption of energy (oral intake minus faecal excretion) measured by 48-hour metabolic balance studies.

The mean absorption of energy increased from 7350.1 kJ/day at baseline to 8387.8 kJ/day at week 24 of 10 mg glepaglutide once weekly, an increase of 1037.7 kJ/day (nominal p= 0.02; 95% CI not reported).

## Other secondary endpoints

## Change in absorption of individual macronutrients (carbohydrates, lipids, and proteins) from baseline to week 24

The mean absorption of carbohydrates increased from baseline to week 24 by 39.8 g/day, lipids by 10.5 g/day, and proteins by 1.0 g/day. Overall, 4 patients had concomitant increases in the absorption of carbohydrates, lipids, and proteins from baseline to week 24.

Change in absorption of electrolytes (sodium, potassium, calcium, and magnesium) from baseline to week 24

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The mean absorption of sodium increased from baseline to week 24 by 28 mmol/day, potassium by 1.9 mmol/day, and calcium by 1.3 mmol/day, whereas the absorption of magnesium decreased by a mean of 2.2 mmol/day.

## Change in weekly parenteral support volume

The mean weekly parenteral support volume decreased from 17.8 l at baseline to 13.1 l at week 12 and 12.5 l at week 24, corresponding to mean decreases from baseline of 4.7 l at week 12 and 5.3 l at week 24. In line with this finding, the amounts of parenteral support macronutrients and electrolytes also decreased.

In line with the study protocol, 48-hour total drinking volume (ml) adjustments were reported for a limited number of study patients (up to 3 patients at a time point) until week 24. After week 24, (almost) all included study patients had ≥2 of such drinking volume (ml) adjustments.

Almost all evaluable study patients had ≥2 adjustments in the weekly parenteral support volume as compared to baseline. The mean reduction in the weekly parenteral support volume as compared to baseline in respective study patients tended to increase from -5.4 l at week 12 to -6.5 l at week 52.

## Secondary pharmacology

## Concentration-QTc analyses in healthy subjects

A concentration-QTc analysis was conducted on data from study 08216 Part 1, in which healthy subjects received a single dose of glepaglutide of up to 96.0 mg or placebo. Three candidate exposure-response models were developed: one model including parent, M1 and M2, one model including the M1 and M2, and one model (the primary model) including M2 only. Key conclusions of the exposure-response analyses were:

- A very shallow and statistically non-significant slope of the concentration-QTcF relationship was observed with all candidate models (i.e., there were no dose-dependent effects on QTcF).
- With the model including M2 only (primary model), the mean predicted ∆∆QTcF effect was only 1.2 ms [-3.4 to 5.8]90% CI at a Cmax of 65.4 nM for this metabolite.
- An upper bound QT effect below 10 ms could be predicted up to parent drug plasma concentrations of approximately 4 nM with all models.

## Exploratory PK analyses to predict Cmax in SBS patients

Exploratory PK analyses were conducted on data from study 08216 Part 2 (patients with Crohn's disease) and study 15073 (patients with SBS) to predict Cmax at clinically relevant doses. Key conclusions of the analyses were:

- For parent drug, the probability of a Cmax of &gt;1.5 nM was less than 1% after dosing with 10 mg glepaglutide daily for a minimum of 7 days.
- For M2, the probability of a Cmax of &gt;30 nM was less than 1% after dosing 10 mg glepaglutide daily for a minimum of 7 days.

## 5.2.3.3. Pharmacodynamic interactions with other medicinal products or substances

In vitro studies have shown no potential for glepaglutide to inhibit or induce CYP450 enzymes, and no potential to inhibit human drug transporter proteins. No pharmacodynamic interactions between glepaglutide and other particular medicinal products have been reported thus far. No interaction studies have been performed. However, based on the pharmacodynamic effects of glepaglutide there is a potential for increased absorption of orally concomitantly administered medicinal products. It is therefore recommended to increase clinical and/or laboratory monitoring of medicinal products which have a narrow therapeutic index or require clinical monitoring (see SmPC section 4.5).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In addition, fluid overload and dehydration should be avoided in SBS patients. Therefore, careful adjustment of parenteral support is required in patients taking glepaglutide. Electrolyte balance and fluid status should be carefully monitored throughout treatment especially during initial treatment and after discontinuation of glepaglutide treatment (see SmPC section 4.4).

## 5.2.3.4. Genetic differences in PD response

The applicant has not evaluated genetic differences in the pharmacodynamic response of glepaglutide. Glepaglutide is a GLP-2 analogue that is used for supplementation treatment in SBS patients. It increases gastro-intestinal absorption in SBS patients. Because of its local action on the gastrointestinal tract in persons with and without SBS, genetic differences in the pharmacodynamic response of glepaglutide are not foreseen. Because of this, it is acceptable that genetic differences in the pharmacodynamic response of glepaglutide have not been evaluated.

## 5.2.3.5. Immunological events

## ADA impact on efficacy

Potential associations between ADA and efficacy were investigated graphically by plotting the individual patients' change from baseline 1 in actual weekly parenteral support volume over time, color coded in accordance with the titer category at the visit, and looking at the treatment continuation pool only. Both associations with glepaglutide-binding antibodies (ADA), M2 binding antibodies (M2 BAb), glepaglutide-neutralizing antibodies (NAb) and GLP-2 cross-reactivity were investigated.

In addition, the individual change from baseline parenteral support volume by month 6, 12, 18, 24 and 30 for the two treatment groups was plotted against the corresponding actual individual titers in scatter plots.

Overall, no apparent associations were seen between the change from baseline parenteral support volume at months 6, 12, 18, 24, and 30 and corresponding titer values for either ADA, M2 BAb, NAb or GLP-2 CR, as there was no trend for diminished parenteral support change with increasing titers of either of the ADA assessments as compared to the parenteral support change seen for the corresponding ADA negative patients (Figure 7).

## ADA impact on safety

Despite the high ADA incidences, there is no indication that antibody formation is associated with clinically relevant effects on safety during treatment with glepaglutide for up to 4.5 years.

In total 3 events in 3 patients were medically evaluated by Zealand to constitute potential generalized hypersensitivity reactions, where a causal relationship to glepaglutide cannot be excluded. A relationship with glepaglutide ADA cannot be ruled out, since the 3 patients were ADA positive at the time of the events. However, the majority of patients dosed with glepaglutide developed ADA, and the data show that the development of anti-glepaglutide antibodies is not generally associated with hypersensitivity reactions.

A total of 75 patients experienced injection site reactions, and when looking at all patients dosed with glepaglutide, an apparent association with ADA was observed, as higher event rates were seen in patients in the high titer (event rate, all adverse events: 685.2) and low titer (event rate, all adverse events: 518.4) as compared to the ADA negative category (event rate, all adverse events: 88.78).

Injection site reactions generally preceeded the development of ADA, whereas ADA were rarely detected before the occurrence of injection site reactions. In addition, there was no indication that presence of ADA increased either the frequency or the severity of the injection site reactions compared to in absence of ADA, see Figure 8 .

## 5.2.5. Dose selection and therapeutic window

## 5.2.5.1. Dose selection

## 5.2.5.1.1. Dose selection for Phase 3

Dose selection for Phase 3 was based on data from the Phase 2 study 15073, in which the primary endpoint (reduction in wet weight of ostomy/diarrhea output as a measure of increased intestinal absorption) was assessed after 3 weeks of daily dosing (i.e., where the pharmacokinetics were in a steady state). By fitting a sigmoid Emax model to the results for the primary endpoint of study 15073 and comparing with pharmacokinetic data from the Phase 1 study 16182 (where healthy subjects were dosed once weekly), the evaluation indicated that both glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly and twice weekly would result in glepaglutide concentrations (the sum of parent, M1 and M2) within the therapeutically effective dose range (Figure 9).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Investigating the assumption that a minimum plasma concentration is needed to drive efficacy, Figure 14 depicts the results of corresponding exposure-response modeling based on minimum ('trough') plasma concentration (Cτ). The analysis predicted that a glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly dosing regimen would result in plasma concentrations within the therapeutically effective range for several days after dosing, but possibly falling below the therapeutically effective level during the latter phase of each weekly dosing interval (Figure 14; bottom panel), in contrast to the twice weekly dosing regimen where levels are predicted to remain within the therapeutically effective range throughout the week. Nevertheless, the once weekly dosing regimen was expected to result in a therapeutically relevant overall effect. This expectation was based on the current knowledge of biological activities related to GLP-2 receptor activation, which has been shown to play a central role as a trophic regulator of mucosal function. The effects of increasing crypt cell proliferation led to a lengthening of the intestinal villi and an expansion of the crypt compartment, with these changes taking days to modulate. Consequently, intermittent stimulation of GLP-2 receptors, as potentially resulting from a once weekly dosing regimen, was expected to be sufficient for inducing therapeutically relevant effects, as also observed in other studies and supported by pre-clinical studies with glepaglutide.

## 5.2.5.1.2. Exposure-response analyses for efficacy

The efficacy measure for all exposure-response analyses was individual patient change in actual weekly parenteral support (PS) volume from baseline to week 24, the primary endpoint in study EASE-1.

Glepaglutide exposure was investigated in terms of individual patient Ctrough and Caverage (all nominal measurements pooled from studies EASE-1 and EASE-2), Cmax and individual patient AUC0-168h and Cmax (measured in the EASE-2 PK sub-study). Both observed and PK model-derived values were investigated for Ctrough and Caverage.

Separate analyses were carried out for glepaglutide, parent, M1 and M2. Analyses were conducted with and without adjustment for stratification factor 1 and parenteral support volume at baseline.

## Results

No apparent association was observed between Ctrough or Caverage and change from baseline in actual weekly parenteral support volume after 24 weeks of treatment. However, higher AUC0-168h was associated with greater reductions in parenteral support volume (Figure 15). This association was stronger with adjustment for stratification factor and parenteral support volume at baseline.

Results were inconclusive for Cmax: no apparent association was noted for observed Cmax following adjustment for stratification factor and parenteral support volume at baseline, whereas a negative association was indicated between PK model derived Cmax and reductions in parenteral support volume (Figure 11).

## 5.2.5.1.3. Exposure-response analyses for safety

Two safety endpoints were selected for exposure-response analyses based on a dose-response relationship suggested by the clinical data:

## 1) Alkaline phosphatase (ALP)

## Background

Patients with SBS are at increased risk of liver impairment, especially if they receive longterm treatment with parenteral support. In EASE-1 and EASE-2, liver function test values showed a decreasing trend over time or little or no change from baseline. Results suggested a larger decrease in ALP and GGT levels for twice weekly versus once weekly dosing, which was most prominent for ALP.

## Results

Using the exposure parameters (Cave, Ctrough and Cmax) derived from the PopPK model, no apparent association was found between glepaglutide exposure and relative change in ALP levels from baseline (Figure 12).

## 2) Events of gastrointestinal stenosis and intestinal obstruction

## Background

Gastrointestinal stenosis and intestinal obstruction are known class effects of treatment with GLP-2 RAs. Event rates were higher for glepaglutide twice weekly than glepaglutide once weekly, suggesting a dose-response. To have enough events in the analyses, data from EASE-3 2 is included in addition to EASE-1 and EASE-2.

## Results

Using the exposure parameters (Cave, Ctrough and Cmax) derived from the PopPK model, no pattern was observed in scatter plots of glepaglutide exposure versus the number of events of gastrointestinal stenosis and intestinal obstruction (Figure 13). These observations are supported by the negative binomial regression analyses, where no significant effect of exposure was found.

No pattern was observed between glepaglutide exposure and time to event of gastrointestinal stenosis and intestinal obstruction. These observations were supported by the Cox regression analyses, where no significant effect of exposure was found.

2 This is an ongoing open-label, single-arm, phase 3 extension trial in patients completing EASE-2.
