## 5.2.6. Citrulline-response analysis for efficacy

## Introduction

Plasma levels of citrulline are a biomarker of enterocyte mass. The efficacy measure for the citrullineresponse analysis was individual patient change in actual weekly parenteral support (PS) volume from baseline to week 24, the primary endpoint in study EASE-1.

The relationship between individual patient plasma citrulline concentration at week 24 and change from baseline in parenteral support volume has been investigated for study EASE-1.

## Results

There was a weak association between citrulline levels and change in actual weekly parenteral support volume from baseline to week 24 but this was not statistically significant (estimate of slope: -0.89 (95% CI - 2.83; 1.04), p= 0.3598, Figure 14).

## 5.2.7. Overall discussion and conclusions on clinical pharmacology

## 5.2.7.1. Discussion

## Pharmacokinetics

A total of 8 completed clinical pharmacology studies have been conducted in support of glepaglutide: three dedicated PK studies have been conducted (study 16182, 19045 and 18131) in addition to a first-in-man study (study 08216). Furthermore, PK and/or PD data are also provided by one phase 2 study (study 15073) and three phase 3 studies ('EASE-1' (study 17111), 'EASE-2' (study 17127), and 'EASE-4' (study 20060)). In addition to individual clinical study data, a population-PK model has been developed to evaluate any potential association between PK and intrinsic factors and to investigate the relationship between exposure and response. Following injection, two main active glepaglutide metabolites, M1 and M2, are formed in the SC depot and account for approximately 98% of overall glepaglutide exposure at steady state. Overall, the parent drug, M1 and M2 account for respectively 1%, 7% and 91% of the glepaglutide exposure. Since the metabolites have similar GLP-2 receptor potency to that of the parent drug, the PK/PD properties of glepaglutide are viewed as a combined effect of unmetabolized parent and its two main metabolites.

## Methods

The bioanalytical methods used for the analysis of glepaglutide, and M1 and M2 in plasma were in general sufficiently validated. However, during the initial bioanalysis of samples from the EASE-1 study unacceptable carryover was identified, requiring a partial validation due to changes to the analytical column and chromatographic conditions. While partial validation supported the subsequent extension studies, including EASE-2, it raises questions regarding the appropriateness of the initial validation. While it would have been preferable for the applicant to provide a cross-validation report considering that the concentration (PK) data from the phase I, II, and III studies are intended to be pooled in the popPK analysis, it is acknowledged that a partial validation covering relevant parameters was successfully performed, and that the use of stable isotope-labelled internal standards appropriately addresses potential matrix effects.

Furthermore, the long-term stability has been established for 392 days at -80°C to -20°C, since the samples in EASE-1 and EASE-2 were stored for a maximum of 743 days and 683 days, respectively, not all samples were analysed within established stability. An additional sensitivity analysis was conducted for EASE-2, comparing results with and without inclusion of the 7 samples. This analysis demonstrated that the inclusion of these samples had no impact on the relevant PK parameters. The overall validation of the multi-tiered approach to anti-glepaglutide antibody testing and characterization is considered sufficient.

Immunogenicity was evaluated by a tiered approach. Samples were first evaluated in a glepaglutide parent ADA screening/confirmatory assay followed by titer determination. Confirmed ADA positives were further characterized in a cell based NAb assay, in a M2 ADA assay and in an endogenous GLP-2 ADA assay (cross reactivity). The applicant argued that an ADA assay detecting ADAs against parent drug as a tier-1 assay would also detect ADA's directed toward the assumed more pharmacological relevant main active metabolite M2, which seems confirmed by the comparable high incidence of Parent ADAs (tier-1 assay) and M2 ADAs (tier-2 assay).

## PopPK analysis

In general, model development steps, covariate analysis, model validation, and model application appear adequate and are satisfactorily summarised. Successful minimization was attained.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The pharmacokinetics of glepaglutide were described using a joint parent-metabolite model with 2 depot compartments for the absorption, describing administration of parent drug into a SC depot compartment and also dose-dependent conversion of parent drug into M1 in the SC depot. The PK was then after described using 2-compartment models for the parent drug and M1 and 1-compartment model for M2 once glepaglutide was in the circulation. All BLQ data was handled per M1 BLQ method, which can be considered acceptable in this case since the M3 method led to an unstable model and the most important compound M2, covering over 90% of the exposure, had a relative small number of BLQ data. The model parameters were fixed or estimated with good precision (RSE% range: 0.721 - 28.7), and shrinkage was low (≤ 13.7%). GoF and pcVPC plots did not indicate any major trends or misspecification, except for the pcVPC of parent drug at later time points where both the simulated median and the 95% CIs underpredicted the observed percentiles. This could possibly be explained by the fact that in only in study EASE-2 rich PK sampling was conducted, which is an extension of study EASE-1, meaning the earlier time points are mostly driven by healthy volunteer data up to 24 weeks. Based on the NCA, the exposure in healthy volunteer is lower than for patients, which could explain that the model simulated lower exposure at later time points in the pcVPC than observed in patients. The GoF plots demonstrate the overall exposure of M2, considered the most clinically relevant, appears comparable between healthy volunteers and patients. For the once-weekly and twice-weekly dosing regimens, the impact is not clearly discernible from the provided GoF plots and VPCs. However, dose-stratified plots show comparable precision across dose strengths (0.1-96 mg), with increased variability observed only at 10 mg. As this dose was the most frequently used in the pivotal trials and included the majority of subjects, the observed variability does not indicate a meaningful concern regarding the different dosing regimens. Covariate evaluation based on forward addition (significant if P&lt;0.01) followed by backward elimination (significant if P&lt;0.001). An additional, sensitivity analysis including the bioanalytical method as a covariate on PK, covering three separate analytical methods was performed. This analysis demonstrated no relevant impact of the bioanalytical method on the conclusions regarding the PK properties of glepaglutide. Effect of weight was not incorporated in the model even glepaglutide is applied as a fixed dose. Due to the complex structure of the model, it is not straightforward to evaluate body weight as a covariate. Forest plots including body weight indicated that subjects of higher body weight (91.5 kg) experience significantly reduced exposure compared to a subject of 62 kg. The applicant concludes this is not clinically relevant based on scatterplots of Cavg and Ctrough concentrations versus BW do not show clear correlations with BW in patients from EASE 1 and 2. In addition, richly sampled healthy volunteer data collected in Study 19-045, also including effect of BW (54.3 kg to 97.2 kg) on AUC and Cmax were analysed and show a slight decrease in exposure with increasing bodyweight. In addition, because body weight has influence on exposure, it was evaluated for impact on efficacy. The analysis was defined as placebo-corrected PS volume change from baseline representing results across 24 weeks treatment in pooled visits of EASE1 and EASE 2, with response data split into baseline body weight (BW at study entrance) tertiles e.g &gt;55kg, 55-70kg, &gt;70 kg. The analysis was further stratified per treatment regimen 10 mg twice weekly and once weekly, respectively. Comparing the placebo-corrected PS volumes are compared, there seems to be less reduction of PS volume at week 24 in the highest tertile (&gt;70 kg). Nevertheless, a clear decrease relative to baseline is still observed. For the once-weekly regimen, the impact of body weight seems smaller than for

twice-weekly dosing, and variability between weight groups is largely overlapping.

## Clinical pharmacokinetics

The recommended dose of glepaglutide is 10 mg administered twice weekly, whereas the characterisation of the PK in healthy volunteers has been evaluated under a once weekly dosing regimen (study 16182). Therefore, the results of the phase 3 studies (EASE-1 and EASE-2) in the target population was leading for the PK assessment. The PK in patients with SBS was based on samples taken during the phase 3 studies (EASE-1 and EASE-2). As expected, the PK profiles of once- and twice-weekly dosing were similar until the second dose of the twice-weekly regimen was administered. The higher AUC in the twice-weekly cohort is also as expected. It is however not understood why the mean Cmax is lower with twice-weekly dosing. Even though there is a substantial variability between subjects, the mean is expected to represent a higher Cmax when increasing the dosing frequency. Especially when looking at the accumulation ratio Cmax of 1.19 calculated from study 16182. The Cthrough at steady-state was 2-fold higher after twice- vs. once-weekly dosing. For OW dosing, steady-state AUC0-168h was considered sufficiently comparable between SBS patients and healthy subjects (286035 vs. 232000, ratio: 0.81). The Cmax after once-weekly dosing is also similar between SBS patients and healthy subjects (5770.7 vs. 5940, ratio: 0.97). The apparent discrepancy that the clinical measurements for M2 exposure do not match with the predicted exposure for ADA-positive subjects is explained by differences in the definition of Ctrough sampling windows between OW and TW dosing regimens (OW: &gt;60 to &lt;198 h; TW: &gt;60 to &lt;114 h), which impact direct comparability of individual trough measurements. When exposure is appropriately assessed using AUC(τ), the observed exposure in patients following OW dosing is 23% lower, which is in close agreement with the model-predicted 25% reduction in ADA-positive subjects. This supports the consistency between the clinical data and the model predictions. Based on SBS patient measurements and population PK modelling, glepaglutide Ctrough was approximately 75% and 30%, respectively, higher following injection into the abdomen compared to the thigh, even after adjusting for body weight. The claim that steady-state was reached after 2-3 weeks of treatment with glepaglutide is based on the results from study 16182 in which a once-weekly dosing regimen was applied and additional simulated glepaglutide concentration-time profiles for TW dosing. It is ageed that the observed concentrations in SBS patients fall within the same concentration range as the simulated concentration-time profiles in healthy subjects, thereby supporting the appropriateness of bridging PK data between healthy individuals in study 16182 and SBS patients. It should however be noted that the amount of variability is extremely high (CV &gt; 100%) for almost all metrics from the EASE studies, this is expected, considering the limited sample size of six subjects per dosing regimen included in the submitted dossier. Furthermore, glepaglutide exposures in terms of Cavg, Cmax (model-based only) and Ctrough were higher following injection into the abdomen compared to the thigh, even after adjusting for body weight. However, the SmPC states that glepaglutide can be injected into either the abdomen or thigh, without mentioning any differences in exposure. Due to the high variability in PK and the limited sample size, no definitive conclusions can currently be drawn regarding injection-site related differences in exposure within the EASE-2 sub-study, which included more intensive pharmacokinetic sampling. As the current data indicate that Ctrough levels, both measured and modelled, increase following abdominal injection by approximately 75% and 30%, respectively, compared to thigh injection, allowing patients to choose either injection site. Overall, there is currently limited pharmacokinetic (PK) data of glepaglutide, i.e. parent drug and both metabolites, in patients. The available data is primarily based on Cthrough measurements, which exhibit high variability in both dosing regimens (311% and 159%). This variability remains unexplained. Furthermore, the richer sampling dataset from the extension EASE-2 trial, to date includes only six subjects, which significantly limits the reliability of the provided data. As a result, comprehensive pharmacokinetic profiles for the proposed posology have not yet been established. Given the substantial limitations of the PK dataset and the resulting uncertainty regarding identification of responders to the proposed twice-weekly dosing regimen, a stopping rule is introduced in section 4.2 of the SmPC. If no overall improvement is achieved after these 6 months, the need for continued treatment should be reconsidered. it should be considered to switch to an alternative treatment or discontinue GLP-2 analogue treatment as a whole.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Clinical studies have shown that following SC injection, two main glepaglutide metabolites (M1 and M2) together with a smaller proportion of unmetabolized parent drug enter the systemic circulation, with the protracted release of M1 and M2 from the SC depot being the rate limiting step that governs overall plasma exposure and effective half-life. Metabolism continues in the circulation where M2 is the predominant metabolite at steady state. However, after IV administration, the parent drug is stated to be the main circulating compound, which suggesting that the majority of the metabolism occurs in a SC compartment.

## Dose proportionality and accumulation

The metrics from trial 16182 suggest that the relationship is not strictly dose-proportional between 10 mg and 5 mg: since the geometric least square mean percentage ratios for AUC0-t comparing 10 mg vs. 5 mg showing values of 267% on day 1 and 221% on day 36. The other analysed metrics (AUC0-inf, AUC0-last, and Cmax) show a similar relation with ratios between 213% and 277% (all with p-values &lt;0.005).

The justification for excluding trial 08216 data from dose proportionality assessment is not accepted, as its limitations still do not necessarily preclude its use. Although multiple injections at higher dose levels (48 and 96 mg) introduce a confounding factor, excluding these specific data points is reasonable, but not the entire dataset. An integrated approach, combining data from trials 08216 and 16182 with modeling exercises, was considered appropriate and showed dose proportionality for glepaglutide single doses up to 24 mg and within the proposed dose range of 5 to 10 mg at steady state, which was updated in the SmPC section 5.2.

Accumulation in healthy subjects (n=14) with glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly was determined to be 1.37 (CV% = 53.3) and 1.19 (CV% = 50.3) for AUC0-τ and Cmax, respectively. Furthermore, additional data presented by the applicant demonstrate that neither the dosing frequency (onceweekly vs. twice-weekly administration) nor the development of anti-drug antibodies (ADAs) has a significant impact on the accumulation of glepaglutide.

## Intra- and inter-individual variability

For healthy volunteers, intra-subject variability (CV%) for AUC0-t and Cmax was 28.2% and 25.5%, respectively. The corresponding values for inter-subject variability (CV%) were 36.4% and 39.7%, respectively. In the population PK analysis, intersubject variability (%CV) was estimated on the clearances of M1 and M2 and were 97.2% and 42.2%, respectively.

## Target population

In EASE-2 study a full PK-profile at steady state after 10 mg TW and OW dosing of a limited number (n=11) of patients was obtained. Steady state PK-parameters, AUC0-168h and Cmax, were determined by NCA. The applicant has addressed the discrepancy in the sum of parent and metabolites (M1 + M2) in the initial dossier, attributing it to the use of geometric mean, which distorts relative percentage values due to its non-linear nature. Although normalization to 100% at the population level would have been more accurate, the relative contributions of the parent and metabolites remain similar regardless of the calculation method. This matter is considered resolved.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Therapeutic window

The therapeutic window was not defined, and thus, an addition to the SmPC section 5.2 was made that higher exposures have not been studied, in order to reflect the remaining uncertainties regarding the therapeutic window and exposure margins.

## Special populations

A dedicated PK study in renally impaired patients was conducted in trial 18131. AUC0-166hr and Cmax of parent glepaglutide was increased by a factor of approximately 2 in patients with severe renal impairment/ESRD compared to subjects with normal renal function, whereas exposure of the metabolites was comparable. Therefore, a overall slight decreases in both Cmax and AUC0-168h of total glepaglutise (parent + M1 + M2) were observed in patients with severe renal impairment/ESRD, with estimated ratios of 0.897 and 0.963, respectively. The apparent higher renal clearance of parent vs metabolites was attributed to their differences in overall charge. The differences on the pharmacokinetics in severe renal impaired patients are not considered clinically relevant (SmPC 5.2).

PK variation of glepaglutide in special population was evaluated across studies by population PK and by linear regression analysis using observed and modelled exposure parameters in SBS patients from EASE 1/EASE-2 studies. As one PK-samples were taken at each nominal visit in EASE trial (apparent Ctrough samples), the Caverage value represents the mean value of all Ctrough values taken at steady state (from week 4 and onwards).

After body weight correction, there was no notable effect of age, renal impairment, or hepatic impairment on glepaglutide exposure in patients with SBS. No conclusions could be made for race and ethnicity due to the limited number of patients in subgroup categories other than White. There was a trend for higher glepaglutide exposure in female patients with SBS, which remained after adjustment for body weight at baseline. However, in healthy subjects there was no clear impact of sex on glepaglutide exposure (AUC0-t and Cmax) following adjustment for body weight at baseline. As the studies in healthy volunteers were the only studies that included rich PK data (except for 6 patients in phase 3 studies), the effect of sex on the glepaglutide PK was evaluated based on these data and was considered not clinically meaningful.

Glepaglutide exposure (AUC0-t and Cmax) decreases with increasing body weight in both healthy volunteers and SBS patients, based on clinical data and the population PK analysis. This effect is as expected. Nevertheless, no clinically relevant effect of body weight on the exposure-efficacy relationship is expected.

27 out of the 344 subjects from all trials were aged &gt;65 years. Age was not identified as a covariate in the analysis and no dose adjustments were proposed based on renal function, which is acceptable.

## Interaction studies

In vitro studies were performed to study the potential for glepaglutide to induce or inhibit CYP enzymes, and the potential to inhibit drug transporters. M2 was determined to not be a reversible inhibitor of the cytochrome P450 isoforms CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6 and CYP3A4 human liver microsomes. M2 was not considered to be an inducer of CYP1A2, CYP2B6 or CYP3A4 in any of the donors tested. Although IC50 values were used instead of Ki, the use of IC50 is considered acceptable in this case, as the values are approximately 10-fold higher than the corresponding Ki values, indicating sufficient separation to support the conclusions drawn.

In the report of study 19-014, it is only stated that there is no inhibition at IC50 (μM) for all included transporters. Although the summary table of observed transporter inhibition in Study 19-014 only reports IC₅₀ values (μM) without detailed acceptance criteria or a justification for using IC50 over Kᵢ, the observed lack of inhibition across all tested transporters is considered sufficiently supported by the available data. Given that no inhibition was observed at relevant concentrations and that the study design appears to follow standard methodological approaches, no further information or clarification is required.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

No drug-drug interaction was evaluated in vivo, which is acceptable. The lack of DDI is appropriately

reported in the SmPC.

## Pharmacodynamics

GLP-2 Is a specific, endogenous intestinal growth factor that plays a key role in enhancing intestinal mucosal morphology, function, and integrity under normal and pathophysiological conditions. Consistent with the mechanism of action of GLP-2, glepaglutide is postulated to improve intestinal absorptive capacity as measured by reductions in fecal wet weight output and increases in urine output and the absorption of wet weight/fluids, energy, and electrolytes. The mechanism of action of glepaglutide has been explained appropriately.

The pharmacodynamic effects of glepaglutide for SBS patients with intestinal insufficiency or failure who -if necessary- received a stable parenteral support volume have been evaluated in the doubleblind randomized Phase 2 study 15073 (n= 18) and the open-label, single-arm Phase 3 study 20060 (EASE-4) (n= 11). In study 15073, included study patients received two of the three evaluated dosage regimens of glepaglutide (0.1, 1.0, or 10.0 mg/day) in consecutive 3-week treatment periods with a 4-week washout period in between the treatment periods. In study 20060, all patients were treated with glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly for 52 weeks. The study treatment compliance in both studies was nearly 100%.

For the primary pharmacology endpoints were chosen that are deemed meaningful for studying intestinal absorption.

## Adjustments of the glepaglutide dosages, oral liquids and parenteral support

In study 15073, the glepaglutide doses could be reduced upon occurrence of moderate to severe adverse events that were treatment-related according to the investigator. Although a constant intake of oral liquids and parenteral support was attempted, respective intakes could be adjusted at the discretion of the investigator to avoid oedema.

In study 20060, study patients were on a fixed drinking menu until week 24. Thereafter, study patients drank an amount that reflected their preferred or habitual fluid intake. During the study, parenteral support volume and content were regulated according to changes in urine output, weight change and clinical symptoms of fluid overload. If the daily urine volume of the current visit was at least 10% higher than baseline urine volume the new weekly parenteral support volume was the current weekly parenteral support volume minus seven times the absolute increase in daily urine volume.

Below, the results for the primary and secondary pharmacodynamic endpoints of both studies are

presented and discussed.

Change in wet weight of ostomy output or diarrhoea. In study 15073, the mean baseline balance wet weight output (g/day) of the 0.1 (n=10), 1 (n= 11), and 10 mg/day (n=11) glepaglutide treatment arms were respectively 2601, 3431, and 3385 g/day. The covariate adjusted mean absolute changes in wet weight output for the 0.1, 1, and 10 mg/day glepaglutide treatment arms were respectively +173.0 (95% CI -160.3; 506.2; p= 0.27), -592.4 (95% CI -912.5; -272.4; p&lt; 0.01), and -833.3 (95% CI -1152.2; -514.5; p&lt; 0.01) g/day. Hence, a positive dose-effect relationship was observed. Similar results were obtained if only data obtained in treatment period 1 were taken into consideration (sensitivity analysis 1), or when the second treatment period would be excluded if study patients had received the 10 mg/day glepaglutide dosage in treatment period 1

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

(sensitivity analysis 2). Overall, the observed effects for the 10 mg/day and 1 mg/day glepaglutide dosages were comparable (difference -240.9 (95% CI -699.4; 217.6; p= 0.27)). For the relative change in wet weight of ostomy output or diarrhoea similar trends were observed in the different treatment groups as for the primary endpoint. Absolute and relative change of urine weight. In study 15073, the mean baseline urine weight (g/day) of the 0.1 (n=10), 1 (n= 11), and 10 mg/day (n=11) glepaglutide treatment arms were respectively 1482, 1675, and 1227 g/day. The covariate adjusted mean absolute changes in urine weight for the 0.1, 1, and 10 mg/day glepaglutide treatment arms were respectively +90.4 (95% CI - 208.1; 388.8; p= 0.52), +530.1 (95% CI 244.5; 815.7; p&lt; 0.01), and +367.9 (95% CI 82.2; 653.5; p= 0.02) g/day. Similar trends were observed for the7.2.2. relative change of urine weight in the different treatment groups. Absolute and relative change in wet weight absorption (i.e. oral intake minus faecal/stoma output). In study 15073, the mean baseline balance wet weight absorption (g/day) of the 0.1 (n=10), 1 (n= 11), and 10 mg/day (n=11) glepaglutide treatment arms were respectively 524.7, 537.9, and 287.8 g/day. The covariate adjusted mean absolute changes in wet weight absorption for the 0.1, 1, and 10 mg/day glepaglutide treatment arms were respectively -211.4 (95% CI -487.1; 64.3; p= 0.12), +650.0 (95% CI 385.4; 914.5; p&lt; 0.01), and +785.8 (95% CI 523.0; 1048.6; p&lt; 0.01) g/day. Small relative changes in wet weight absorption were observed (p&gt; 0.05 for all treatment arms). In study 20060, the mean change from baseline to week 24 in the absorption of wet weight/fluids increased from 841.6 g/day at baseline to 1240 g/day at week 24 in the study patients (n= 10) who were treated with glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly. This increase of 398.4 g/day was not statistically significant (p=0.06). However, upon adjustment of the baseline parenteral support volume the results became statistically significant (LS means 398.4 (95% CI 29.0; 767.8); p= 0.04). These data indicate that changes in wet weight absorption upon glepaglutide treatment are influenced by the baseline parenteral support volume. Absolute and relative change in urine weight minus oral intake. In study 15073, the mean baseline balance urine weight minus oral intake (g/day) of the 0.1 (n=10), 1 (n= 11), and 10 mg/day (n=11) glepaglutide treatment arms were respectively -1644, -2294, and -2447 g/day. The covariate adjusted mean baseline balance urine weight minus oral intake for the 0.1, 1, and 10 mg/day glepaglutide treatment arms were respectively +117.6 (95% CI -244.3; 479.4; p= 0.49), +486.7 (95% CI 140.5; 833.0; p= 0.01), and +411.5 (95% CI 65.1; 757.8; p= 0.02) g/day. Similar trends were observed for the relative change of urine weight minus oral intake in the different treatment groups. Absolute and relative change in intestinal absorption of electrolytes. In study 15073, increases in intestinal absorption of some electrolytes (sodium and potassium) were observed during treatment with 1 mg and 10 mg glepaglutide per day, while no changes were found for other electrolytes (calcium and magnesium). In study 20600 (n= 10), the mean absorption of sodium increased from baseline to week 24 by 28 mmol/day, potassium by 1.9 mmol/day, and calcium by 1.3 mmol/day, whereas the absorption of magnesium decreased by a mean of 2.2 mmol/day. Energy and macronutrients. In study 15073, the observed mean absolute changes in intestinal energy absorption as measured by bomb calorimetry were 391.0 (1374.9) kJ/day in the 1 mg group,

and 643.9 kJ/day in the 10 mg group. The calculated intestinal energy absorption increased in the combined 1 mg and 10 mg dose group (covariate adjusted mean 822 kJ/day; p=0.01) and in the 1 mg dose group (1099 kJ/day; p=0.02).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In study 15073, overall increases in intestinal absorption of macronutrients (protein, carbohydrates, lipids) were observed in the 1 mg and/or 10 mg dose groups. No additional benefit was seen in macronutrient absorption when increasing the glepaglutide dose from 1 mg/day to 10 mg/day.

In study 20600 (n= 10), the mean absorption of energy increased from 7350.1 kJ/day at baseline to 8387.8 kJ/day at week 24 of 10 mg glepaglutide once weekly, an increase of 1037.7 kJ/day (nominal p= 0.02).

In study 20600 (n= 10), the mean absorption of carbohydrates increased from baseline to week 24 by 39.8 g/day, lipids by 10.5 g/day, and proteins by 1.0 g/day. Overall, 4 patients had concomitant increases in the absorption of carbohydrates, lipids, and proteins from baseline to week 24.

Change in weekly parenteral support volume. In study 20600 (n= 10), the mean weekly parenteral support volume decreased from 17.8 l at baseline to 13.1 l at week 12 and 12.5 l at week 24, corresponding to mean decreases from baseline of 4.7 l at week 12 and 5.3 l at week 24. In line with this finding, the amounts of parenteral support macronutrients and electrolytes also decreased.

Citrulline levels. In study 15073, the mean baseline citrulline levels (µmol/l), a biomarker of enterocyte mass, of the 0.1 (n=10), 1 (n= 11), and 10 mg/day (n=11) glepaglutide treatment arms were respectively 43.9, 46.5, and 42.6 g/day. The covariate adjusted mean absolute change in plasma citrulline levels for the 0.1, 1, and 10 mg/day glepaglutide treatment arms were respectively - 1.4 (95% CI -9.3; 6.5; p= 0.69), +15.3 (95% CI 7.7; 22.9; p&lt; 0.01), and +15.6 (95% CI 8.2; 23.0; p&lt; 0.01) g/day. Hence, the increase in enterocyte mass tended to be larger for higher glepaglutide dosages.

Quality of life. In study 15073, small improvements in general quality of life as assessed by SF-36 were observed in the 0.1 mg and 1 mg dose groups, while a slight impairment in general quality of life was observed in the 10 mg dose group.

Based on explorative visual analogue scale on appetite and thirst, the patients' appetite was unchanged in the 0.1 mg group and decreased in the 1 mg and 10 mg groups from baseline to the end of the 3-week treatment periods. Thirst decreased from baseline to the end of the 3-week treatment periods in all dose groups.

For most of the 17 items of the SBS quality of life there was a small improvement in health-related quality of life in the 0.1 mg and 1 mg groups from baseline to the end of the 3-week treatment period (up to -2.2 points) and an impairment in health-related quality of life in the 10 mg group (up to +2.3 points).

It is unknown why improvements in general quality of life as assessed by SF-36 and the SBS quality of life scale tended to be worse in study 15073 for the study patients who were treated with glepaglutide 10 mg/day as compared to those who were treated with glepaglutide 0.1 and 1 mg/day. However, since the evaluated glepaglutide dosages in study 15073 have not been further evaluated in the conducted confirmative studies, this issue is closed.

Although the evaluated glepaglutide posologies in study 15073 (0.1, 1, and 10 mg/day) differ from the evaluated and recommended glepaglutide posologies in the confirmative studies (10 mg once or twice weekly), the glepaglutide exposure was similar upon administration of the same glepaglutide dose with formulation strengths of 10 mg/ml (Phase 1 PK study (study 16182) and 20 mg/ml (all Phase 3 studies among other clinical studies) that were used during the clinical development of glepaglutide (see pharmacokinetic section above). At a similar glepaglutide exposure, the pharmacodynamic effects will then also be similar.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

QT/QTc prolongation. Exposure-response analyses of data from single ascending subcutaneous doses of glepaglutide up to 96 mg demonstrated that glepaglutide will not cause clinically concerning QT effects at plasma concentrations within the studied range. The upper end of this range clearly exceeds concentration levels that can be expected in patients receiving a dose of 10 mg twiceweekly. Based on PK analyses but also non-clinical studies (see above) it is therefore unlikely that glepaglutide induces QT prolongation of ≥10 ms at the recommended posology of 10 mg twice weekly.

In summary, glepaglutide was shown to decrease the wet weight of ostomy output or diarrhoea, the parenteral support volume and to increase the urine weight, the wet weight absorption, the change in urine weight minus oral intake, absorption of energy, macronutrients and electrolytes, and citrulline levels. In study 15073, a positive dose-effect relationship was observed for the primary and many secondary endpoints. These findings support the pharmacodynamic action of glepaglutide in SBS patients.

In theory, potential adjustments in oral intake and parenteral support may affect the observed pharmacodynamic effects of glepaglutide. The integrity of the study results of study 20060 (n= 10) and conclusions are ensured from the fixed-dose study design (i.e. glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly), and the limited number of adjustments to the drinking menu volume during the first 24 weeks of study treatment (up to 3 patients at a time point). After week 24, (almost) all included study patients (9 or 10 out of 10 patients) had ≥2 of such drinking volume (ml) adjustments. Despite these adjustments, a reduction in mean weekly parenteral support volume (from -5.4 l at week 12 to -6.5 l at week 52) was observed. This supports the pharmacodynamic effects of glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly.

## Pharmacokinetics-Pharmacodynamics (PK/PD)

The presented data support evaluation of the glepaglutide dosages of 10 mg once weekly and twice weekly in the Phase 3 studies. Higher AUC0-168h seems to be associated with greater reductions in parenteral support volume, however, this effect is not significant and based on a small number of patients. Therefore, these results should be interpreted with caution, and no robust conclusions can be drawn based on these data. The appropriateness of the proposed glepaglutide posology is further addressed with respect to the evaluated clinical effects of glepaglutide (see Ch. 5.3 Clinical Efficacy).

## 5.2.7.2. Conclusions

The clinical pharmacology of glepaglutide has been investigated in clinical studies and supported by modelling and simulation studies. The pharmacokinetic (PK) data of glepaglutide in patients is limited, and the available data is primarily based on Cthrough measurements, which exhibit high variability thatremains partially unexplained. Even though the available PK data is limited and should therefore be interpreted with caution, the totality of the data in the clinical pharmacology package is considered sufficient to support the characterisation of the PK.

The submitted pharmacodynamic studies support the pharmacodynamic effects of glepaglutide at a similar glepaglutide exposure as in the conducted clinical studies.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Figure 15: Overview of patient trajectories in the EASE-1, EASE-2, and EASE-3 studies

## 5.3.1. Dose response studies

The pharmacodynamic effects of different glepaglutide dosages (0.1, 1 and 10 mg/day) have been evaluated in SBS-II and SBS-IF patients in the Phase 2 proof-of-concept dose-finding study ZP1848-15073 (see section 6.2.3.2.1. on pharmacodynamics).

In addition, the clinical effects of glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly and glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly in SBS-IF patients have been evaluated in the pivotal efficacy study EASE-1 and the supportive EASE-2, and EASE-3 studies (see below).

The justification for the dose-selection for the Phase 3 studies is discussed in section 5.2.5.1.

## 5.3.2. Main studies

## 5.3.2.1. Study ZP1848-17111 (EASE-1)

## 5.3.2.1.1. Study title

A Phase 3, international, multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial to evaluate the efficacy and safety of glepaglutide in patients with short bowel syndrome (SBS).

## 5.3.2.1.2. Study design

The EASE-1 study (study ZP1848-17111) was designed to demonstrate the superiority of once weekly and twice weekly subcutaneous injections of 10 mg glepaglutide versus placebo in stable SBS patients.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Subsequent to an initial optimization and stabilization phase, patients were randomized 1:1:1 to 24 weeks of treatment to either placebo or 10 mg glepaglutide once- or twice weekly. Efficacy assessment included the need for parenteral support as determined from regularly conducted 48-hour measurement periods.

Figure 16: Design EASE-1 study

<!-- image -->

## Treatment

Study patients were randomized to either:

- Glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly
- Glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly
- Placebo

Glepaglutide and placebo were delivered as ready-to-use solutions in vials, from which the study patients were to withdraw 0.5 ml using single-use syringes and inject it subcutaneously (SC). Dosing on visit days was done at the study site following lab sampling and assessments. The initial dose of the investigational product was administered by a member of the study staff at the first visit (day 1), during which the patient was instructed on the preparation, administration, storage, and return of investigational product.

## Injection site

Study patients had to choose their preferred injection site area (either abdomen or thigh), which was to be used during the entire trial unless otherwise agreed with the investigator. The precise location of injection within the injection site was to be rotated for each injection, such that each injection was administered at least 5 cm away from where the previous injection was administered; for thigh injection, it was acceptable to inject in both thighs.

## Parenteral support

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The included SBS-IF patients in the EASE-1 study received concomitant parenteral support. After inclusion, the patients entered a run-in phase consisting of parenteral support optimization and stabilization phases.

During the optimization phase, the investigator could change the parenteral support volume and content according to institutional standard practice if the patient was considered unstable or not optimized, while aiming at a urine volume of 1-2.5 l per day.

A stabilization phase of 2 to 4 weeks of duration immediately followed the optimization phase (the last optimization phase visit could serve as the first stabilization phase visit). No changes in the prescribed weekly parenteral support volume or schedule were allowed during this phase.

During the subsequent 24-week treatment phase of the pivotal EASE-1 study, parenteral support requirements were evaluated through the use of 48-hour balance periods scheduled to occur as close as possible to and within 7 days of the respective site visits at weeks 1, 2, 4, 8, 12, 16, 20 and 24 following treatment initiation. The balance periods involved a fixed drinking menu (individually predefined during the optimization phase) and measurements of urine volume, based on which the parenteral support volume was to be adjusted according to the following predefined algorithm:

- IF: daily urine volume of the current visit is at least 10% higher than baseline urine volume.
- THEN: New parenteral support volume (weekly) = Current parenteral support volume (weekly) - 7 x absolute increase in daily urine volume from baseline.

Actual parenteral support use was recorded by the patient in the eDiary on an ongoing basis, whereas the type, content, and volume of parenteral support being prescribed were recorded by the investigator in the eCRF.

## Randomisation

Study patients were randomized 1: 1: 1 via an interactive web response system.

## Blinding

All double-blind treatment arms involved twice-weekly dosing (glepaglutide and/or placebo) to maintain the blind of study patients and study personnel. The second weekly dose of each week was to be administered either 3 days or 4 days after the first weekly dose, with the interval chosen at the randomization visit and adhered to throughout the study period.

## Patient population

The EASE-1 study was conducted in (number of sites that randomized patients in parenthesis) Belgium (1), Canada (3), Denmark (2), France (2), Germany (5), the Netherlands (1), Poland (3), the United States of America (7), and the United Kingdom (5).

Main inclusion criteria

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

- Age ≥ 18 years and ≤ 90 years at screening.
- Diagnosis of SBS defined as remaining small bowel in continuity of estimated less than 200 cm and with the latest intestinal resection being at least 6 months prior to screening and considered stable with regard to parenteral support need. No restorative surgery planned in the study period.
- Requiring parenteral support at least 3 days per week.
- Willing to adhere to an individual pre-defined drinking menu during 48-hours measuring intervals.
- Willing to maintain a stable weight (± 5%) for the duration of the study (24 weeks).

Main exclusion criteria

- More than 2 SBS-related or parenteral support-related hospitalizations (e.g., catheter related bacteremia/sepsis, bowel obstruction, severe water-electrolytes disturbances, etc.) within 6 months prior to screening.*
- Poorly controlled inflammatory bowel disease (IBD) that is moderately or severely active or fistula interfering with measurements or examinations required in the study.**
- Bowel obstruction.**
- Known radiation enteritis or significant villous atrophy, e.g., due to active coeliac disease.
- Any history of colon cancer. History of any other cancers (except margin-free resected cutaneous basal or squamous cell carcinoma or adequately treated in situ cervical cancer) unless disease-free state for at least 5 years.
- Use of GLP-1, GLP-2, human growth hormone (HGH), somatostatin, or analogs thereof, within 3 months prior to screening.**
- Use of dipeptidyl peptidase (DPP)-4 inhibitors within 3 months prior to screening.**
- Systemic immunosuppressive therapy that has been introduced or has been unstable within 3 months prior to screening.**
- Unstable biological therapy (e.g., anti-TNF-α, natalizumab, etc.) within 6 months prior to screening, including significant changes in doses or switch of drug.**
- Known or suspected hypersensitivity to glepaglutide or related products.
- Previous exposure to glepaglutide.

* Additional randomization criterion that there were no SBS-related hospitalizations (other than any related to trial procedures) within 30 days prior to randomization.

** Non-qualification on criterion during the period between screening and randomization was included as a separate randomization criterion.

## 5.3.2.1.3. Objectives, endpoints and estimands

## Primary objective and endpoint

Treatment with glepaglutide is aimed to reduce and -if possible- stop the parenteral support.

The primary efficacy endpoint in EASE-1 is the change in actual weekly parenteral support volume from baseline to week 24.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

|-------------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Treatment conditions                            | Assignment to glepaglutide 10 mg once or twice weekly, regardless of discontinuation, compared to assignment to placebo, regardless of discontinuation. |
| Endpoint (variable)                             | Change in actual weekly parenteral support volume from baseline to week 24                                                                              |
| Population-level summary                        | Difference in least square means (95% CI)                                                                                                               |
| Intercurrent events and strategy to handle them | Intercurrent events and strategy to handle them                                                                                                         |
| Intercurrent event in primary estimand          | Treatment discontinuation (treatment policy)                                                                                                            |
| Intercurrent event in supplementary estimand    | Treatment discontinuation (Hypothetical)                                                                                                                |

## Statistical methods for estimation and sensitivity analysis on primary estimand

## Derivation of parenteral support endpoints

The eDiary was set-up to capture events of parenteral support use when they occur, and processes were in place to ensure the validity of the data. The algorithm was consequently modified in SAP version 5.0 to consider days without parenteral support entry in the eDiary as corresponding to parenteral support use of 0. Further, the following rules were implemented to ensure a conservative assessment of parenteral support use:

For each visit, the actual weekly parenteral support volume will be derived by using a valid 7-day period closest to the visit were

1. the actual weekly parenteral support volume accounted for &gt;= 90% of prescribed weekly parenteral support volume

or

2. there is at least one actual parenteral support volume accounted for by the patient in the eDiary for each day in the valid 7-day period

If neither of the two conditions above is justified, the weekly parenteral support use should be considered missing.

## Primary endpoint

The primary endpoint analysis of EASE-1 applied a restricted maximum likelihood (REML) based repeated-measures approach to compare treatment groups with respect to the mean change from baseline in weekly parenteral support volume at week 24. The model used weekly parenteral support volume assessments at weeks 1, 2, 4, 8, 12, 16, 20, and 24 (derived as the weekly parenteral support volume during a valid 7-day period) as an independent variable. Treatment group, baseline weekly parenteral support volume, visit (categorical variable), stratification factor (weekly parenteral support

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

volume requirements &lt;12 l/week versus ≥12 l/week), and visit-by-treatment group interaction were included as covariates. Variance estimation was based on an unstructured covariance matrix, which did not presume a particular correlation structure for repeated weekly PS volume measurements within patients over time. The primary comparisons were the contrasts (differences in least squares means) between the glepaglutide treatment groups and the placebo group at the week 24 visit in this mixedeffects model for repeated measures (MMRM).

## Sensitivity analyses primary endpoint

Missing values were imputed using multiple imputation methods. For the primary analysis, a Copy Reference (CR) approach was used (main estimator), while for sensitivity analyses, a Jump to Reference (J2R) approach (sensitivity estimator 1) and a Copy Incremental from Reference (CIR) approach (sensitivity estimator 2) were applied.

The Copy Reference approach is considered justified by the applicant as the trophic effects on the intestines mediated by glepaglutide would take weeks to abate after treatment withdrawal. A longitudinal growth of the remaining intestines could also have occurred after prolonged therapy, and such modifications would not be expected to fully return to the baseline condition. Other effects of GLP-2 agonism like reduced fluid secretion from the upper part of the gastro-intestinal tract and effects on reducing motility would, however, be expected to return to the baseline conditions when treatment is withdrawn.

As an additional sensitivity analysis (sensitivity estimator 3) for the primary endpoint, the weekly parenteral support volume was derived using a valid 14-day period prior to the visit.

Furthermore, a tipping point sensitivity analysis (sensitivity estimator 4) was conducted to examine the impact of missing data on the primary efficacy endpoint analysis. The aim of this analysis was to explore the plausibility of missing data assumptions under which the conclusions change. The CRimputed values were varied independently in each treatment arm by adding an appropriate delta parenteral support volume. The range was determined such that it contained the tipping point, i.e., where conclusions start changing. Only clinically relevant scenarios were included. The primary analysis model was otherwise retained.

Lastly, a sensitivity analysis on the primary efficacy endpoint analysis (sensitivity estimator 5) was conducted to examine the impact of using multiple imputation methods on missing data. This sensitivity analysis applied the primary analysis model on observed data only, regardless of treatment discontinuation.

For the remaining key secondary (responder) endpoints, missing values were imputed as no response, with multiple imputation applied as sensitivity analysis.

## Supportive analysis of primary endpoint derivation

The primary endpoint, actual weekly PS volume, will be derived as specified above. The robustness of the primary analysis, with regard to the algorithm, will be explored through several supportive analyses, which each will address the impact of key algorithm elements. The analyses are considered 'supportive' as patients with missing PS entries continue in the trial, and no relevant intercurrent events are expected to trigger the missing entries. Supportive analyses will be conducted according to the following table.

## Secondary objectives

Key secondary endpoints:

- Clinical response, defined as achieving at least 20% reduction in weekly parenteral support volume from baseline to both weeks 20 and 24.
- Reduction in days on parenteral support ≥ 1 day per week from baseline to week 24.
- Change in weekly parenteral support volume from baseline to week 12.
- Reduction in weekly parenteral support volume of 100% (weaned off) at week 24.

Secondary endpoints:

- Change of at least 20% in actual weekly parenteral support volume from baseline to both weeks 12 and 24
- Change in calculated energy content of parenteral macronutrients from baseline to week 24
- Change in number of days on actual parenteral support per week from baseline to week 24
- Reduction of at least 40% in actual weekly parenteral support volume from baseline to both weeks 20 and 24
- Reduction of at least 30% in actual weekly parenteral support volume from baseline to both weeks 20 and 24
- Reduction in prescribed weekly parenteral support volume of 100% (weaned off) at week 24

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

- PGIC improvement at weeks 4, 12, 20, and 24
- Change in weight from baseline to week 24
- Prescribed weekly parenteral support volume

## Other efficacy endpoints:

- Reduction in days on parenteral support ≥ 2 days per week from baseline to week 24.
- Reduction in days on parenteral support ≥ 3 days per week from baseline to week 24.
- Change in duration of parenteral support infusions per week from baseline.
- Concentration through levels of glepaglutide and metabolites.
- Change in plasma citrulline levels from baseline to week 24.
- Change in weekly need for parenteral micronutrients (sodium, potassium, magnesium, and calcium) from baseline to week 24.
- Change in patient-reported outcomes (SBS-I and EQ-5D-5L) from baseline to week 24.
- Change in bowel movements or stoma bag emptying from baseline to week 24.
- Measurements of hydrational status.

## Estimands for the secondary objectives

The table below demonstrates the primary and supplementary estimands for the binary key secondary objectives of

- Clinical response, defined as achieving at least 20% reduction in weekly parenteral support volume from baseline to both weeks 20 and 24.
- Reduction in days on parenteral support ≥ 1 day per week from baseline to week 24.
- Reduction in weekly parenteral support volume of 100% (weaned off) at week 24.

For the primary estimand for the secondary objectives, a composite strategy was used, where the outcomes for patients who discontinued treatment or had missing values for any other reasons were imputed as failures.

For the supplementary estimands for the secondary objectives, a treatment policy strategy was used, where response status is derived using the same multiple imputation model as for primary analysis of primary efficacy endpoint.

For the key secondary outcome \"change in weekly parenteral support volume from baseline to week 12.\", estimands are defined as the ones for the primary outcome, thus treatment policy for the primary estimand and hypothetical for the supplementary.

## Statistical methods for estimation and sensitivity analysis on the secondary estimands

Secondary endpoints were analysed similarly to the key secondary endpoints of the study, i.e., responder endpoints were analysed using the Cochran-Mantel-Haenszel test adjusted for stratification factor, and continuous endpoints were analysed using an MMRM, with multiple imputation for parenteral support-related endpoints. Change in body weight was summarized descriptively.

For secondary endpoints defined as 'other efficacy endpoints', the responder endpoints of reduction in weekly days on parenteral support were analysed similarly to the corresponding key secondary endpoint, and continuous endpoints were analysed using an MMRM. The remaining 'other efficacy endpoints' were summarized descriptively.

A total of 154 patients were screened, and 141 patients were enrolled in the run-in period. 35 Subjects were enrolled but not randomised due to noncompliance with in-/exclusion criteria (n=13), failure to meet parenteral support stability criteria during the parenteral support stabilisation phase leading up to randomisation (n=8), or other, less frequent reasons (n=14) (e.g. patient decision, non-compliance with the protocol).

106 Study patients were randomized: 35 in each of the glepaglutide groups and 36 in the placebo group.

Of the 35 patients in the glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly group, 31 (88.6%) completed the 24 weeks of treatment. In the glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly group, 34 out of 35 patients (97.1%) were treatment completers as were all patients (100%) in the placebo group.

A total of 5 patients discontinued treatment during the study:

- 4 patients (11.4%) in the glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly group (2 were due to adverse events and 2 were due to patient decision). These patients withdrew from the study and did not attend the week 24 visit, but 2 patients attended a follow-up visit instead.
- 1 patient (2.9%) in the glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly group (due to adverse events). This patient remained in the study and attended the week 24 visit.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Deviations from study plan

For the pivotal study EASE-1, the primary outcome was changed at version 4 of the protocol, dated October 2018 (primary outcome of 20% reduction in weekly PS volume was moved to key secondary and primary of Change from Baseline in weekly PS volume at week 24 was changed from key secondary to primary outcome). The rationale for these changes is described as advised by the FDA.

SAP v3/4 (unknown date) had a different algorithm for calculating weekly PS volume actually received, were missing daily PS volumes were imputed with the average of the available days. The SAP v5 Algorithm is unknown. SAP v6 (dated August 2022) mentions that from v5 to v6 \"Algorithm Updated\".

## Baseline data

## Demographics and baseline characteristics

Demographics and standard baseline characteristics for patients of EASE-1 are summarized by treatment group in Table 24.

For the total population (N=106), mean age (SD) at time of consent was 55.0 (12.4) years. Patients in the glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly group had a mean age (56.9 years) which tended to be higher than in the glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly and placebo groups (both: 54.0 years).

At baseline, 74.5% of the patients had a BMI in the normal range (18.5-25.0 kg/m 2 ), and only 2 patients (1.9%) were underweight (BMI &lt;18.5 kg/m 2 ). Mean BMI (SD) was 23.3 (3.4) kg/m 2 .

## SBS characteristics and disease history

In accordance with the selection criteria for this trial, all patients were Type III according to the ESPEN functional classification of chronic intestinal failure, i.e., metabolically stable patients with a chronic condition requiring intravenous supplementation. Table 25 summarizes the key baseline SBS characteristics.

The EASE-1 patient population consisted of a broad range of SBS patients, with either colon-in-continuity (SBS anatomical group 2 and 3) or small intestinal stomas (group 1). The most common underlying cause for SBS was Crohn's disease, followed by surgical complications and mesenteric vascular disease.

## Baseline parenteral support characteristics

Baseline parenteral support characteristics are presented in Table 19. The mean weekly parenteral support volume requirement at baseline was 14.4 l/week, with no noteworthy differences between the treatment groups. However, there was a tendency towards a higher median parenteral support requirement in the placebo group (14.7 l/week) compared to the glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly and once weekly groups (12.2 and 12.5 l/week, respectively).

In line with the mean weekly parenteral support volume requirement at baseline, slightly more patients had a parenteral support requirement ≥12 l/week (56.6%) compared to patients requiring &lt;12 l/week (43.4%) based on the stratification factor.

At baseline, patients on average received parenteral support approximately 6 days/week.

## Outcomes and estimation

Unless otherwise specifically stated, all the following result presentations related to parenteral support pertain to 1) parenteral support volumes as recorded in the patient eDiary (i.e., actually administered volumes), and 2) the full analysis set.

## Primary endpoint: change in weekly parenteral support volume from baseline to week 24

The parenteral support volume generally decreased over time in all three treatment groups, with a dose-response relationship of increasing parenteral support volume reduction with increasing glepaglutide dose.

Results of the primary statistical analysis are shown in Table 20 and Figure 21. Superiority relative to placebo with respect to the primary endpoint of change in weekly parenteral support volume from baseline to week 24 was shown for glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly (estimated difference of -2.28 l/week (95% CI -3.83;-0.73); p=0.0039) but not for glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly (estimated difference of -0.91 l/week (95% CI -2.52; 0.71); p=0.27).

## Sensitivity analyses for the primary estimand

An overview of the result of the primary analysis of the primary endpoint and results of associated sensitivity analyses is provided in Table 21 and Figure 22. The conclusion of superiority for glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly relative to placebo with respect to the primary endpoint was supported by the applied sensitivity analyses. Specifically for sensitivity analysis 4, it was found that imputation of an additional 3.9 l/week (i.e., in addition to the copy-reference imputed value) was required for the 4 patients with permanent treatment discontinuation in the glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly group in order to tip the primary endpoint analysis to non-superiority.

Figure 18: Parenteral support volume (l/week) change from baseline to week 24 - treatment policy estimand - forest plot of analysis results - EASE-1

Approach 1: The algorithm-driven PS volume adjustment pertaining to any given visit is calculated based on the actual urine volume (as reported by the patient in the eDiary) and prescribed PS volume (as reported by the investigator in the eCRF) assigned to the specific visit (in addition to the baseline urine volume). While this is consistent with the data feeding into the PS algorithm, the impact of any previous hypothetical changes to the PS prescription (which would impact both the urine volume and the PS volume feeding into the PS algorithm calculation at subsequent visits) is ignored. Zealand Pharma considers that this assumption poses limitations with respect to the interpretability of the analysis.

Approach 2: At all post-baseline visits, the hypothetically derived PS volume from the previous visit is used as input to the PS adjustment algorithm at the following visit. This ignores that the urine output pertaining to a given visit is substantially impacted by the actual PS volume, again imposing limitations with respect to the interpretability of the analysis.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

When applying approach 1, no substantial difference is indicated when comparing hypothetically prescribed PS volumes and prescribed PS volumes, with the median volume at Week 24 being slightly higher for glepaglutide TW, similar for glepaglutide OW, and slightly lower for placebo (Table 22). When considering mean values, the mean hypothetical prescribed PS volume was lower than the mean prescribed volume across all three treatment arms at Week 24, albeit with a greater mean (SD) difference between hypothetically prescribed and prescribed PS volumes for placebo (-0.68 [1.55] L/week) than for active treatment (glepaglutide TW: -0.16 [1.10] L/week; glepaglutide OW: -0.20 [0.92] L/week). Similar results were seen when comparing actually taken PS volumes (as reported by the patient in eDiary) and hypothetical prescribed PS volumes.

Approach 1: algorithm applied per visit, using recorded urine volume and investigator prescribed PS at a given visit to derive the following hypothetical prescribed PS volume according to the algorithm.

Statistical outputs parallel to those presented above are available for approach 2, in which the hypothetically derived PS volume from the previous visit is used as input to the PS adjustment algorithm at the following visit; see Table 23. Of note, the deviations between the hypothetical prescribed PS volume and the prescribed and actually taken PS volume are of similar magnitude for the placebo and glepaglutide OW arms but slightly larger for the glepaglutide TW arm.

Table 30 shows the results obtained by using the 'approach 2'-derived, hypothetical prescribed PS volume as response variable in the primary analysis. The glepaglutide treatment effect is increased when compared to the original primary endpoint analysis, with an estimated difference for glepaglutide TW relative to placebo of -3.29 L/week [-4.87; -1.70]95% CI for approach 2 versus -2.28 L/week [-3.83; -0.73]95% CI for the original primary analysis.

## Missing Data

To clarify, the exact weekday at which a patient is prescribed a given type of PS bag is in principle not known, which has implications for the determination of sufficiently justified days with no registered PS volume ('0-days'). For example, if a patient is prescribed bag A 3 times weekly and bag B 3 times weekly, the actual administration of the prescribed bags can theoretically be distributed across anywhere from 3 to 6 days per week. Therefore, in this example (Figure 23, Exemplification of applied concept for identifying insufficiently justified 0-days), the patient could theoretically have anywhere from 1 to 4 days without PS (0-days) per week. Under the assumption of no overlap of prescription days ('worst-case'), any number of 0-days beyond 1 per week would in this example be considered insufficiently justified. On the other hand, under the assumption that the prescription days overlap as much as possible ('best-case'), any number of 0-days beyond 4 per week would be considered insufficiently justified. Of note, during periods where the patient is confirmed as being weaned off PS, all 0-days are considered sufficiently justified.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

<!-- image -->

0-days

Figure 19: Exemplification of applied concept for identifying insufficiently justified 0-days

An overview of insufficiently justified 0-days (worst-case assumption) and all 0-days is presented in Table 31. Using the worst-case assumptions for identifying insufficiently justified 0-days, the 0-days that do not fall in this category (i.e., the remaining 0-days) are considered as sufficiently justified. Results show that even under worst-case assumptions the proportion of 0-days that may be considered as insufficiently justified is relatively low, which supports the robustness of the EASE-1 trial conclusions.

## Post-hoc data adjustment

The proportion of data points entered by DCRs was approximately 2%, which is considered as expected for the clinical trial setting. There were no consistent differences across visits and treatment groups with respect to the proportion of data points entered by DCRs.

As seen from the by-patient figures Figure 20, Figure 21 and Figure 22, most of the data collected were diary entries (marked with green circles), and only few were manual DCR entries (yellow triangles). The vast majority of data used for derivation of the primary endpoint at Week 24 were based on data as reported by the patient (green circles).

## Supplementary estimand analyses

In addition, a supplementary estimand was included using a hypothetical strategy. Results were similar to those obtained for the primary (treatment policy) estimand.

## Supportive analysis of primary endpoint derivation

The potential impact of any days with no parenteral support eDiary entry was investigated in a prespecified tipping point supportive analysis. The analysis evaluated the sensitivity of the statistical results towards imputation of parenteral support use on days with no parenteral support eDiary entry, ranging from imputation of 0 (corresponding to tipping factor 0.0 in Figure 23) up until imputing the daily average of days with parenteral support use (corresponding to tipping factor 1.0 in Figure 23).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

<!-- image -->

Abbreviations: TW: twice weekly; OW: once weekly; N: number of patients in Full Analysis Set; CI: confidence interval; CIR: copy increment from reference; CR: copy-reference; J2R: jump to reference; MI: multiple imputation; PS: parenteral support. Notes: MMRM model includes treatment group, visit, stratification factor, treatment-by-visit interaction as factors and Baseline PS volume (L/Week) as covariate. Variance estimation is based on an unstructured covariance matrix within treatment group. Stratification factor: weekly PS volume requirements &lt;12 L/week and &gt;=12 L/week. Delta: Minimum amount added to CR-imputed values for Glepa 10 mg groups in order to get insignificant p- value for the comparison with placebo (nothing is added/subtracted).

Cross-reference: EASE-1, Figure 14.2.1.30

Figure 23: Parenteral support volume (l/week) change from baseline to week 24 - forest plot of supportive analysis results - EASE-1

Of importance for the comparison of glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly vs placebo in Figure 23, the shift of point estimate and confidence interval in the direction of non-significance when going from tipping factor 0.0 to tipping factor 0.1 (and correspondingly at higher tipping factors) is due to the fact that patients who are completely weaned off parenteral support are considered missing and hence treated according to a copy reference approach in the statistical analysis. When including the weaned off patients with 0 litre parenteral support volume at week 24 in the tipping point analyses (Figure 24), the decrease in parenteral support volume with glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly is consistently significantly greater than with placebo, regardless of the degree of imputation (p=0.0151 with tipping point factor=1.0).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

<!-- image -->

Abbreviations: TW: twice weekly; OW: once weekly; CI: confidence interval; CR: copy-reference; MI: multiple imputation; N: number of patients in Full Analysis Set; PS: parenteral support.

Notes: Variance estimation is based on an unstructured covariance matrix. MMRM model includes treatment group, visit, stratification factor, treatment-by-visit interaction as factors and Baseline PS volume (L/Week) as covariate. Stratification factor: weekly PS volume requirements &lt;12 L/week and &gt;=12 L/week. Tipping point: For days with missing PS volume, the average of the non-missing days times a tip factor from 0.1 to 1.0 is used. Weaned off patients: Visits where no PS is taken (PS volume set to 0) are included with PS volume = 0 (i. e. nothing is added for tip-factors 0.1 - 1.0).

Cross-reference: Appendix 2, Figure 4.1.5

Figure 24: Parenteral support volume (l/week) change from baseline to week 24 - forest plot of tipping point analyses was weaned off patients are included (MI CR) - EASE-1

Further supporting the robustness of the primary endpoint conclusions, the results for the primary endpoint were not noticeably affected when:

- 1) disregarding parenteral support volume data decisions based on notes-to-file and supportive analysis 2 in EASE-1

or

- 2) when using a 95% threshold (as opposed to the pre-defined 90% threshold) for the comparison between actual and prescribed parenteral support volumes.

In contrast, no treatment effect was demonstrated for supportive analysis 1. In this analysis, parenteral support volume is imputed as the volume average of days with recorded parenteral support use. Weekly parenteral support volumes based on 3 or less days with parenteral support entry per week were considered missing and hence imputed with a placebo response.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Key secondary endpoints

Clinical response, defined as achieving at least 20% reduction in weekly parenteral support volume from baseline to both weeks 20 and 24, was included as the 1st key secondary endpoint in the parallel gatekeeping testing procedure (testing hierarchy) applied for EASE-1. The clinical relevance to patients of the ≥20% reduction threshold is supported by an anchor-based analysis assessing the association between Patient Global Impression of Change (PGIC) data (ordinal data) and percentage change in parenteral support volume from baseline to weeks 12 and 24.

The percentage of patients achieving clinical response in EASE-1 is shown by visit in Figure 25.

<!-- image -->

Abbreviations: TW: twice weekly; OW: once weekly; N: number of patients in Full Analysis Set; PS: parenteral support.

Cross-reference: EASE-1, Figure 14.2.2.2

Figure 25: Percentage of patients achieving at least 20% reduction in parenteral support volume (l/week) from baseline by visit - categorical summary plot - EASE-1

The main statistical analysis of the defined clinical response endpoint (i.e., achieving at least 20% reduction from baseline at both weeks 20 and 24) is shown in Table 25. Superiority relative to placebo was shown for glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly (estimated difference of 26.6% (95% CI 4.3; 48.9); p=0.02) but not for glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly (estimated difference of 5.5% (95% CI 16.2; 27.1; p=0.63).

## Reduction in weekly parenteral support volume from baseline to week 12

The reduction in weekly parenteral support volume from baseline to week 12 align with those of the primary endpoint, indicating an early onset of treatment effect of glepaglutide. Of note, the nominal p-value of 0.0019 for the main analysis comparing glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly vs placebo did not lead to a formal conclusion of superiority of glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly on this endpoint according to the pre-defined statistical testing hierarchy.

## Reduction in weekly parenteral support volume of 100% (weaned off) at week 24

Weaning off from parenteral support was achieved at the end of the treatment period (week 24) for 5 (14.3%) patients receiving glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly and for 4 (11.4%) patients receiving glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly. In contrast, no patients of the placebo group were weaned off at week 24.

The baseline parenteral support volume requirement in the patients who achieved enteral autonomy was on average 6.8 l/week, ranging from 3 to 14 l/week. Of the 9 patients who achieved enteral autonomy, 2 patients had a stoma.

Results of the main statistical analysis are shown in Table 34. The nominal p-values (p=0.02 for glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly vs placebo; and p=0.04 for glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly vs placebo) did not lead to a formal conclusion of superiority for either of the glepaglutide treatment arms relative to placebo according to the pre-defined statistical testing hierarchy.

Results were similar to those for the corresponding key secondary endpoint focusing on reduction from baseline to both weeks 20 and 24, but without reaching statistical significance.

## Change in calculated energy content of parenteral macronutrients from baseline to week 24

Although the median change from baseline to week 24 was numerically greater with glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly (-7361 kJ/week) and glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly (-3086 kJ/week) than with placebo (-734 kJ/week), the data variability was substantial, and no statistically significant differences were identified.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Results similar to the above were obtained when performing the analysis based on data derived using a valid 14-day period prior to the visit. Furthermore, conclusions were similar when normalizing energy content by body weight (kjoule/kg).

## Change in number of days on parenteral support per week from baseline to week 24

For the secondary endpoint of change in number of days on parenteral support per week from baseline to week 24, a statistically significant difference relative to placebo was shown for glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly (estimated difference of -0.85 days/week (95% CI -1.42; -0.27); p=0.004), whereas the comparison for glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly relative to placebo did not reach statistical significance (estimated difference of -0.80 days/week (95% CI -1.62; 0.02); p=0.06).

## Patient Global Impression of Change (PGIC) improvement at weeks 4, 12, 20, and 24

## Change in weight from baseline to week 24

Mean body weight overall remained similar from baseline to week 24 in all three treatment groups (Table 17). The mean percentage change in body weight from baseline to week 24 was -0.27% for glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly, 0.41% for glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly, and -0.46% for placebo. There were no specific trends across treatment groups with respect to shifts between BMI groups during the treatment period of the study.

There was no apparent correlation between change in weekly parenteral support volume from baseline to nominal visit week 24 versus change in body weight from baseline to nominal visit week 24 when considering EASE-1 and EASE-2 combined overall and by BMI subgroup.

## Prescribed weekly parenteral support volume

Statistical analysis results for prescribed weekly parenteral support volume for glepaglutide as compared to placebo are summarized in Table 38. Results were similar to those of the primary endpoint of change in actual parenteral support volume, showing a difference relative to placebo of approximately -2 l/week and -1 l/week for the glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly and once weekly groups, respectively, which was statistically significant for the comparison of glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly versus placebo.
