## Other efficacy endpoints of EASE-1

## Reduction in days on parenteral support ≥ 2 and ≥ 3 days per week from baseline to week 24

Results of EASE-1 for these endpoints overall align with the observed reduction in parenteral support requirement with glepaglutide treatment, showing greater proportions of responders for glepaglutide than for placebo.

The endpoint of reduction in days on parenteral support ≥2 days/week from baseline to week 24 was achieved for 34.3% of patients receiving glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly, for 22.9% of patients receiving glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly, and for 13.9% of patients receiving placebo.

The endpoint of reduction in days on parenteral support ≥ 3 days/week from baseline to week 24 was achieved for 22.9% of patients receiving glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly, for 22.9% of patients receiving glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly, and for 2.8% of patients receiving placebo. No statistical analyses were performed.

## Change in duration of parenteral support infusions per week from baseline

The most notable reduction in duration of parenteral support infusion was observed for patients receiving glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly, who achieved a mean (SD) change from baseline to week

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

24 of -24.4 (23.3) hours/week compared to -10.9 (23.5) hours/week for glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly and -9.4 (16.1) hours/week for placebo. No statistical analysis was performed.

## Change in plasma citrulline levels from baseline to week 24

Mean citrulline concentration (a biomarker for small bowel enterocyte mass (Crenn et al. 2000)) tended to increase in both glepaglutide treatment groups but not for placebo, with the greatest increase observed for the glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly group. With glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly dosing, the increase from baseline was significantly greater vs. placebo (p=0.0025). The dosedependent increase in citrulline levels provides clinical evidence that glepaglutide is acting via the GLP-2 receptor to increase enterocyte mass, supporting the expected intestinotrophic effect of glepaglutide on the intestinal epithelium.

## Change in weekly need for parenteral micronutrients (sodium, potassium, magnesium, and calcium) from baseline to week 24

A minor trend towards greater reductions from baseline to week 24 with glepaglutide relative to placebo was observed, but results should be interpreted with caution due to substantial data variability.

## Change in patient-reported outcomes (SBS-I and EQ-5D-5L) from baseline to week 24

Trends towards a treatment benefit of glepaglutide were noted for SBS-I for pain, exhausted/tired, sleep and affected mood, whereas no noteworthy differences between treatment groups were noted for EQ-5D-5L.

## Change in bowel movements or stoma bag emptying from baseline to week 24

A decreased number of bowel movements and stoma bag emptyings from baseline to week 24 were observed for all treatment groups but most prominently for the glepaglutide treatment groups.

## Exploratory bone markers

No difference between active treatment and placebo was shown for change from baseline to week 24 for the EASE-1 exploratory bone markers serum C-terminal telopeptide of type I-collagen (P-CTx) and plasma procollagen type I N-propeptide (P-PIND).

## Pre-defined and post-hoc subgroup analyses

## Analysis of the impact of COVID-19 on the parenteral support endpoints

The influence of COVID-19 on the EASE-1 primary endpoint result was evaluated by repeating the primary endpoint analysis (carried out in the same way as for the other subgroup analyses in EASE-1) for subgroups of patients defined by having ≥2 remote visits (nall subgroups= 17) in EASE-1 versus by having &lt;2 remote visits (nall subgroups= 89). In study patients with &lt;2 remote visits, similar trends were observed as in the primary analysis. However, in study patients with ≥2 remote visits, comparable results were obtained in all subgroups.

## Comparison of results in sub-populations

Potential subgroup effects were evaluated for the primary endpoint of EASE-1. A forest plot of results of subgroup analyses is provided in Figure 27.

For the three subgroup parameters of race, ethnicity, and underlying cause of SBS, the numbers of patients within individual subgroup categories were insufficient to allow for statistical analysis.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

A subgroup analysis investigating the effect of region (Europe vs North America) was conducted. No notable regional subgroup effect was found.

## Injection into abdomen or thigh

For glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly the change in weekly parenteral support volume from baseline at week 24 tended to be larger in the abdomen (n= 59) as compared to the thigh (n= 47)(LS means -5.4 (95% CI -6.7; -4.1) vs. LS means -4.7 (95% CI -6.5; -2.8)) (Figure 29). A similar trend was observed for glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly (LS means -4.8 (95% CI -6.6; -3.0) vs. LS means -2.9 (95% CI - 4.5; -1.2)). Only upon injection of either glepaglutide dosage into the abdomen the observed differences to placebo treatment reached statistical significance at week 24. Upon post-hoc exclusion of 3 outliers in the thigh placebo group, the change in parenteral support volume (l/week) was larger for glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly as compared to placebo twice weekly at week 24. This was not observed for the comparison between glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly and placebo twice weekly for this endpoint.

In the active dose groups, all 9 patients who weaned off parenteral support (5 with glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly and 4 with glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly) injected in the abdomen.

## Ancillary analyses

## Analyses including EASE-2 data

Data from the first 6 months of glepaglutide treatment in study EASE-2 for those patients who were randomized to placebo in study EASE-1 (and for the additional patients who were recruited directly into EASE-2) were added in a pre-specified analysis. The aim of this analysis was to use all available data to estimate the treatment effect at week 24. The analysis was done on data pooled by nominal visit, i.e., with weeks corresponding to the time since initiation of glepaglutide treatment, irrespective of whether this occurred in EASE-1 or EASE-2.

The results are shown in Figure 33. Relative to the EASE-1 primary endpoint analysis, this analysis includes approximately 75% more patients exposed to active treatment, with 61 and 62 patients exposed to glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly and once weekly, respectively. A statistically significant difference was shown for glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly versus placebo (estimated difference of 2.29 (95% CI -3.62;-0.96); p=0.001). The analysis also showed a statistically significant difference for glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly versus placebo (estimated difference of -2.00 (95% CI -3.34;-0.67); p=0.0039).

## 5.3.2.2. EASE-5 study

Another Phase 3 study (EASE-5) on glepaglutide for SBS is ongoing (start 11 th of Fabruary 2026) on request of the Food and Drug Administration (FDA) in the United States of America.

The EASE-5 study includes a one-year, double-blind, placebo-controlled part (with a primary endpoint of change in weekly parenteral support volume from baseline to week 24, i.e., identical to EASE-1). This is followed by an open-label part, during which all study patients will receive glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly for up to 3 years, depending on the commercial availability of glepaglutide. An overview of the design of EASE-5 is provided in Figure 31.

<!-- image -->

* Stratified by patient's weekly PS volume requirements (&lt;12 L/week versus ≥12 L/week).

Figure 31: Study design EASE-5 study

In the EASE-5 study, patients will be required to confirm that no parenteral support was infused on days without recorded parenteral support.

## 5.3.5. Supportive studies

## 5.3.5.1. Study ZP1848-17127 (EASE-2)

The study design of study ZP1848-17127 (EASE-2) is presented below.

<!-- image -->

Visits

Figure 32: Design EASE-2 study

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Study population

Study patients in the EASE-2 study concerned study patients who had completed the EASE-1 study (n= 106), and additional study patients (n= 18) who complied with the same inclusion and exclusion criteria as in the EASE-1 study. The proportion of stoma patients tended to be higher among patients in the EASE-1 study compared to study patients who were directly randomized into the EASE-2 study (54.7 vs. 27.8% of patients).

## Randomisation

Study patients receiving once weekly or twice weekly glepaglutide in EASE-1 continued their blinded treatment with the same dosing regimen. Patients receiving placebo in the lead-in study and patients being screened and randomized directly to this study were evenly randomized via an interactive web response system to blinded treatment with 10 mg glepaglutide either once weekly or twice weekly.

Maintaining the block randomization strategy from EASE-1 for glepaglutide, re-randomization of patients receiving placebo in EASE-1 as well as randomization of patients screened directly into the EASE-2 study were performed using a block randomization scheme stratified by the patients' weekly parenteral support volume requirements (&lt; 12 l per week versus ≥ 12 l/week), as evaluated prior to visit 1 of the EASE-2 study.

## Blinding

EASE-2 was a double-blind study.

## Study treatment

Study patients receiving once or twice weekly glepaglutide in EASE-1 continued their blinded treatment with the same dosing regimen. Patients receiving placebo in the lead-in study and patients being screened and randomized directly to this study were evenly randomized to blinded treatment with 10 mg glepaglutide either once weekly or twice weekly.

The treatment groups of EASE-2 were therefore as follows:

- Glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly
- Glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly

All treatment arms involved twice-weekly dosing (glepaglutide and/or placebo) to maintain the blind. The second weekly dose of each week was to be administered either 3 days or 4 days after the first weekly dose, with the interval chosen at the randomization visit and adhered to throughout the trial period.

Glepaglutide and placebo were delivered as ready-to-use solutions in vials, from which the study patients were to withdraw 0.5 ml using single-use syringes and inject it subcutaneously (SC). Dosing on visit days was done at the trial site following lab sampling and assessments.

Choice of the injection site was identical to the EASE-1 protocol.

## Concomitant treatment

Concomitant treatment with GLP-1, GLP-2 (teduglutide), human growth hormone (HGH), DPP-4 inhibitors or somatostatins was not allowed. Other than that, medications commonly used to treat SBS symptoms, including anti-diarrhoea agents, were allowed during the study; however, changes were to be kept to a minimum.

## Parenteral support

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The methodology for assessment and adjustment of parenteral support during the EASE-2 study, which included evaluation of parenteral support requirements every 3 months, was similar to that in the EASE-1 study. However, in EASE-2 the drinking menu could be changed during the treatment phase if warranted based on clinical signs of increased intestinal absorption. An initial parenteral support optimization and stabilization phase was furthermore included for patients recruited directly into EASE-2.

As additional measures of the treatment effect on parenteral support requirements, the calculated energy content of parenterally supplied macronutrients and the amounts of parenterally supplied micronutrients (calculated separately for sodium, potassium, magnesium, and calcium) were included as secondary efficacy outcome measures.

## Endpoints

Primary endpoint: change in weekly parenteral support volume from baseline to month 6

Key secondary endpoints:

- Change in weekly parenteral support volume from baseline.
- Reduction of at least 20% in weekly parenteral support volume from baseline (clinical response).
- Reduction in days on parenteral support ≥1 day per week from baseline.
- Reduction in weekly parenteral support volume of 100% (weaned off).

The endpoints were analysed at 6, 12, and 24 months.

Secondary endpoints:

- Reduction in days on parenteral support ≥ 2 days per week from baseline to week 24.
- Reduction in days on parenteral support ≥ 3 days per week from baseline to week 24.
- Change in duration of parenteral support infusions per week from baseline.
- Concentration trough levels of glepaglutide and metabolites.
- Change in plasma citrulline levels from baseline to week 24.
- Change in weekly need for parenteral micronutrients (sodium, potassium, magnesium, and calcium) from baseline to week 24.
- Change in patient-reported outcomes (SBS-I and EQ-5D-5L) from baseline to week 24.
- Change in bowel movements or stoma bag emptying from baseline to week 24.
- Measurements of hydrational status.

## Statistical analysis

For efficacy endpoints, baseline was defined as baseline in EASE-1 for patients randomized in EASE-1 and baseline in EASE-2 for patients screened and randomized directly into EASE-2.

The key efficacy parameters corresponded to those of in the EASE-1 study, but with the aim to evaluate the efficacy of glepaglutide during extended exposure. For EASE-2, differences between treatment groups were analyzed using similar methods as for the corresponding endpoints of EASE-1 (using a mixed effects model for repeated measures [MMRM] for non-responder endpoints and the Cochran-Mantel-Haenszel test for responder endpoints) but using observed data prior to any permanent treatment discontinuation (hypothetical strategy). A two-sided type I error rate of 0.05 was applied for inferential tests, with no adjustments for multiplicity.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The secondary endpoints and 'other efficacy endpoints' were summarized descriptively or - where statistical analysis was applied - analysed using similar methods as for the corresponding endpoints of the EASE-1 study, but without applying multiple imputation methods.

A two-sided type I error rate of 0.05 was applied for inferential tests, with no adjustments for multiplicity.

## Results:

## Efficacy

The baseline for patients of EASE-2 was identical to that of the EASE-1 lead-in study. Accordingly, the overall baseline characteristics of the EASE-2 study population were similar to those of EASE-1.

The following efficacy results pertain to data collected until the interim cut-off date of 01 January 2024 for the EASE-2 extension trial. Of the 114 patients enrolled and randomized in EASE-2 at the time of the interim cut-off (Table 30), 18 had been randomized directly into the trial, and 96 were rolled over from EASE-1. Of the 96 patients rolled over from EASE-1, 27 had been randomized to glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly, and 34 to glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly in EASE-1 and continued this treatment in EASE-2. The remaining 35 patients, who had been randomized to placebo in EASE-1, were rerandomized 1:1 to glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly (17 patients) and glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly (18 patients) in EASE-2.

## A total of 23 patients discontinued treatment:

- 11 patients discontinued treatment due to adverse events: 5 (9.4%) in the glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly group and 6 (9.8%) in the glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly group. Two adverse events had a fatal outcome: 1 serious adverse event of cardiopulmonary failure for which the primary reason for withdrawal was registered as 'Adverse event', and 1 serious adverse event of multiple organ dysfunction syndrome for which the primary reason for withdrawal was registered as 'Death'. Both fatal serious adverse events occurred in the glepaglutide once weekly group, and both were evaluated to be not related to glepaglutide.
- 8 patients discontinued treatment due to patient decision, and 3 for reasons marked as 'other' (2 of these were patients' decision, and 1 was due to surgery

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

- patient in the glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly group (twice weekly-twice weekly sequence) discontinued treatment with the reason 'lack of efficacy'. The patient withdrew from the trial.

Treatment duration as of the cut-off date varied between patients. At the time of the interim cut-off, 62 patients, all rolled over from EASE-1, had completed EASE-2. The remaining patients rolling over from EASE-1 had been treated for at least 6 months in EASE-2. A total of 4 patients did not continue in the EASE-3 extension trial and therefore attended the follow-up visit in EASE-2.

## Key efficacy endpoint

- At the month 6 visit in EASE-2, the estimated change from baseline in weekly parenteral support volume was -4.69 l/week in the glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly group and -5.94 l/week in the glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly group. There was no statistically significant treatment difference (glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly - glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly): 1.25 l/week (95% CI -0.45; 2.95), p=0.15.

## Other key efficacy endpoints

- The reduction in mean parenteral support volume seen at month 6 was sustained at month 12 and month 24. No statistically significant treatment differences (glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly - glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly) were seen in change from baseline in weekly parenteral support volume to month 12 and month 24. For patients treated with placebo in EASE-1, an additional reduction in mean weekly parenteral support volume was observed during glepaglutide exposure in the EASE-2 extension study compared to the reduction seen in EASE-1.
- The proportion of patients achieving a reduction of at least 20% in parenteral support volume (l/week) from baseline to month 6 (clinical response) was numerically greater with glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly (74.5%) compared to glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly (67.9%), with no statistically significant treatment difference (glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly - glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly).
- The proportions of patients with a reduction in days on parenteral support by ≥ 1 day per week increased during the study, both with glepaglutide 10 mg twice and once weekly. The proportion of patients who had achieved a reduction of at least 1 day/week on parenteral support at month 6 was numerically greater in the glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly group (61.7%) than in the glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly group (45.3%), with an estimated treatment difference (glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly - glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly) of 20.3 %-points (95% CI 2.5; 38.1); p=0.03).
- A 100% reduction in parenteral support volume (weaned off) at month 6 was achieved by 7 patients in the glepaglutide TW group (14.9% of patients with observed responder status) and 11 patients in the glepaglutide OW group (20.8%), with no statistically significant treatment difference (glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly - glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly). At the interim cut-off, a total of 24 patients were weaned off parenteral support (glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly: 9, glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly: 15).

## Secondary efficacy endpoints

- At month 6, the median change from baseline in parenteral macronutrient energy content was - 8230.6 kJ/week in the glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly group and -10272.8 kJ/week in the glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly group.
- The estimated mean change from baseline at month 6 in days on parenteral support was -1.26 days/week in the glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly group and -1.84 days/week in the glepaglutide

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

10 mg once weekly group, with no statistically significant treatment difference (glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly - glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly).

- The proportion of patients achieving at least 30% reduction in weekly parenteral support volume at month 6 was 61.7% in the glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly group and 56.6% in the glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly group, with no statistically significant treatment difference (glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly - glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly).
- The proportion of patients achieving at least 40% reduction in weekly parenteral support volume at month 6 was 48.9% in the glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly group and 49.1% in the glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly group, with no statistically significant treatment difference (glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly - glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly).
- In line with the reduction in actual weekly parenteral support volume, the change from baseline in prescribed parenteral support volume (l/week) obtained during EASE-1 was sustained in both treatment groups during the EASE-2 extension trial. At month 6, the observed change from baseline in mean prescribed parenteral support volume was -5.4 l/week in the glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly group and -4.9 l/week in the glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly group.
- For prescribed parenteral support volume, a 100% reduction (weaned off) at month 6 was achieved by 7 patients in the glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly group (14.6% of patients with observed responder status) and 11 patients in the glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly group (20.4%), with no statistically significant treatment difference (glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly - glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly).
- Mean body weight remained essentially unchanged during the trial. At month 6, the observed change from baseline in mean body weight was -1.31 kg in the glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly group and 0.51 kg in the glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly group.

## Other efficacy endpoints

- The proportion of patients achieving a reduction in days on parenteral support ≥2 days/week was 42.6% (20 patients) in the glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly group and 35.8% (19 patients) in the glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly group at month 6.
- The proportion of patients achieving a reduction in days on parenteral support ≥3 days/week was 23.4% (11 patients) in the glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly group and 32.1% (17 patients) in the glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly group at month 6.
- The change from baseline in mean duration (hours/week) of parenteral support infusion was numerically greater with glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly (-24.4 hours/week) compared to glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly (18.9 hours/week) at month 6.
- The mean change from baseline in plasma citrulline, a biomarker of enterocyte mass, was numerically greater with glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly (11.5 µmol/l) compared to glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly (7.0 µmol/l) at month 6.
- No apparent differences in change from baseline in the weekly need for parenteral micronutrients were observed between the glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly and glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly groups at month 6.
- No clinically relevant changes from baseline were observed for SBS-Impact Scale (SBS-I) scores or European Quality of Life-5 Dimension 5 Level (EQ-5D-5L) scores at month 6.
- The reduction in number of bowel movements or stoma bag emptyings achieved during EASE-1 was sustained in the EASE-2 extension study.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

- Supporting the validity of the obtained results for change from baseline in parenteral support volume, no major excursions in mean oral fluid intake and urine production were observed up to month 6, indicating appropriate adherence to the predefined algorithm for parenteral support volume adjustment.

## 5.3.5.2. Study ZP1848-20110 (EASE-3)

<!-- image -->

a: if a deterioration in parenteral support needs was observed, the dosing regimen of glepaglutide could be increased to 10 mg twice weekly, at the investigator's discretion.

Figure 33: Design EASE-3 study

## Study population

Study patients who had completed the EASE-2 study.

## Study treatment

Study patients received open-label glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly, which could be increased to 10 mg twice weekly in individual patients as judged by the investigator due to increased parenteral support needs. For this study, glepaglutide was administered via the to-be-marketed auto injector.

Choice of the injection site was identical to the EASE-1 protocol.

## Randomisation and blinding

Not applicable.

## Concomitant treatment

Concomitant treatment with GLP-1, GLP-2 (teduglutide), human growth hormone (HGH), DPP-4 inhibitors or somatostatins was not allowed. Other than that, medications commonly used to treat SBS symptoms, including anti-diarrhoea agents, were allowed during the study; however, changes were to be kept to a minimum.

## Parenteral support

Changes to the volume and content of parenteral support during the 104-week treatment phase were at the discretion of the investigator based on institutional practice. Details of prescribed parenteral support were recorded on an ongoing basis in the eCRF, and unscheduled trial visits could be arranged to assess parenteral support volume/content needs. Intake of enteral liquid/food was managed according to clinical practice and at the investigator's discretion, and no patient diary/electronic diary was used.

As additional measures of the treatment effect on parenteral support requirements, the calculated energy content of parenterally supplied macronutrients and the amounts of parenterally supplied

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

micronutrients (calculated separately for sodium, potassium, magnesium, and calcium) were included as secondary efficacy outcome measures.

## Endpoints

Primary endpoint: not applicable

Secondary endpoints:

- Change in weekly prescribed parenteral support volume from baseline.
- Reduction of at least 20% in weekly prescribed parenteral support volume from baseline (clinical response).
- Reduction in days on parenteral support ≥1 day per week from baseline.
- Reduction in weekly prescribed parenteral support volume of 100% (weaned off).

Other efficacy endpoints:

- Reduction in calculated energy content of parenteral macronutrients from baseline.
- Reduction in number of days on parenteral support per week from baseline.
- Reduction of at least 40% in weekly parenteral support volume (prescribed) from baseline.
- Reduction in days on parenteral support ≥ 2 days/week from baseline.
- Reduction in days on parenteral support ≥ 3 days/week from baseline.
- Change in weekly need for parenteral micronutrients (sodium, potassium, magnesium, and calcium) from baseline.
- Change in weight from baseline.

Statistical analysis

For efficacy endpoints baseline was defined as baseline in EASE-1 for patients randomized in EASE-1 and baseline in EASE-2 for patients screened and randomized directly into EASE-2.

The endpoints of the EASE-3 study were summarized descriptively.

No adjustment for multiplicity was done.

## Results:

## Efficacy

The following efficacy results pertain to data collected until the interim cut-off of 01 January 2024. All 57 patients enrolled at this time point had completed both the EASE-1 lead-in study and the EASE-2 extension study before entering EASE-3.

Efficacy conclusions describe results at month 6, where 81% of patients (46 of 57) had reached the month 6 visit and hence had a prescribed parenteral support value observation. Results pertain to all enrolled patients with an observation at month 6, regardless of previous treatment in EASE-2 (glepaglutide 10 mg once or twice weekly). Change from baseline refers to the baseline of EASE-1.

## Key efficacy endpoints

- At month 6 of EASE-3, the mean change from baseline in prescribed parenteral support volume was -7.6 l/week. Overall, the prescribed parenteral support volume remained stable during the EASE-3 extension study.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

- A total of 93.5% of patients had a reduction of at least 20% in prescribed parenteral support volume at month 6.
- A total of 12 patients (26.1%) were weaned off parenteral support (had a 100% reduction in prescribed parenteral support volume) in month 6. In total, 25 patients were weaned off parenteral support at the time of the interim cutoff.

## Other efficacy endpoints

- A total of 82.6% of patients had a reduction of at least 30% in prescribed parenteral support volume at month 6.
- A total of 69.6% of patients had a reduction of at least 40% in prescribed parenteral support volume at month 6.
- Other endpoints
- Overall, mean body weight remained stable during the study. The mean change from baseline in body weight at month 6 was -1.8 kg.
- Mean plasma citrulline remained stable during the trial, with a change from baseline at month 6 of 8.7 µmol/l.

## 5.3.6. Analysis performed across studies (pooled analyses and metaanalysis)

## Persistence of efficacy and/or tolerance effects

## Other supportive analyses

Persistence of the glepaglutide treatment effect was investigated via an evaluation of change from baseline in weekly parenteral support volume during glepaglutide treatment. Study results were pooled longitudinally, thereby obtaining the trajectory of the treatment effect over the extended glepaglutide treatment period. To account for study effect, baseline was as determined in EASE-1, also for patients randomized to placebo in EASE-1 and hence not initiating glepaglutide treatment until at entry into EASE-2. The parenteral support volume change from baseline was calculated from start of glepaglutide treatment by pooled nominal visit. Corresponding presentations were made for the absolute parenteral support volume over time. EASE-3 Did not collect eDiary data and hence did not contribute to the above longitudinal presentations of actual parenteral support volumes.

Similar presentations as described above were made for prescribed parenteral support volume over time, which included data from EASE-3 comprising a single treatment arm of glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly, with the option to increase dosing to twice weekly in individual patients.

Individual patient plots of parenteral support over time were based on actual parenteral support data from EASE-1 (n= 106) and EASE-2 (n= 39), with prescribed parenteral support data from EASE-3 added.

Furthermore, an MMRM model was applied for analysis of change from baseline in weekly parenteral support volume, with baseline defined as in EASE-1. In this analysis the treatment group was a time varying variable for patients allocated to placebo in EASE-1, as treatment group for these patients changed to glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly or glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly when entering EASE-2. In addition, the MMRM model included visit as a categorical variable, with covariates comprising the stratification factor (weekly parenteral support volume requirements &lt;12 l/week versus ≥12 l/week), treatment-by-visit interaction, and baseline weekly parenteral support volume. Treatment had 3 levels in the randomized placebo-controlled period, and 4 levels in the randomized extension period, i.e.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

- glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly → glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly
- glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly → glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly
- placebo → glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly
- placebo → glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly

For EASE-1, the two placebo groups above were collapsed to one group denoted 'placebo'. Within treatment group estimates of the mean change from baseline in weekly parenteral support volume were presented.

No imputation of missing data was done prior to applying the MMRM.

A longitudinal pooled presentation was also made for the three key secondary responder endpoints, which were presented descriptively (frequency and corresponding percentage) by nominal visit using an as-observed approach. No imputation of missing data was applied for the analysis of these binary endpoints. This is in alignment with the presentation of long-term data for other drugs within the same disease area, and comparison with those results will therefore be possible with this approach.

A longitudinal plot of change in weekly parenteral support volume from baseline during glepaglutide treatment in EASE-1 and EASE-2 is shown in Figure 34. In the supportive analysis that included EASE-1 data plus 6-month EASE-2 data, the difference for glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly versus placebo (estimated difference of -2.29 l/week (95% CI -3.62;-0.96); p=0.0010) align with the corresponding analysis results based on EASE-1 data alone (estimated difference of -2.28 l/week (95% CI -3.83;- 0.73); p=0.0039).

Please note that while Figure 34 depicts the trajectory since initiation of glepaglutide treatment, the change from baseline in parenteral support volume is for all patients relative to the baseline as defined in EASE-1, except for patients randomized directly into EASE-2 where EASE-2 baseline is used.

Similarly to what was observed for the 24-week observation period of EASE-1, mean body weight remained relatively stable during the extended exposure period of EASE-1 and EASE-2 combined, supporting that maintenance of reduced parenteral support requirements during extended exposure to glepaglutide was not achieved at the expense of loss in body weight. This conclusion is further supported by the lack of any apparent correlation between change in weekly parenteral support volume from baseline to nominal visit month 24 versus change in body weight from baseline to nominal visit month 24 when considering EASE-1 and EASE-2 combined overall and by BMI subgroup. A corresponding analysis of the correlation between the total energy content of parenterally

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

administered macronutrients versus change in body weight showed some degree of correlation, but the correlation was modest and the variation in data was large.

## 5.3.9. Healthcare professional engagement

The EMA invited the United European Gastroenterology (UEG) association to share information on different aspects of SBS treatment, including the standard of care of SBS treatment, determination of the required parenteral support volume, and unmet medical needs. The UEG response is summarized below.

According to the UEG, SBS requires multidisciplinary intestinal failure care. Parenteral support remains essential for many. GLP-2 analogues are adjuncts to improve absorption and reduce parenteral support. Therapy is usually long-term, with response judged over months.

For glepaglutide, clinicians prioritise durable, clinically meaningful parenteral support reductions (volume and infusion days), fewer dehydration admissions, improved nutrition/quality of life, and convenient dosing.

A manageable safety profile includes transient gastrointestinal symptoms and fluid retention mitigated by monitoring and PS adjustment. Guidance is needed on obstruction risk, hepatobiliary/pancreatic events and surveillance.

Parenteral support is titrated via 24-h intake/output, urine output targets, weight and laboratory markers, with frequent early professional review.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Glepaglutide could improve access and adherence and may shift practice if efficacy is at least comparable with favourable safety.

Key gaps: predictors of response, very long-term safety (including neoplasia), pregnancy/lactation data, and real-world outcomes in complex patients (e.g., Crohn's).

Implementation guidance and central line-associated bloodstream infection (CLABSI)-prevention protocols will support safe, equitable uptake.

## 5.3.10. Overall discussion and conclusions on clinical efficacy

## 5.3.10.1. Discussion

## Design and methodology

The pivotal study ZP1848-17111 (EASE-1) is a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled study in which 106 SBS patients with intestinal failure (SBS-IF) were included, randomized 1: 1: 1 and treated with glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly (n= 35), glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly (n= 35), or placebo (n= 36) subcutaneously during a 24-week treatment period after a stabilization and optimization of parenteral support treatment. To maintain blinding, all 3 treatment arms involved twice weekly dosing (glepaglutide and/or placebo). Parenteral support requirements were evaluated through the use of 48-hour balance periods scheduled as closely as possible to and within 7 days of study site visits. Efficacy assessment included parenteral support use as recorded by the patient in an eDiary on an ongoing basis. The primary endpoint was the change in weekly parenteral support volume from baseline to week 24.

Study patients who had completed the EASE-1 study could enroll into the ongoing double-blind, long-term extension study ZP1848-17127 (EASE-2) in which the clinical effects of subcutaneous glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly (n= 61), and glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly (n= 53) are evaluated during 2 years. Some patients who met the selection criteria of the EASE-1 study were directly included into the EASE-2 study (n= 18). Study patients who had completed the EASE-2 study could enroll into the ongoing double-blind study ZP1848-20110 (EASE-3) with a treatment period of 2 years. In this study, study patients (n= 57) are treated with glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly, which could be increased to glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly subcutaneously.

Limited numbers of study patients are included in the conducted clinical studies. Since SBS is rare with a prevalence in the European Union below 100,000 cases (Massironi et al. 2020), the low patient numbers are acceptable provided the efficacy of glepaglutide is demonstrated at an acceptable safety level.

The pivotal EASE-1 study, and its subsequent open-label extension studies EASE-2 and EASE-3 have been conducted in SBS patients with intestinal failure (SBS-IF) whereas the proposed indication includes all patients with SBS. It was initially unclear whether the observed clinical effects in SBS-IF patients can be extrapolated to SBS patients with intestinal insufficiency (SBS-II) and SBS-IF patients who receive limited amounts of parenteral support (i.e. &lt; 12 l/week) In a post-hoc analysis, reductions in parenteral support volume upon glepaglutide treatment tended to be observed in SBSIF patients with both larger (≥12 l/week) and smaller (&lt;12 l/week) parenteral support requirements. SBS-IF patients may become SBS-II patients upon discontinuation of parenteral support in case of effective glepaglutide treatment. There is a risk of rebound effects with increased parenteral support needs upon glepaglutide treatment discontinuation. Because of this, glepaglutide may also be considered for SBS-II patients.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The clinical effects of glepaglutide were evaluated in SBS study patients who were stabilized after surgery. The company agreed to include this in the indication statement. Several methodological limitations were identified in the study designs of the conducted EASE-1, EASE-2, and EASE-3 studies. The primary outcome in the pivotal EASE-1 study is change in weekly parenteral support volume from baseline to week 24. Study patients measured their weekly parenteral support volumes and recorded the measured volumes in eDiaries. Such a primary outcome is associated with a number of uncertainties. The applicant has confirmed that the eDiary design does not allow to deduce whether there was no reported parenteral support volume or there was no parenteral support required, with notes-to files being the only way to separate true missing values from actual 0Ls consumed. Based on that important shortcoming, the actual volume was calculated on the basis of an algorithm which was designed to replace missing daily recordings with 0s under specific conditions (in short: if other weekly measurements accounted for &gt;= 90% of the prescribed parenteral support volume). The applicant has provided further information about how missing data entries were distributed. This included an overview of how many days were expected and not expected to be missing (e.g. in a week with a prescription for 5 days, 2 days would be expected to be missing), and this turned out to be only a small number of days. The applicant has also provided another sensitivity analysis, where all these days would be imputed (to the disadvantage of the applicant, as missing days could be truly 0), and this impacted the results only in minor ways. While there are overall uncertainties in this calculation, the initial calculation is considered to be sufficiently representative of the true effect of glepaglutide. In the EASE-1 study, the primary outcome was changed at version 4 of the protocol dated October 2018. The primary outcome of 20% reduction in weekly parenteral support volume was moved to key secondary and the outcome change from baseline in weekly parenteral support volume at week 24 was changed from key secondary to primary outcome in line with FDA and CHMP advice (EMEA/H/SA/3790/1/2018/SME/II). SAP v5 changed the algorithm for actual parenteral support recording. The change was substantial and the v3/4 algorithm was considerably more conservative. More specifically, according to this former algorithm, missing daily parenteral support volumes would be imputed by the average of the available daily recording, as opposed to 0s, dictated by the final algorithm. Additionally, if there were 3 or less recordings in a 7-day period, this was considered missing. The applicant has clarified that SAP was updated to v5 when 97% of the patients were recruited and for 84% the primary endpoint (24w follow up) was available. Alignment with clinical practice and interpretation of the algorithm were cited as reasons for doing so. Even though the arguments related to alignment with clinical practice brought forward are well appreciated (there are days with truly 0L required and the previous algorithm can be argued to be overconservative), clinical practice had not changed over the course of the trial. Yet, the overall reasoning for the changes can be understood and is considered to be not unreasonable. The applicant has also supplied another sensitivity analysis showing that the results are sufficiently robust. Considering the amount of parenteral support volume was prescribed by the hospital investigator at study visits, and because this prescription is directly related to the primary efficacy endpoint - decrease in parenteral support volume at week 24 compared to baseline - it is essential that the parenteral support volumes are prescribed according to protocol and administered to patients. Uncertainties were identified in this process, including over- and under-prescription of the PS volume. The applicant has provided a requested worst-case analysis, assuming that all under-prescriptions should have happened and that their effect is sustained (which is not realistic, but the worst case). This calculation reduces the effect of glepaglutide by less than 400 ml. The initial calculation is therefore, despite initial insecurities, robust enough and can be considered valid. It was initially unclear whether the hospital staff or study monitors could adjust recorded eDiary data of study patients, and whether blinding of study patients and personnel during the conduct of the EASE-1 and EASE-2 studies was appropriately maintained. It has in the meantime been clarified that these modifications were possible, but it only happened to a minor extent. The applicant has provided more information about the instances when eDiary entries were adjusted. This did indeed happen in only a smaller number of cases. Multiple changes were performed for the entries of two patients, but neither was weaned off. Overall, the changes are considered to be small and without impact on the analysis. Study treatment was injected into the abdomen or one of the thighs during the whole treatment period. In pharmacokinetic analyses, glepaglutide exposure (Cavg en Ctrough) was about 35% higher upon injection into the abdomen as compared to injection in one of the thighs. Further sensitivity analyses show that the impact on the effect size per injection site is considerable. Subgroup analyses show that for both glepaglutide 10 mg once and twice weekly that injection into the abdomen is statistically significant for the endpoint change in parenteral support volume at week 24 as compared to baseline, but that injection into the thigh is not. However, upon post-hoc exclusion of 3 outliers in the thigh placebo group, the observed difference between glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly and placebo twice weekly became statistically significant for this endpoint. This does not apply to the comparison between glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly and placebo twice weekly for this endpoint.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Participants

Adult SBS-IF patients who required parenteral support at least 3 days per week and with a remaining small bowel in continuity of estimated length of less than 200 cm were eligible for inclusion into the pivotal EASE-1 study. The study population was diverse enough in terms of age, and underlying cause of SBS to be deemed representative of the SBS population to be treated. It was initially unclear whether glepaglutide dosage should be adjusted in patients with severe renal or hepatic impairment. The applicant indicated later that severe renal impairment/end-stage renal disease had neither a clinically relevant impact on the primary PK endpoints of glepaglutide AUC0-168h and Cmax in study ZP1848-18131, nor in a population PK model. In addition, in PK analyses no clear association was found between mild or moderate hepatic impairment and glepaglutide exposure in patients with SBS. Further, the results from the hepatic indocyanine green clearance study in the Phase 2 SBS population suggest that the liver does not contribute significantly to the overall plasma clearance of glepaglutide. Limited data are available in patients with moderate hepatic impairment and no data are available in patients with severe hepatic impairment. Hence, according to the currently available study data no glepaglutide dose adjustments are needed in patients with renal or hepatic impairment, but caution should be exercised if treating SBS patients with hepatic impairment with glepaglutide as outlined in 4.2 of the SmPC. 154 Subjects were screened in the EASE-1 study, 35 were enrolled but not randomized. Reasons were non-compliance with the in-/exclusion criteria (n= 13), failure to meet parenteral support stability criteria during the parenteral support stabilisation phase leading to randomisation (n= 8), or other, less frequent reasons (n= 14). It is unknown why 5 SBS study patients (glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly: n= 4, placebo: n=1) who had completed study EASE-1 did not enrol into the EASE-2 study. Considering the limited number of study patients, and non-enrollment prior to the administration of study medication in the EASE-2 study, it is anticipated that the non-enrollment of aforementioned 5 SBS study patients will not affect the results of the EASE-2 study to a clinically relevant extent. Study patients who completed the EASE-1 study were eligible to enroll in the EASE-2 study. The baseline characteristics of SBS patients in the EASE-1 study (n= 106) and SBS patients who directly

enrolled in the EASE-2 study (n= 18) were comparable. Although there is a high risk of chance findings in the limited number of SBS patients who directly enrolled in the EASE-2 study there appear some differences with respect to the underlying cause of SBS and the proportion of stoma patients in these studies (54.7 vs. 27.8% of patients). The treatment response to glepaglutide may be larger in patients with a stoma due to a larger need for parenteral support and larger clinical effects of glepaglutide. However, considering the limited number of SBS patients who were included directly in the EASE-2 study, the impact on the overall study results of the EASE-2 study is limited. Results Change in weekly parenteral support volume from baseline to week 24. In the EASE-1 study, the primary endpoint parenteral support volume generally decreased over time in all three treatment groups, with a dose-response relationship of increasing parenteral support volume reduction with increasing glepaglutide dose. Superiority relative to placebo with respect to the primary endpoint change in weekly parenteral support volume from baseline to week 24 was shown for glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly (estimated difference of -2.3 l/week (95% CI: -3.8;-0.7); p=0.0039), but not for glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly (estimated difference of -0.9 l/week (95% CI -2.5; 0.7); p=0.27) (treatment policy estimand). Similar trends were observed in sensitivity analyses with respect to the primary endpoint and for another estimand using a hypothetical strategy. No notable subgroup effects were identified in the EASE-1 study. Upon glepaglutide treatment at a dosage of 10 mg twice weekly the parenteral support volume until week 24 is about 10 l/week from week 8 to week 24 in the EASE-1 and EASE-2 study. Hence, the clinical effects of glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly, i.e. the recommended glepaglutide dosage, are comparable in both studies. An unexpectedly high treatment response was observed in placebo-treated SBS patients at week 24 in the EASE-1 study for the primary endpoint (LS means -2.9 (95% CI -3.9; -1.8 )) and multiple secondary endpoints. Such a phenomenon is observed commonly, but the reason for this in the studies on glepaglutide is unknown. The analysis of the supplied raw data indicates that the observed effects of study treatment are impacted by non-adherence of the physicians to the treatment algorithm, and that the parenteral support volume should have been further reduced by the physicians. Further worst-case calculations by the applicant indicate this effect is potentially present, but not to an extent that it affects the interpretation of the study. Clinical response, defined as achieving ≥20% reduction in weekly parenteral support volume from baseline to both weeks 20 and 24. Based on an anchor-based analysis assessing the association between Patient Global Impression of Change (PGIC) data (ordinal data) and percentage change in parenteral support volume from baseline to weeks 12 and 24 a ≥20% reduction in weekly parenteral support volume from baseline to both weeks 20 and 24 is considered clinically relevant. In the EASE-1 study, at least 20% reduction in weekly parenteral support volume from baseline to both weeks 20 and 24 was observed in 65.7%, 45.7%, and 38.9% of study patients, who were treated with respectively glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly, glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly, and placebo. The reported proportion as compared to placebo treatment was higher for glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly (difference 26.6 (95% CI 4.3; 48.9) p= 0.02) but not for glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly (difference 5.5 (95% CI -16.2; 27.1) p= 0.63). By contrast, in the EASE-2 study the proportions of patients achieving a reduction of at least 20% in parenteral support volume (l/week) from baseline to month 6 was comparable for glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly (n= 53) and glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly (n= 61) (74.5% vs. 67.9%). Reduction in days on parenteral support ≥1 day/week from baseline to week 24. In the EASE-1 study, at least 1 day/week reduction in the days on parenteral support from baseline to week 24 from baseline to both weeks 20 and 24 was observed in 51.4%, 34.3%, and 19.4% of

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

study patients, who were treated with respectively glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly, glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly, and placebo. The reported proportion as compared to placebo treatment was higher for glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly (difference 31.7 (95% CI 11.4; 51.9) p= 0.0043) but comparable for glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly (difference 13.3 (95% CI -5.4; 32.0) p= 0.17).

In line with the EASE-1 study, the proportion of patients who had achieved a reduction of at least 1 day/week on parenteral support at month 6 was higher in the glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly group (61.7%) than in the glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly group in the EASE-2 study (45.3%) (estimated treatment difference 20.3% (95% CI 2.5; 38.1); p= 0.03).

Change in number of days on parenteral support per week from baseline to week 24. In the EASE-1 study, the change in the number of days on parenteral support per week from baseline to week 24, tended to be larger for glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly as compared to placebo treatment (estimated difference of -0.85 days/week (95% CI 1.42; -0.27); p=0.004), whereas the comparison for glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly relative to placebo did not reach statistical significance (estimated difference of -0.80 days/week (95% CI -1.62; 0.02); p=0.06).

However, in the EASE-2 study, the estimated mean change from baseline at month 6 in days on parenteral support was comparable for glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly (-1.3 days/week) and glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly (-1.8 days/week).

Reduction in weekly parenteral support volume of 100% (weaned off) at week 24. Complete weaning off from parenteral support (achieving enteral autonomy) represents the ultimate treatment goal in SBS patients with intestinal failure. In the EASE-1 study, weaning off from parenteral support was observed in 14.3%, 11.4% and 0% of study patients who were treated with glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly, glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly, and placebo. The estimated treatment differences as compared to placebo treatment tended to be higher for glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly (14.1% (95% CI 2.5; 25.6); p= 0.02) as compared to glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly (11.2% (95% CI 0.7; 21.6); p= 0.04). In the EASE-2 study in contrast with the EASE-1 study, the proportions of study patients with a 100% reduction in parenteral support volume at month 6 were comparable for glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly (14.1%) and glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly (20.8%). In the EASE-3 study, 26.1% of patients were weaned off parenteral support. 24.4 (23.3) hours/week, compared to -10.9 (23.5) hours/week for glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly,

Change in duration of parenteral support infusions per week from baseline. In the EASE-1 study, the mean (standard deviation) reductions in the duration of parenteral support infusions per week (hours) from baseline to week 24 tended to be larger for glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly (- and -9.4 (16.1) hours/week for placebo. No statistical analysis was conducted.

Also, in the EASE-2 study, the change from baseline in mean duration (hours/week) of parenteral support at month tended to be larger with glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly (-24.4 hours/week) compared to glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly (-18.9 hours/week).

Change in body weight from baseline to week 24. In study EASE-1, the mean percentage change in body weight from baseline to week 24 was -0.27% for glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly, 0.41% for glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly, and -0.46% for placebo. There were no specific trends across treatment groups with respect to shifts between BMI groups during the treatment period of the study.

Similarly, the mean body weight remained comparable to baseline in both glepaglutide arms in the EASE-2 study. Also, the EASE-3 study mean body weight remained stable during the study.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Change in plasma citrulline levels from baseline to week 24. Mean citrulline concentration (a biomarker for small bowel enterocyte mass (Crenn et al. 2000)) tended to increase in both glepaglutide treatment groups but not for placebo in the EASE-1 study, with the greatest increase observed for the glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly group. With glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly dosing, the increase from baseline was significantly greater vs. placebo (p=0.0025). The dosedependent increase in citrulline levels provides clinical evidence that glepaglutide is acting via the GLP-2 receptor to increase enterocyte mass, supporting the expected intestinotrophic effect of glepaglutide on the intestinal epithelium.

In line with this, in the EASE-2 study, the mean change from baseline in plasma citrulline at month 6 tended to be larger for glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly (11.5 µmol/l) compared to glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly (7.0 µmol/l). The mean plasma citrulline levels remained stable during the EASE-3 study with a change from baseline at month 6 of 8.7 µmol/l.

Patient Global Impression of Change (PGIC) improvement at week 24. In the EASE-1 study, the PGIC was much/very much improved in 48.6%, 31.4%, and 5.6% of study patients who were treated with glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly, glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly, and placebo respectively. The reported proportion as compared to placebo treatment tended to be higher for glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly (difference 42.9 (95% CI 24.8; 61.0) p&lt; 0.0001) and glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly (difference 25.4 (95% CI 8.3; 42.4) p= 0.006).

Change in SBS-I and EQ-5D-5L from baseline to week 24. Trends towards a treatment benefit of glepaglutide were noted for SBS-Impact Scale (SBS-I) for pain, exhausted/tired, sleep and affected mood, whereas no noteworthy differences between treatment groups were noted for European Quality of Life-5 Dimension 5 Level (EQ-5D-5L) in the EASE-1 study.

In the EASE-2 study, no clinically relevant changes from baseline were observed for SBS-I scores or EQ-5D-5L scores for either treatment at month 6.

Change in bowel movements or stoma bag emptying from baseline to week 24. A decreased number of bowel movements and stoma bag emptyings from baseline to week 24 were observed for all treatment groups but most prominently for the glepaglutide treatment groups in the EASE-1 study. The reduction in number of bowel movements or stoma bag emptyings achieved during the EASE-1 study was sustained in the EASE-2 extension study.

Exploratory bone markers. In the EASE-1 study, no difference between active treatment and placebo was shown for change from baseline to week 24 for the EASE-1 exploratory bone markers serum C-terminal telopeptide of type I-collagen (P-CTx) and plasma procollagen type I N-propeptide (P-PIND).

In additional analyses, remote visits during the COVID-19 pandemic had no or limited impact on the clinical effects of study treatment with respect to the primary endpoint of the EASE-1 study. No notable subgroup effects were identified.

Pooled analyses. Data from the first 6 months of glepaglutide treatment in study EASE-2 for those patients who were randomized to placebo in study EASE-1 (and for the additional patients who were recruited directly into EASE-2) were added to EASE-1 study data in a pre-specified analysis. In this analysis, a statistically significant difference was shown for glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly (n= 61) versus placebo (n= 36) (estimated difference of 2.3 (95% CI -3.6; -1.0); p=0.001), but also for glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly (n= 62) versus placebo (n= 36) (estimated difference of -2.0 (95% CI -3.3;-0.7); p=0.004).

In another pooled analysis of study data from the EASE-1 and EASE-2 studies, reductions in parenteral support volume compared to baseline are maintained up to 21 months. Whether this also applies to time points beyond 21 months is unknown due to the limited study data in that time frame.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Overall, the observed clinical effects of glepaglutide for most parenteral support-related endpoints were larger for glepaglutide as compared to placebo. The clinical effects of glepaglutide remain with time upon continued glepaglutide administration. In addition, the Patient Global Impression of Change (PGIC) at week 24 compared to baseline was larger for glepaglutide than for placebo treatment in the EASE-1 study. Further, mean plasma citrulline concentrations, a biomarker for small bowel enterocyte mass (Crenn et al. 2000), tended to increase upon glepaglutide treatment but not for placebo in the EASE-1 study.

The achievement of at least 20% reduction in weekly parenteral support volume from baseline to both weeks 20 and 24 was considered clinically relevant based on patients' scoring with respect to the patient's global impression of change (PGIC). In line with this, in the EASE-1 study, reductions in parenteral support volume and also the PGIC at week 24 as compared to baseline tended to be larger for glepaglutide compared to placebo treatment. However, the scores for other quality of life parameters such as the SBS-Impact Scale (SBS-I) and the European Quality of Life-5 Dimension 5 Level (EQ-5D-5L) did not clearly improve upon glepaglutide treatment in the EASE-1 and EASE-2 studies. Hence, the perceived benefit of glepaglutide treatment to SBS study patients is limited.

For the primary endpoint and most secondary endpoints of the EASE-1 study, the observed effect sizes as compared to placebo treatment were larger for glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly as compared to glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly. This distinction seems to be smaller in the combined analysis of the EASE-1 and EASE-2 study. However, in the supportive analysis that included EASE-1 data plus 6-month EASE-2 data, the difference for glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly versus placebo (estimated difference of -2.29 l/week (95% CI -3.62;-0.96); p=0.0010) align with the corresponding analysis results based on EASE-1 data alone (estimated difference of -2.28 l/week (95% CI -3.83;-0.73); p=0.0039). Hence, the available study results support the appropriateness of the general recommended glepaglutide posology of 10 mg twice weekly.

In general, the proportions of subjects in particular special populations in the controlled and noncontrolled studies are comparable.

No additional study results of the EASE-2 and EASE-3 studies are yet available after the interim database lock. The final reports of these studies will be available in 2027.

Another phase 3 study, similar to the EASE-1 study, has been developed on request of the US Food and Drug Administration (EASE-5 study). The applicant provided a brief outline of this study. The EASE-5 study is ongoing (start date: 11 th of February 2026).

## 5.3.10.2. Conclusions on the clinical efficacy

Statistically significant, and clinically meaningful improvements in parenteral support were observed in patients treated with glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly compared to placebo-treated patients.

The proposed indication has been updated to include that patients should be stable following a period of intestinal adaptation after surgery and is therefore now in line with the studied SBS patient population.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 5.4. Clinical safety

For the purpose of this document, the following definitions apply:

'Adverse event - AE' means any untoward medical occurrence in a subject to whom a medicinal product is administered, and which does not necessarily have a causal relationship with this treatment.

'Serious adverse event - SAE' means any untoward medical occurrence that at any dose requires inpatient hospitalisation or prolongation of existing hospitalisation, results in persistent or significant disability or incapacity, results in a congenital anomaly or birth defect, is life-threatening, or results in death. The definition (in line with ICH E2A) includes important medical events that may not be immediately life-threatening or result in death or hospitalisation but may jeopardise the patient or may require intervention to prevent one of the other outcomes listed in the definition above.

'Adverse drug reaction - ADR' means any untoward and unintended response to a medicinal product related to any dose administered, for which, after a thorough assessment, a causal relationship between the medicinal product and the adverse event is at least a reasonable possibility, based for example, on their comparative incidence in clinical studies, or findings from epidemiological studies and/or on an evaluation of causality from individual case reports.

## 5.4.1. Safety data collection

## Safety data collection

Please also refer to the table of clinical studies in section 5.1.2. Tabular overview of clinical studies.

The glepaglutide clinical development program for the treatment of SBS consists of four phase 1 studies, one phase 2 trial and four phase 3 studies. The majority of the safety data in the glepaglutide marketing authorization application (MAA) originate from three phase 3 studies: EASE-1 (pivotal phase 3 trial, placebo-controlled), EASE-2 (extension trial to EASE-1, randomised to twice weekly [TW] or once weekly [OW] treatment regimen), and EASE-3 (extension trial to EASE-2, open-label starting with OW regimen).

All adverse events (AEs), whether serious (SAE) or non-serious, were to be reported from the time a signed and dated Informed Consent Form (ICF) is obtained until the end of the post-treatment followup period (which may include contacts for follow-up of safety). All (S)AEs were to be recorded by the investigator on the standard AE form in the case report form (CRF).

In the phase 3 studies, the following adverse events of special interest (AESIs) were defined in the protocols based on the pharmacodynamic (intestinotrophic) effects and/or reported class effects of GLP-2 receptor agonists (RAs):

- Neoplasms (malignant and benign)
- Suspicion of liver injury
- Pancreatitis
- Cholecystitis

## Pooling of studies

For the comparison of the safety profile of glepaglutide TW and glepaglutide OW to that of placebo, data from the placebo-controlled pivotal 24-week phase 3 trial, EASE-1, is used. In this trial, the treatment groups were randomised 1:1:1. Data from this trial are used for comparative purposes to evaluate potential differences in the AE profile between glepaglutide TW vs glepaglutide OW vs placebo.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The treatment continuation pool includes safety data from EASE-1 and EASE-2 with up to 2.5 years of exposure to glepaglutide. Both studies have a double-blind, randomised, parallel-group design with a 1:1 ratio between TW and OW, and the dosing frequency within each treatment group is fixed (i.e. patients do not switch between TW and OW). Patients randomised to placebo in EASE-1 were rerandomised to TW or OW (1:1) in EASE-2. Therefore, the treatment continuation pool allows for a randomised comparison of glepaglutide TW vs glepaglutide OW.

The long-term extension pool includes data from the randomised, parallel-group studies (EASE-1 and EASE-2) and from the open-label, single-arm trial (EASE-3), where all patients receive glepaglutide OW with the possibility to increase the dosing frequency to TW at the investigator's discretion if deterioration in PS need is observed. This pool is used to provide a comprehensive safety assessment of glepaglutide TW and OW across the full time period spanned by EASE-1, EASE-2, and EASE-3 (up to 4.5 years of exposure).

## Data cut-off dates

The overall safety profile of glepaglutide is based on the 1 January 2024 cut-off. Furthermore, available data in the safety (pharmacovigilance) database on SAEs, pre-defined AESIs, other important events and pregnancies collected between the data cut-off for the clinical database and the data lock point for this submission (i.e. from 2 January 2024 to 1 February 2025) are reported separately (or clearly marked).

## 5.4.3. Adverse events

## Adverse events in the placebo-controlled trial EASE-1

During 24 weeks of double-blind treatment in EASE-1, approximately 94% of the patients in the glepaglutide groups and 75% of the patients in the placebo group experienced at least one AE; the event rates were numerically higher in the two glepaglutide groups than in the placebo group (Table 46). The event rate for glepaglutide TW was slightly higher than for glepaglutide OW.

## Supportive information from other studies and longitudinal pool

Longitudinal treatment pool

Of the 123 patients treated with glepaglutide across EASE-1, EASE-2, and EASE-3 as of 1 January 2024 during treatment with glepaglutide for up to 4.5 years, 118 (95.9%) experienced at least one AE.

Of the 3212 AEs in total, 223 (approximately 7%) were serious; more than half of the patients (64.2%) experienced at least one SAE.

Like for the treatment continuation pool, the majority of the AEs were considered mild and only 3% were considered severe (102 out of 3212 events). Of the 3212 events, 1859 (57.9%) were considered related to glepaglutide; the majority of these were injection site reactions.

Phase II trial 15073

In trial 15073 where patients with SBS-IF or SBS-II were treated with glepaglutide 0.1 mg, 1 mg, or 10 mg once daily during two 3-week dosing periods, injection site reactions were the most frequently reported AEs.

## 5.4.3.1. Adverse drug reactions

AEs were medically reviewed, taking into account the known effects of GLP-2 analogues, their relative incidence between treatment groups and whether there was a dose-response between glepaglutide TW, OW and placebo. In addition, AEs were reviewed based on an evaluation of causality from individual case reports. The selected terms are all events, where a causal relationship between glepaglutide and the AE is at least a reasonable possibility. AEs without at least a suspected causal relationship were excluded, hereunder AEs that are clearly not related to glepaglutide, AEs that are more likely to be related to the patients' underlying SBS or use of PN, and AEs deemed contrary to GLP-2 effects. The incidences of the adverse reactions are based on the percentage of patients who received glepaglutide 10 mg TW or OW (i.e. glepaglutide total) and experienced at least one occurrence of each specific adverse reaction. Glepaglutide total was selected to ensure completeness of the evaluation, given that the safety profile (qualitatively speaking) of the two dosing regimens, i.e. glepaglutide TW and OW, did not differ. The frequencies were derived using data from the long-term extension pool (EASE-1, EASE-2, and EASE-3 studies) with a cut-off date of 01-Jan-2024. This pool is based on 123 SBS patients aged 18 to 82 years, who were treated with glepaglutide TW/OW for up to 4.5 years. Per the European Commission guideline on Summary of Product Characteristics (SmPC), the adverse reactions are categorized by MedDRA system organ classification and frequency: Very common (≥ 1/10); common (≥1/100 to &lt; 1/10); uncommon (≥ 1/1,000 to &lt; 1/100); rare (≥ 1/10,000 to &lt; 1/1,000); very rare (&lt; 1/10,000). Within each system organ class, the adverse reactions are ranked with the most frequent reactions first, while within each frequency category, the adverse reactions are presented in the order of decreasing seriousness (per Sponsor evaluation).

## 5.4.4. Adverse events of special interest, serious adverse events and deaths, other significant events

## Deaths

As of the data lock point for this submission (1 February 2025), a total of 7 deaths has been reported in patients treated with glepaglutide across all the studies in the clinical development program. In addition, 3 post-trial deaths have been reported to Zealand Pharma.

The 7 deaths occurring during treatment with glepaglutide comprised:

- 4 deaths occurring before the interim cut-off (1 January 2024): 2 patients died during EASE-2, treated with glepaglutide OW (n=1 of cardiopulmonary failure and multiple organ dysfunction syndrome), 1 patient died during open-label treatment phase of EASE-3 (death) and 1 patient receiving glepaglutide OW during EASE-4 (metastatic neoplasm).
- 3 deaths occurring between the interim cut-off and the data lock point (all occurring during open-label treatment with glepaglutide in EASE-3). For these 3 deaths, the AE data have been extracted from the safety (pharmacovigilance) database: pneumonia, cerebral haemorrhage, and duodenal ulcer haemorrhage.

The 3 post-trial deaths comprised:

- 2 deaths occurring 3 and 6 months after withdrawal from EASE-1: ileus with necrotising pancreatitis and death.
- 1 death occurring 2½ months after withdrawal from EASE-2: acute hepatic failure.

All 3 patients had completed the safety follow-up visit and were hence no longer active in the trial. By nature, the unsolicited information received by Zealand Pharma after trial completion is sparse and should be interpreted with caution. The information was captured in the safety (pharmacovigilance) database only.

## Serious Adverse Events

Serious Adverse Events the placebo controlled trial - EASE-1

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

During 24 weeks of double-blind treatment with investigational product in EASE-1, the incidence of SAEs was 25.7% in the glepaglutide TW group, 28.6% in the glepaglutide OW group, and 19.4% in the placebo group (Table 50). The risk difference [95% CI] to placebo was 6.3 [-13.1; 25.7] in both glepaglutide groups.
