## Serious Adverse Events in the treatment continuation pool

During double-blind treatment with glepaglutide for up to 2.5 years in EASE-1 and EASE-2, a total of 176 SAEs were reported in 67 patients: 76 SAEs in 30 patients (49.2%) in the glepaglutide TW group and 100 SAEs in 37 patients (59.7%) in the glepaglutide OW group.

The overall event rates for glepaglutide TW and OW were 75.97 vs 86.56 events per 100 PYO; risk difference [95% CI]: -10.6 [-34.7;13.5].

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The majority (157 of 176 events; 89%) had resolved or were resolving as of 1 January 2024.

The SOCs with the highest overall incidences and event rates were 'Infections and infestations' (primarily CVC-related infections) and 'Gastrointestinal disorders', with no clinically relevant differences between glepaglutide TW and OW at SOC, HLT, or preferred term level.

A total of 3 SAEs were reported in the SOC 'Immune system disorders'; all occurred in the glepaglutide TW group. Similarly, in the SOC 'Blood and lymphatic system disorders', a total of 10 SAEs related to anaemia were reported in 2 patients, both in the glepaglutide TW group. However, these apparent differences are small in absolute terms and should be interpreted with caution based on the low number of patients with these events.

## Supportive information regarding Serious Adverse Events from other studies and the longitudinal pool

## Longitudinal pool

During treatment with glepaglutide for up to 4.5 years across EASE-1, EASE-2, and EASE-3, a total of 223 SAEs had been reported in 79 patients (64.2%) as of 1 January 2024. Of those, 47 SAE in 25 patients were reported during open-label treatment with glepaglutide in EASE-3.

Approximately one-third of the SAEs (74 out of 223 SAEs) occurred in the SOC 'Infections and infestations', primarily CVC-related infections (22 events of 'vascular device infection', 17 events of 'device related sepsis', 2 events of 'device related infection', and 1 event of 'catheter site abscess').

A total of 29 SAEs in 24 patients (19.5%) occurred in the SOC 'Gastrointestinal disorders': 8 SAEs pertaining to intestinal stenosis (including 1 event of 'subileus'), 2 SAEs of 'abdominal pain', and 19 SAEs distributed across different preferred terms.

A total of 18 SAEs in 13 patients (10.6%) occurred in the SOC 'Metabolism and nutrition disorders': 4 SAEs of 'dehydration', 5 SAEs of 'metabolic acidosis', 2 SAEs of 'lactic acidosis (in 1 patient), 2 SAEs of hypomagnesaemia, and 5 SAEs across different preferred terms.

A total of 15 SAEs in 13 patients (10.6%) occurred in the SOC 'General disorders and administration site conditions': 9 SAEs of 'pyrexia' and 4 CVC-related complications.

A total of 11 SAEs in 3 patients (2.4%) in the SOC 'Blood and lymphatic system disorders'; of these 10 SAEs (9 SAEs in 1 patient and 1 SAE in 1 patient) were 'iron deficiency anaemia' (all were worsening of existing iron deficiency anaemia).

Less than 10 SAEs were reported in the remaining SOCs; the majority of preferred terms were single occurrences.

## Other studies

There were no SAEs in any of the four phase 1 studies.

In the Phase 2 trial (Trial 15073), a total of 16 SAEs were reported in 7 patients (38.9%): 8 SAEs in 2 patients (16.7%) during treatment with the 0.1 mg dose and 8 SAEs in 6 patients (50.0%) during treatment with the 10 mg dose. The most frequently reported SAEs were within the SOCs 'Infections and infestations' (7 SAEs in 4 patients) and 'Gastrointestinal disorders' (4 SAEs in 3 patients), with no dose-response or difference in reporting pattern between treatment periods.

During EASE-4, a total of 14 SAEs in 6 patients (60%) were reported. The most frequently reported SAEs were within the SOCs 'Infections and infestations' (5 SAEs in 3 patients) and 'Gastrointestinal disorders' (5 SAEs in 2 patients).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Adverse Events of Special Interest and Other Safety Areas of Interest

## Neoplasms (malignant and benign)

Due to the intestinotrophic effects of GLP-2, GLP-2 receptor agonists (RAs) will increase the enterocyte mass and intestinal surface area. Thus, there is a hypothetical risk that glepaglutide may accelerate neoplastic growth in the intestinal tract, where GLP-2 receptors are mainly located.

As of the interim cut-off (1 January 2024) for this MAA, a total of 22 AESIs of neoplasms (benign and malignant) had been reported in the clinical database in 17 patients across EASE-1, EASE-2, and EASE-3. In addition, 7 new AESIs in ongoing studies (EASE-2 and EASE-3) have been reported in the safety (pharmacovigilance) database between 2 January 2024 and 1 February 2025, giving a total of 29 AESIs of neoplasms.

Of the 29 AESIs, 17 neoplasms reported in 14 patients were located in the gastrointestinal tract and 12 neoplasms in 11 patients were located in different organ systems (kidney, breast, bladder/ovary, parotid gland, enteric nervous system, and skin). In addition, 1 patient with multiple metastases of died during EASE-4.

No AESIs of neoplasms were reported in the 24-week, placebo-controlled trial, EASE-1. In the treatment continuation pool based on data from first dose of glepaglutide and up to 2.5 years of treatment in EASE-1 and EASE-2, 14 AESIs of neoplasm had been reported in 12 patients as of 1 January 2024: 4 AESIs in 4 patients (6.6%) in the glepaglutide TW group and 10 AESIs in 8 patients (12.9%) in the glepaglutide OW group. No dose-response was observed in the overall incidences of neoplasms for glepaglutide TW vs OW (6.6 vs 12.9%). Likewise, no dose-response was observed for gastrointestinal neoplasms or for neoplasms located outside the gastrointestinal tract.

Of the 29 AESIs reported in EASE-2 and EASE-3 up until 1 February 2025, 19 events in 16 patients were classified as benign, and 10 events in 9 patients were classified as malignant/unclassified. All classified neoplasms located in the gastrointestinal tract were benign, and the 10 malignant/unclassified neoplasms were distributed in different organ systems.

The 19 benign events comprised 16 gastrointestinal polyps (14 colon polyps, 1 gastric polyp, 1 rectal polyp), 1 pleomorphic adenoma, 1 pyogenic granuloma, and 1 adenoma (worsening of medical history).

## Suspicion of liver injury

Approximately 30% of the patients treated with glepaglutide in EASE-1, EASE-2 and EASE-3 had mild or moderate hepatic impairment at Baseline 2 (defined as last available value prior to the first glepaglutide dose).

In the 24-week, placebo-controlled trial, EASE-1, 4 AESIs were reported across all 3 treatment groups (2 with glepaglutide TW, 1 with glepaglutide OW, and 1 with placebo). In the treatment continuation pool based on data from first dose of glepaglutide and up to 2.5 years of treatment in EASE-1 and EASE-2, there was an indication of a faster onset for the first AESIs for glepaglutide TW vs OW, and the patients also seemed to have more events on average over the course of the trial. It should be noted, however, that during treatment with glepaglutide for up to 4.5 years across EASE-1, EASE-2 and EASE-3, liver function test mean values showed a decreasing trend over time or little or no change from baseline. Likewise, the proportion of patients with liver function test values above the reference range did not appear to increase over time for any of dosing regimens.

As of 1 January 2024, a total of 43 AESIs of suspicion of liver injury had been reported in the clinical database in 21 glepaglutide-treated patients during treatment for up to 4.5 years across EASE-1, EASE-2, and EASE-3. In addition, 5 AESIs have been reported in 5 patients in ongoing studies (EASE-2 and EASE-3) between 2 January 2024 and 1 February 2025 have been extracted from the safety database, giving a total of 48 AESIs of suspicion of liver injury. Of the 48 events in total, 43 were considered mild and 5 considered moderate; 8 of the 48 events were considered related to glepaglutide. All events were non-serious, but one Patient had co-reported SAEs of 'autoimmune hepatitis' and 'parenteral nutrition associated liver disease' (considered not/ unlikely related to glepaglutide), and treatment with glepaglutide was permanently discontinued.

For the majority of the AESIs, the increases in AST/ALT were transient; after the elevation, the values returned to the baseline level during continued treatment with glepaglutide. Likewise, the majority of the events (&gt;90%) were not associated with any clinical symptoms.

Two AESIs fulfilled the laboratory criteria of Hy's law. However, as there were other etiological factors affecting the liver parameters in these patients (an ongoing SAE of 'bile duct stone' and a diagnosis of IFALD with a component of autoimmune hepatitis), the two cases did not fulfil Hy's law.

## Pancreatitis

Due to the pharmacological (growth-promoting) actions of GLP-2, there is a theoretical risk that this, in susceptible patients, may lead to obstruction of the pancreatic duct. An obstruction will preclude the pancreatic juices from passing through the duct, resulting in an inflammatory response and ultimately pancreatitis.

As of 1 January 2024, 2 AESIs of pancreatitis had been reported in the clinical database across EASE-1, EASE-2, and EASE-3; both occurred in EASE-3. In addition, 1 AESIs reported in ongoing studies

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

(EASE-2 and EASE-3) between 2 January 2024 and 1 February 2025 has been extracted from the safety database; this event occurred in EASE-2.

One event was related to biliary obstruction and the other event was potentially related to concomitant treatment with doxycycline. The 2 events occurred approximately 3-4 years after initiation of treatment with glepaglutide, and both events have resolved. The last event (from the safety database; reported after the interim cut-off) occurred on Day 541 in a patient with a past history of alcohol addiction for 11 years. All 3 patients recovered from the events.

## Cholecystitis

In humans a risk of gallbladder diseases associated with GLP-2 RA treatment has been observed. In nonclinical studies, gallbladder dilatation and epithelial hyperplasia in the gallbladder have been observed in mice treated with glepaglutide.

As of 1 January 2024, 2 AESIs of cholecystitis had been reported in the clinical database across EASE-1, EASE-2, and EASE-3. In addition, 1 AESIs reported in ongoing studies (EASE-2 and EASE-3) between 2 January 2024 and 1 February 2025 has been extracted from the safety database.

One event occurred within the first 3 months of treatment in EASE-1; the event occurred in the presence of gallstones and was treated medically. One event occurred in EASE-2 after approximately 7 months of treatment and led to cholecystectomy. The two patients recovered within 2 weeks.

The last event (from the safety database; reported after the interim cut-off) occurred in EASE-3 after more than 3 years of treatment with glepaglutide. This patient also had a bile duct stone at the same time and had a medical history of cholecystolithiasis. The patient recovered with sequelae.

## Gastrointestinal stenosis and intestinal obstruction

Gastrointestinal stenosis and intestinal obstruction are known class of effect for treatment with GLP-2 RAs. Due to the intestinotrophic effects of this drug class, stenosis or obstruction may arise if the epithelial hyperplasia occurs at sites where the gastrointestinal lumen is restricted. Other causes of intestinal obstruction include hernia, adhesions in the abdomen following surgery, or strictures from an inflamed intestine, as observed in Crohn's disease.

As the patients included in the phase 3 studies had a surgical history of multiple resections or other abdominal surgeries and as one-third of the patients had Crohn's disease, they were at an increased risk of developing an intestinal obstruction compared to the healthy population.

During treatment with glepaglutide for up to 4.5 years across EASE-1, EASE-2, and EASE-3 up until 1 January 2024, 11.4% of the patients experienced one or more AEs related to gastrointestinal stenosis and intestinal obstruction. Of the 20 AEs reported in total, 1 event occurred during EASE-1 ('subileus' [glepaglutide TW]), and the remaining events occurred during EASE-2 and EASE-3. This may be explained by the fact that a partial or complete blockage of the intestinal lumen due to epithelial hyperplasia requires many weeks of treatment to develop.

The extent of epithelial hyperplasia is expected to increase with increasing doses, and in line with this, a dose-response was observed, with higher event rates for glepaglutide TW than for glepaglutide OW in the treatment continuation pool; with 11.00 vs 4.33 events per 100 PYO; risk difference [95% CI]: 6.7 [-0.9; 14.2]. A similar trend was observed for the exposure-adjusted incidence rates (6.38 vs 3.63 patients per 100 PYR; risk difference [95% CI]: 2.8 [-3.5; 9.0]). This dose-response is also observed in Figure 37.

In 2 patients, treatment with glepaglutide was permanently discontinued, and the patients recovered. In 3 other patients, treatment was temporarily discontinued:

- In 1 of these patients, the obstruction had an alternative aetiology.
- 1 patient (who had a medical history of numerous intestinal sub-obstructions) experienced 2 SAEs related to intestinal obstruction, where treatment was temporarily discontinued. For the first event, the patient recovered after 6 days, and treatment was resumed. For the second event, the patient recovered after 17 days and resumed treatment with no further obstructionrelated events were reported.
- 1 patient experienced an SAE of 'intestinal obstruction'; the event was conservatively managed (bowel rest and symptom control). Glepaglutide was discontinued for 5 weeks; no further obstruction-related events were reported.

In addition, 1 SAE of 'intestinal obstruction' reported in the ongoing trial, EASE-2, between 2 January 2024 and 1 February 2025 has been extracted from the safety database; although the investigator deemed the obstructive symptoms in this patient to be due to adhesions, a possible relationship to glepaglutide cannot be fully dismissed.

## Stoma complications

Stoma complications are a well-known class effect for GLP-2 RAs. Treatment with native GLP 2 as well as GLP-2 analogues leads to an increased mesenteric blood flow that, combined with the growthpromoting actions of these agents, may lead to swelling/enlargement of the stoma site or to stoma bleeding.

Of the 123 patients treated with glepaglutide in EASE-1, EASE-2, and EASE-3, 62 patients (50.4%) had a stoma and were hence included in the analysis of AEs related to stoma complications.

A total of 43 AEs related to stoma complications were reported in 20 (32.3%) patients across EASE-1, EASE-2 and EASE-3 as of 1 January 2024. Most of these events had an early onset; approximately

Glepa 10 mg OW (N=62)

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

two-thirds of the events were reported during EASE-1, and the remaining events were primarily reported in EASE-2 (2 events were reported in EASE-3).

Figure 38: Stoma complications (MedDRA HLT) by patient - EASE-1, EASE-2 and EASE-3 - swimmer plot

Of the 43 events, 40 were non-serious and considered mild or moderate, and 3 were SAEs, of which 1 led to permanent discontinuation of glepaglutide; this patient had other AEs reported at the same time.

In addition, 1 SAE of 'stoma site haemorrhage' reported in ongoing studies (EASE-2 and EASE-3) between 2 January 2024 and 1 February 2025 has been extracted from the safety database.

In the 24-week, placebo-controlled trial, EASE-1, a dose-response was observed, and no events were reported with placebo. Similarly, a dose-response was observed in the phase 2 trial. This may be explained by a higher GLP-2 receptor activity for the higher doses/dosing regimen. In the treatment continuation pool based on data from first dose of glepaglutide and up to 2.5 years of treatment in EASE-1 and EASE-2, stoma complications occurred at similar incidences and numerically lower event rates for glepaglutide TW than for glepaglutide OW. The fact that the difference between the doses seems to level off over time likely reflects adaptive changes in the intestinal mucosa occurring over time.

During 52 weeks of treatment with open-label glepaglutide OW in EASE-4, 90% of the patients experienced one or more stoma complications. These were primarily stoma nipple enlargements, which generally occurred during the first month of treatment. A similar trend was observed in the phase 2, cross-over trial 15073.

Overall, stoma complications appear to be a common adverse drug reaction for glepaglutide and other GLP-2 RAs due to their intestinotrophic actions that form the basis for their mechanism of action. The events were readily managed; &gt;90% of the events were non-serious, approximately 80% were mild, and only one event led to permanent discontinuation of treatment with glepaglutide; this patient had other AEs reported at the same time.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 5.4.5. Discontinuation due to adverse events

Across all studies in the development program, 15 patients had adverse events leading to withdrawal from trial as of 1 January 2024:

- 3 patients across the four phase 1 studies
- 1 patient in the phase 2 trial
- 11 patients across the four phase 3 studies (EASE-1, EASE-2, EASE-3, and EASE-4)

During treatment with glepaglutide for up to 4.5 years across EASE-1, EASE-2, and EASE-3, a total of 20 AEs in 16 patients led to permanent discontinuation of treatment with glepaglutide

Adverse events leading to permanent discontinuation of glepaglutide - phase 3:

- 3 events in 3 patients in EASE-1 (TW: 2 patients; OW: 1 patient)
- 14 events in 11 patients in EASE-2 (TW: 6 patients; OW: 5 patients)
- 3 events in 3 patients in EASE-3

In addition, 1 event led to permanent discontinuation of treatment in 1 patient in EASE-4 (glepaglutide OW).

## 5.4.6. Safety in special populations

In EASE 1, most patients were in the age group ≥18 to &lt;65 years. The age group ≥75 years consisted of 4 patients, 2 treated with glepaglutide TW and 2 with placebo.

In the 24-week, double-blind, placebo-controlled treatment period of EASE-1, the incidence of all AEs was overall similar for patients &lt;65 years and patients ≥65 years in the glepaglutide groups, and lower for patients &lt;65 years in the placebo group. In contrast, the incidence of SAEs was higher in patients &lt;65 years than in patients ≥65 years in the glepaglutide groups, and similar for placebo across age groups.

As also seen in EASE-1, exposure for patients ≥65 years of age was numerically higher in the glepaglutide TW group compared to OW during double-blind treatment with glepaglutide for up to 2.5 years in EASE-1 and EASE-2. Rates of AEs appeared similar across the age groups &lt;65 years and ≥65 years for the most common preferred terms.

## Sex

In the 24-week, double-blind, placebo-controlled treatment period of EASE-1, the incidences of all AEs and SAEs were overall similar for female patients compared to male patients, both for glepaglutide total and for placebo.

During double-blind treatment with glepaglutide for up to 2.5 years in EASE-1 and EASE-2, the overall incidences and rates of all AEs and SAEs were similar for male and female patients. In the glepaglutide TW group, the rate of SAEs was higher in male patients compared to female patients (mainly driven by 1 male patient reporting multiple SAEs), whereas the opposite trend was noted in the glepaglutide OW group. Differences in rates of injection site reactions (multiple preferred terms) were observed between female and male patients, but with no consistent pattern. Overall, the results did not indicate any differences in the safety profile between female and male patients.

## Stoma yes/no

In the 24-week, double-blind, placebo-controlled treatment period of EASE-1, incidences of all AEs were similar for patients with and without a stoma in the total glepaglutide group, whereas the incidence of all AEs was higher for patients without a stoma in the placebo group.

The incidence of SAEs was numerically lower for patients with a stoma compared to patients without a stoma for total glepaglutide, whereas the incidence of SAEs in the placebo group was higher for patients with a stoma compared to those without a stoma.

See section 6.2.3.5

## 5.4.8. Safety related to drug-drug interactions and other interactions

The pharmacokinetic (PK) drug-drug interaction potential of glepaglutide is low, with no inhibition of cytochrome P450 (CYP) isoenzymes or human transporters. The main route of elimination is via proteolytic metabolism.

Due to the reduced length of the remaining bowel in patients with SBS and thereby a reduced absorption of orally administered drugs, some oral concomitant medications are prescribed in doses higher than usual in patients with SBS. Once treatment with glepaglutide is initiated, the absorption of orally administered drugs may increase as a consequence of the intestinotrophic action of glepaglutide, forming the basis for pharmacodynamic drug-drug interactions. There was no indication of clinically relevant pharmacodynamic interactions between glepaglutide and narrow-therapeutic-index medications commenced or ongoing at the first glepaglutide dose date of EASE-1 and EASE-2.

## 5.4.9. Vital signs and laboratory findings

No notable differences in haematology, chemistry, and coagulation results were observed over time, and no consistent or clinically relevant changes from baseline in results were observed.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 5.4.10. Post-marketing experience

Thus far, glepaglutide is not authorized for marketing in any country.

## 5.4.11. In vitro biomarker test for patient selection for safety

Not applicable

## 5.4.12. Overall discussion and conclusions on clinical safety

## 5.4.12.1. Discussion

## 5.4.12.1.1. Overall assessment of available safety data

## Safety database

The clinical development program for glepaglutide consists of in total 4 phase 1 studies, 1 phase 2 study and 4 phase 3 studies. The Phase 1 studies enrolled healthy adults (studies 08216 part 1, 16182, 19045, overall n=213 exposed to glepaglutide), subjects with renal impairment (Phase 1 study 18131, n=8 exposed to glepaglutide) and patients with Crohn's disease (study 08216 part 2, n=8 exposed to glepaglutide). The Phase 2 study and 1 Phase 3 study enrolled patients with SBS-IF or SBS-II (study 15073 and EASE-4 respectively, n=28 exposed to glepaglutide), while EASE-1 through EASE-3 enrolled patients with SBS-IF (n=123).

The clinical safety information of different studies was pooled into the treatment continuation pool and the long-term extension pool. The treatment continuation pool (EASE-1 and EASE-2) includes safety data from up to 2.5 years of exposure to glepaglutide. The long-term extension pool consists of data from the EASE-1, EASE-2 and EASE-3 studies for patients receiving at least one dose of glepaglutide.

The main information regarding the safety profile of glepaglutide was obtained from the placebocontrolled study (EASE-1), in which in total 35 patients received glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly for 24 weeks, and the treatment continuation pool (EASE-1 + EASE-2), in which 61 patients provided information on the glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly regimen. Therefore, the assessment of the safety profile regarding the twice weekly administration of glepaglutide is based on a limited number of patients, with 61 patients attributing to 100.0 PYO in EASE-1 and EASE-2. Only 42 patients were exposed for at least 1 year, which at least somewhat reflects the proposed unlimited and - for most of the patients - lifelong treatment. The limited database can be considered sufficient, in view of the below issues resolved. Whilst a limited database hampers a thorough assessment of the safety profile with regards to the detection of less common adverse drug reactions this can be considered acceptable in this rare disease. The applicant confirmed that final CTRs for EASE-2 and EASE-3 is expected in Q1 2027.

In all Phase 3 studies, the assessment of adverse events occurred in a similar manner. This ensures comparability of safety assessment across studies. In general, the methods to assess the safety of glepaglutide in these studies are appropriate. No clear justification was provided why not all safety information was provided based on a cut-off date of 1 February 2025 for the ongoing Phase 3 studies EASE-2 and EASE-3. However, considering that information regarding serious adverse events, pre-defined adverse events of special interest, other important events and pregnancies collected between the data cut-off for the clinical database and the data lock point for this submission (i.e. from 2 January 2024 to 1 February 2025) are included, this issue is closed.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Adverse events

During EASE-1, the vast majority of patients experienced at least 1 adverse event, with most of these adverse events being mild to moderate in intensity. The most commonly reported treatmentemergent adverse events and number of patients experiencing these were as follows: Injection site reaction (9 [26%] patients in the glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly group, 15 [43%] in the glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly group and 1 [3%] in the placebo group), nausea (7 [20%] patients in the glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly group, 3 [9%] in the glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly group and 1 [3%] in the placebo group), abdominal pain (5 [14%] patients in the glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly group, 6 [17%] in the glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly group and 2 [6%] in the placebo group), and injection site erythema (9 [26%] patients in the glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly group, 15 [43%] in the glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly group and 1 [3%] in the placebo group). The fact that both injection site reactions and gastrointestinal adverse events are dominant in the amount of treatment-emergent adverse events is expected in view of the subcutaneous injections and the pharmacodynamic profile of glepaglutide. \"Injections site reactions\" has been included as adverse drug reaction in Table 1 of section 4.8 of the SmPC. In the section \"description of selected adverse reactions\" more specific information to describe the type of injection site reactions that might occur is included.

It is noteworthy that dehydration is only seen in the glepaglutide group and more frequently in the glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly group compared to the glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly group. Based on the underlying medical condition (the vast majority of patients with dehydration had no or only a remnant small intestine &lt;100 cm) and the lack of a clear mechanism, it can be agreed that a causal relationship is unlikely. However, it remains that dehydration only occurred in the glepaglutide group and not in the placebo group. This might be due to the fact that parenteral support was more likely to be reduced in the treated group versus the untreated group. Therefore, the warning surrounding dehydration in section 4.4 of the SmPC has been strengthened.

Adverse events in the system organ class \"Psychiatric disorders\" were more frequently observed in the glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly group compared to the placebo group. Adverse events of anxiety and insomnia (both n=1) were observed in the glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly group and not in the placebo group. This is noteworthy as those adverse events are considered adverse drug reactions for an approved GLP-2 analogue (teduglutide). It should be noted that 25% of participants in EASE-1 and -2 had a concurrent medical history in the SOC 'Psychiatric disorders', primarily 'depression' and 'insomnia'. Only 1 AE of anxiety was reported during the studies, which was not considered related to glepaglutide (medical history included bipolar disorder). Therefore, it can be agreed that the evidence to include anxiety as an ADR is currently not sufficient. In EASE-1, -2, -3 and -4 studies 7 patients experienced insomnia/nocturnal restlessness, which were mostly mild to moderate in intensity. Only 1 AE of nocturnal restlessness was considered possibly related to the investigational drug. This is not considered a strong signal to include insomnia as an ADR.

Although the frequency of events increased with time, the safety profile of the treatment continuation pool and the long-term treatment pool were largely comparable to the safety profile observed during the placebo-controlled trial. However, it should be noted that differences in event rate and frequency of severe adverse events during start of treatment (EASE-1), after longer term exposure (including EASE-2) are no longer visible. This indicates that no clear dose effect in safety was observed between glepaglutide 10 mg once and twice weekly regimens.

Overall, the safety profile of glepaglutide was mainly characterised by injection site reactions, gastrointestinal disorders, intestinal obstruction and stoma complications. This is in line with the pharmacological effects of glepaglutide, its main target organ and mode of administration. In addition, this is in line with the known safety profile of GLP-2 receptor agonists.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Deaths

In total 7 deaths occurred (6% of the exposed population), which is considerable in this relatively small population. However, the condition of these patients should be kept in mind. It is not agreed that none of the deaths are considered related to glepaglutide. For one death, which occurred in a patient diagnosed with liver metastasis from an adenocarcinoma most likely of colorectal origin, based on this narrative, a role of glepaglutide cannot be excluded. Glepaglutide is contraindicated in active or suspected malignancy.

## Serious adverse events

The most frequently reported serious adverse events occurred in the system organ class infections and infestations for both treatment groups, with 16 patients (26%) experiencing 27 serious adverse events (event rate 26.99) in the glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly group and 21 patients (34%) experiencing 34 serious adverse events (event rate 33.9). The majority of Serious adverse events in this system organ class were related to device infections (8 patients in the glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly group and 9 patients in the glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly group experienced vascular device infection and 5 vs 6 patients device related sepsis respectively). This is not unexpected in this vulnerable population.

In total, 7 (12%) and 12 (19%) of patients in the TW and OW group respectivley experienced serious adverse events in the system organ class \"gastrointestinal disorders\". In total 4 patients exposed to glepaglutide (2 each in the glepaglutide 10 mg once and twice weekly group) experienced gastrointestinal stenosis and obstruction and 2 patients (both in the glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly group) experienced duodenal and small intestinal stenosis and obstruction. Due to the growth promoting effect of glepaglutide, intestinal obstruction is not an unexpected finding, however, due to the vulnerability of the population it is disadvantageous. Intestinal obstruction is a labelled side effect and the SmPC recommends in section 4.4. temporarily discontinuation of glepaglutide if this occurs which is considered acceptable.

In total 4 patients in the glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly group (7%) and 7 patients in the glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly group (11%) experienced serious adverse events in the system organ class \"metabolism and nutrition disorders\". Three patients (n=2 in the glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly group and n=1 in the glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly group) experienced a serious adverse event of dehydration. This could potentially point to an excessive reduction in parenteral solution. The applicant had been asked to discuss the potential influence of reduction in parenteral solution in the occurrence of these serious adverse events. Based on the information provided, it can be agreed that it is unlikely that for the cases provided reduction of PS played a substantial role in the development of the SAEs of dehydration. It is more likely that these events occurred due to heat, diarrhoea and kidney failure. However, the warning surrounding fluid status following parenteral support reduction in SmPC 4.4 had been strengthened, which is appreciated and is considered sufficient for prescribers.

In total &gt;60% of patients experienced 223 serious adverse events of which 22 were considered related to glepaglutide. The majority of these events were included as adverse drug reactions in section 4.8 of the SmPC. Constipation was initially not included, while GLP-2 is known to reduce gastric motility and as stated in the narrative by the applicant, constipation is common after treatment with GLP-2 analogue. This is supported by the fact that constipation considered related to glepaglutide treatment was observed in EASE-1 (n=1 in the glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly group compared to n=0 in the placebo group) and over all 3 clinical studies, related adverse events of constipation were observed in 3 patients and 1 case of gastrointestinal hypomotility. Based on a plausible mechanism of action, reduction of gastric motility, and no other aetiology being provided for 2 at least possibly related cases, constipation has finally been included as an ADR in Table 1 in section 4.8 of the SmPC.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Based on the information presented, inclusion of the term \"colorectal polyp\" in Table 1 of section 4.8 is appreciated, due to the instestinotrophic effects of glepaglutide, pre-clinical and clinical findings. This term covers \"large intestine polyp\", \"rectal polyp\" and \"colon adenoma\" AEs, which were observed and considered related to the investigational drug.

Nasopharyngitis, influenza, upper respiratory tract infection, and lower respiratory tract infection are adverse drug reactions for GLP-2 analogue teduglutide medicinal product Revestive (EU/1/12/787/001-002), but is not seen as ADR for glepaglutide

## Adverse events of special interest

In the phase 3 studies, based on the pharmacodynamic (intestinotrophic) effects and/or reported class effects of GLP-2 receptor agonists the following adverse events of special interest were defined: pancreatitis, cholecystitis, suspicion of liver injury and neoplasms (benign and malignant). As for pancreatitis and cholecystitis adverse events and serious adverse events have been observed during the studies, inclusion of pancreatitis and cholecystitis as an adverse drug reaction in Table 1 of section 4.8 in the SmPC is justified and was added. Three adverse events of special interest of cholecystitis had been reported until 1 February 2025. Two of the events were related to gall or bile duct stone. Cholecystitis is an adverse drug reaction in the SmPC, and in addition a relevant precautionary statement is present in section 4.4 of the proposed SmPC. The applicant addressed that, based on the same mechanism underlying the ADR of cholecystitis i.e. relaxation of the gallbladder and potential proliferation and cell hyperplasia in the gallbladder, glepaglutide may have contributed to the development of cholangitis in one patient during the studies. In total, 48 AESIs of suspicion of liver injury were reported in 26 glepaglutide treated patients across the studies. Of these 48 adverse events of special interest, the vast majority were mild, and none were serious and none of the elevations met the criteria of Hy's law. Considering the population, it is not unexpected to find numerous adverse events of special interest of suspicion of liver injury. Based on the information presented there is no clear indication that glepaglutide increases the risk of liver injury.

In total, 29 adverse events of special interest of neoplasms (benign and malignant) have been reported across three clinical studies, EASE-1, EASE-2 and EASE-3. This number is considerable given the small number of patients exposed to glepaglutide (n=124 across all 3 studies). From the theoretical point of view, as glepaglutide is a growth factor inducer and induces epithelial hyperplasia, there is some concern of promotion of benign and/or malignant tumours. This concern is further corroborated by the data derived in animals, where adenoma and adenocarcinoma have been induced by high doses of glepaglutide. Therefore, with the current data available, the concern of promotion of benign and/or malignant tumours cannot be excluded (see section serious adverse events above). The applicant has included latest Development Safety Update Report from the period 17 December 2024 up to 16 December 2025 in the latest response. In this period seven events of neoplasm AESIs were reported in 6 participants (1 Duodenal polyp, 2 Colorectal adenoma, 4 Large intestine polyp). All of the events were deemed as possibly related. In section 4.4 of the SmPC, endoscopy or other relevant imaging is recommended once a year during the first two years of treatment and then at a minimum of five years. This is in line with what is recommended for other GLP-2 receptor agonists. -) The applicant has added duodenal polyp as ADR section 4.8, and updated section 4.4 regarding risk of upper GI-tract neoplasia which is considered acceptable.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Other adverse events

Over the duration of the clinical studies, 20 AEs related to gastrointestinal stenosis and intestinal obstruction were reported in 14 patients (11.4%) as of 1 January 2024. As expected, based on the pharmacodynamic effect of glepaglutide on epithelial hyperplasia, higher event rates of gastrointestinal stenosis and intestinal obstruction were seen with glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly compared to glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly, with 11.00 vs 4.33 events per 100 PYO; risk difference [95% CI]: 6.7 [-0.9; 14.2]. As physicians treating these patients are experienced in diagnosing and treating cases of gastrointestinal stenosis and intestinal obstruction, the risk is considered manageable. To aid prescribers in the diagnosis of clinical presentations, the adverse drug reaction \"intestinal obstruction\" was reworded in the SmPC to \"intestinal stenosis and/or obstruction\". In addition, a description of intestinal stenosis and/or obstruction was included in the section \"description of selected adverse reactions\" to highlight which preferred terms were observed contributing to this overarching term. In total 62 patients (50%) exposed to glepaglutide had a stoma, of which 20 patients (32%) experienced a stoma related complication during any of the studies and 4 serious adverse events occurred. It is noteworthy that the majority of stoma complications occurred early during treatment. The applicant suggests that this is due to adaptive changes in the intestinal mucosa, however this adaptation is not reflected in the gastrointestinal obstructions. It might also be due to small numbers. Based on the pharmacological effect of glepaglutide (swelling of intestinal mucosa due to hypertrophy of intestinal mucosa as well as increased intestinal blood flow) swelling of the stoma, nipple enlargement and bleeding and in some cases, obstruction can be expected. Inclusion of \"stoma complications\" in the SmPC as an adverse drug reaction is appreciated. Furthermore, a description of stoma related complications was included in the section \"description of selected adverse reactions\" under 4.8 of the SmPC to highlight which preferred terms were observed contributing to the overarching term of \"stoma complication\". During EASE-1, adverse events of fluid retention and fluid overload were more commonly reported in the TW group compared to both OW and placebo groups (20.0%, 5.7% and 8.3%, respectively). In the treatment continuation pool, no dose-response was seen anymore. Based on the pharmacological effect of glepaglutide (enhancement of intestinal absorptive capacity) fluid overload is an adverse event that can be expected, especially during initiation of therapy. Inclusion of \"fluid retention/fluid overload\" as an adverse drug reaction is appreciated. However, \"fluid retention/fluid overload\" is an overarching term and a description of \"fluid retention/fluid overload\" was therefore included in the section \"description of selected adverse reactions\" to specify the preferred terms considered related to glepaglutide observed that led to inclusion of this term (i.e. 'oedema peripheral', 'hypervolaemia', 'generalised oedema' and 'fluid retention'). Hypersensitivity reactions are a known risk for administration of peptides/proteins. A slight dose response relationship was observed with a numerically higher incidence of broad scope SMQ was seen in the glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly group compared to the glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly group (26% vs 23%). Three serious adverse events of hypersensitivity were observed, which were all considered treatment-related. Hypersensitivity is described in SmPC section 4.8. The episodes observed across all studies with glepaglutide administered for up to 4.5 years were readily managed. No episodes of anaphylactic reactions to glepaglutide and no toxic epidermal necrolysis were observed. In the long-term extension pool a high proportion (87.6%) of the patients developed antiglepaglutide antibodies (ADA) and in vitro glepaglutide neutralizing antibodies (NAb)

(47%). Overall, a total of 208 ADA positive subjects out of 314 exposed and evaluable subjects have been identified. Incidences were seen with both glepaglutide 10 mg once and twice weekly treatment, with a tendency to higher incidences for glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly as compared to 10 mg twice weekly, time to development was 12-24 weeks and longer for the neutralising antibodies, the ADA levels were seen to decrease again overtime during continued glepaglutide. Three patients experienced generalized hypersensitivity reactions, all 3 patients were ADA positive at the time of the event. Also, an association between injection site reactions and ADA was observed, with higher event rates in patients, who developed ADA, as compared to ADA negative patients. Since the majority of patients developed ADA, it is agreed that these observations are difficult to interpret. Hypersensitivity is described in SmPC section 4.8.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In the proposed SmPC, (safety mitigating) situations where glepaglutide therapy should be paused (and potentially restarted) are proposed. 87.6% Of the study patients develop ADAs. Based on very limited data there is no suggestion of immunogenicity concerns following re-initiation of glepaglutide treatment after a pause.

In the EASE-1 study, iron deficiency tended to be more common among study patients who were treated with glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly as compared to glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly (9 vs. 1 event), but the imbalance could be explained by multiple events in the same patient in the glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly group (6 events). However, also in the treatment continuation pool iron deficiency anaemia tended to occur mor often in the glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly versus glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly group (17.99 vs. 2.60 events/100 PYO). The applicant clarifies that most of the patients had an ongoing medical history of anaemia or of iron deficiency anaemia. In addition, either haemoglobin values did not decrease during treatment compared to during placebo treatment and/or the run-in period and/or treatment with iron supplements during continued treatment with glepaglutide increased the haemoglobin values.

No clinically meaningful trends were observed for clinical laboratory values, including chemistry, haematology, or other laboratory parameters over time in any of the studies. No clinically meaningful observations were noted for vital signs.

## Discontinuations

In total across all studies, 15 patients had adverse events leading to withdrawal from any trial. In the phase 3 studies, 11 out of 124 patients withdrew (9%). In total 21 adverse events in 17 patients led to permanent discontinuation of treatment with glepaglutide across all phase 3 studies (including EASE-4). The majority of adverse events leading to withdrawal from the trial or treatment discontinuation are included as adverse drug reaction in section 4.8 of the SmPC. All adverse events leading to withdrawal/treatment discontinuations not included as adverse drug reaction were single occurrences and considered unlikely related to glepaglutide, which can be agreed based on underlying disease, co-morbidities and other aetiologies that could play a role.

## Safety in special populations

The applicant has presented subgroup analyses for age, gender and presence of stoma on adverse events. Due to the low patient numbers in some subgroups these analyses are of limited value. However, overall, it appears that there are no extreme differences for the investigated subgroups compared to the overall population.

12 Adverse events related to liver disease or liver injury were reported in 8 patients across the EASE-1, EASE-2, and EASE-3 studies.in 7 of the 8 patients there was an alternative reason more likely to explain the event than treatment with glepaglutide (history of TIPS, ongoing liver disease history, no increase from baseline, multiorgan failure after iatrogenic bowel perforation, autoimmune hepatitis). The last patient had no relevant liver disease history and had an event of moderate, nonserious 'hepatic fibrosis'. The applicant provides a reference showing up to 40% of adult patients with SBS develop intestinal failure associated liver disease (IFALD) (Zafirovska M, Nutrients 2023;15(14). It is agreed that patients with SBS-IF are at high risk of developing hepatic complications, and a single event without any alternative explanation is not enough for suspicion of a causal relationship to glepaglutide. A low proportion of study patients with moderate hepatic impairment (n= 3) have been included in the study, which is reflected in the proposed SmPC.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Drug-drug interactions

The main route of elimination of glepaglutide is via proteolytic metabolism rather than to common drug metabolising enzymes. Therefore, drug interactions with concurrently administered drugs are unlikely. However, because glepaglutide can cause increased gastrointestinal absorption, treatment may lead to increased bioavailability of concomitant medications. Based on the information provided no clear evidence of a clinically relevant pharmacodynamic interaction can be observed. However, with the current data available, this concern cannot be fully ruled out and the treating physicians and patients are made clearly aware of this potential effect. Although information is provided regarding increased absorption in section 4.5, a warning is included in section 4.4. on the monitoring of medicinal products requiring titration of with a narrow therapeutic index.

## 5.4.12.1.2. Adverse drug reactions (ADRs) in the SmPC

The ADRs proposed by the applicant for inclusion in the SmPC are described in section 6.4.3.1 above and in the proposed SmPC.

## 5.4.12.2. Conclusions on clinical safety

The safety package for glepaglutide is acceptable in size and duration, even though it is unlikely that the safety package will detect rare adverse reactions, due to the limited number of study patients. The proposed target population is comparable to the patient population included in the clinical (safety) studies.

Overall, the safety profile of glepaglutide was mainly characterised by injection site reactions, gastrointestinal disorders, intestinal stenosis/obstruction and stoma complications. This is in line with the pharmacological effects of glepaglutide, its main target organ and mode of administration. In addition, this is in line with the known safety profile of GLP-2 receptor agonists. The vast majority of patients experienced at least one treatment-emergent adverse event, with the majority of adverse events being mild to moderate in intensity.

Adverse drug reactions related to the pharmacodynamic action of glepaglutide lead to a relatively high burden of serious, and sometimes severe adverse events, including obstructive effects to bowel, biliary, and pancreatic epithelia as well as GI-tract polyps (including few events of gastric and small intestine polyps). However, these can be considered manageable by the prescriber with the implemented SmPC wordings.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 6. Risk management plan

## 6.1. Safety specification

## 6.1.1. Proposed safety specification

The applicant proposed the following adjusted summary of safety concerns in the RMP:

Table 58: Summary of safety concerns in the proposed RMP

| Summary of safety concerns   | Summary of safety concerns                                                                          |
|------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Important identified risks   | Gallbladder and Biliary tract Disease (cholecystitis, cholangitis, and cholelithiasis) Pancreatitis |
| Important potential risks    | Not applicable                                                                                      |
| Missing information          | Not applicable.                                                                                     |

## 6.1.2. Discussion on proposed safety specification

The safety profile of glepaglutide is based on safety data from clinical studies EASE-1, EASE-2 and EASE-3, comprising a total of 123 patients of which 93 patients had exposure duration ≥ 1 year, 16 patients exposure duration ≥ 24 weeks &lt; 1 year, and 14 patients had &lt; 24 weeks exposure duration.

The CHMP advised the applicant to monitor the below initially proposed safety concerns through routine pharmacovigilance activities such as monitoring in the PSUR, which is deemed sufficient to minimize and further characterize the risks at the moment.

The important identified risks, which were proposed by the applicant and are considered sufficiently covered in the proposed SmPC and have been removed from the RMP as safety concerns:

- Colorectal polyp (sections 4.3, 4.4 and 4.8)
- GI stoma complication (sections 4.3, 4.4 and 4.8)
- Intestinal stenosis and/or obstruction (4.4 and 4.8)

The proposed important potential risk 'Hypersensitivity' is mentioned in the proposed SmPC in sections 4.3 and 4.8, for which further characterization through routine pharmacovigilance activities is considered sufficient. Because of this, the applicant removed 'Hypersensitivity' from the initially proposed list of safety concerns in the RMP.

Cholecystitis is included as ADR in section 4.8 of the proposed SmPC. Upon request the applicant has included it as important, identified risk. The same applies to pancreatitis.

Gastric polyps/neoplastic growth have been seen in clinical studies with glucagon-like peptide-2 (GLP-2) analogues. Large intestine polyps are considered well-characterized and sufficiently covered in the proposed SmPC. Since polyps in the GI tract elsewhere than in colon (i.e. one duodenal and one gastric polyp) have been observed in clinical studies. Upon request, polyps in the GI tract are now mentioned in the proposed SmPC in sections 4.4 and 4.8, therefore this risk is now adequately covered.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Regarding 'Lack of experience in patients with severe hepatic impairment', and 'Lack of experience in pregnant or lactating women', without specific safety concerns, inclusion of these populations as missing information is not supported. in addition, the proposed safety concern 'Lack of experience in paediatric population' is considered off-label use. Later, the applicant removed aforementioned safety specifications from the RMP.

## 6.2. Pharmacovigilance plan

## 6.2.1. Proposed pharmacovigilance plan.

Based on the currently available information, routine pharmacovigilance activities that entail adverse reactions reporting and signal detection are deemed sufficient. The applicant has a structured plan for the identification of any new safety concerns. The applicant maintains systems and standard practices for routine pharmacovigilance activities to collect reports of suspected adverse reactions (including spontaneous reports, reports from clinical studies, reports of pregnancy/lactation exposures, overdoses and medication errors); prepare reports for regulatory authorities (e.g. individual case safety reports (ICSR), periodic safety update reports (PSUR), etc.), and maintain continuous monitoring of the safety profile of approved products (including signal detection, issue evaluation, updating of labeling, and liaison with regulatory authorities). The applicant maintains a Pharmacovigilance System Master File (PSMF), which contains details of these systems and standard practices. The routine pharmacovigilance activities described above combined with targeted follow-up questionnaire are deemed adequate and appropriate for the ongoing evaluation of the safety profile of glepaglutide.

No additional pharmacovigilance activities are deemed necessary.

## Planned additional pharmacovigilance activities

The applicant did not propose any additional pharmacovigilance activities.

## 6.2.2. Discussion on the pharmacovigilance plan

## 6.2.2.1. Routine pharmacovigilance activities

Routine pharmacovigilance is sufficient. Proposed routine PhV activities are appropriate.

## 6.2.2.2. Additional pharmacovigilance activities

There are no additional pharmacovigilance activities proposed, and this is acceptable.

## 6.3. Plans for post-authorisation efficacy studies

There are no post-authorisation efficacy studies proposed, and this is acceptable.

## 6.5. RMP summary and RMP annexes overall conclusion

The RMP Part VI and the RMP Annexes are acceptable.

## 6.6. Overall conclusion on the Risk Management Plan

The CHMP and PRAC consider that the risk management plan version 0.3 is acceptable.

## 7. Pharmacovigilance

## 7.1. Pharmacovigilance system

The CHMP considers that the pharmacovigilance system summary submitted by the applicant fulfils the requirements of Article 8(3) of Directive 2001/83/EC.

## 7.2. Periodic safety update reports (PSURs) submission requirements

The active substance is not included in the EURD list and a new entry will be required. The new list of Union reference dates (EURD list) entry uses the European birth date (EBD) to determine the forthcoming Data Lock Points. The requirements for submission of periodic safety update reports for this medicinal product are set out in the Annex II, Section C of the CHMP Opinion.

## 8. Product information

## 8.1. Summary of product characteristics (SmPC)

## 8.1.1. SmPC section 4.1 justification

The following indication is proposed for glepaglutide medicinal product Zeydovio:

Zeydovio is indicated for the treatment of adults with Short Bowel Syndrome (SBS). Patients should be stable following a period of intestinal adaptation after surgery.

In the pivotal study ZP1848-17111 (EASE-1), adult SBS patients were included who had a diagnosis of SBS with the latest intestinal resection being at least 6 months prior to screening and considered stable with regard to parenteral support need and who had no restorative surgery planned in the study period. In this patient population the efficacy of glepaglutide was demonstrated at an acceptable safety level.

In conclusion, the approved indication is aligned with the population studied in the pivotal clinical study.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 8.2. Labelling

## 8.2.1. User consultation

## 8.2.1.1. Conclusion from the checklist for the review of user consultation

The results of the user consultation with target patient groups on the package leaflet submitted by the applicant show that the package leaflet meets the criteria for readability as set out in the Guideline on the readability of the label and package leaflet of medicinal products for human use.

## 8.3. Additional monitoring

Pursuant to Article 23(1) of Regulation No (EU) 726/2004, Zeydovio (Glepaglutide) is included in the additional monitoring list since it contains a new active substance which, on 1 January 2011, was not contained in any medicinal product authorised in the EU.

Therefore, the summary of product characteristics and the package leaflet include a statement that this medicinal product is subject to additional monitoring and will allow quick identification of new safety information. The statement is preceded by an inverted equilateral black triangle.&gt;

## 9. Benefit-risk assessment

## 9.1. Therapeutic context

## 9.1.1. Disease or condition, proposed therapeutic indication

The proposed indication for glepaglutide is: \"Zeydovio is indicated for the treatment of adults with Short Bowel Syndrome (SBS). Patients should be stable following a period of intestinal adaptation after surgery.\"

Patients with short bowel syndrome (SBS, defined as &lt;200 cm of functional small intestine) have a reduced intestinal absorptive surface area due to extensive surgical bowel resection or congenital diseases. As a consequence, the uptake of nutrients, fluids, electrolytes, vitamins and trace elements is reduced, which impairs the ability of affected individuals to maintain fluid and metabolic balances when receiving a conventional diet. It can lead to dehydration, malnutrition and weight loss if left untreated. It is a highly heterogenous disease, in part due to variability with respect to the location and extent of resection as well as the degree of adaption of the residual bowel. Some SBS patients are able to adapt physiologically and compensate for their malabsorption by increasing their oral intake (also referred to as intestinal insufficiency; SBS-II). More severely affected individuals depend upon parenteral support (PS) to compensate their malabsorption (also referred to as intestinal failure; SBSIF).

SBS is a rare condition and estimates of prevalence is uncertain, primarily due to under-reporting and the absence of reliable patient databases. The prevalence of SBS is reported to be approximately 1.4 cases per million in Europe with large variability between countries (0.4/million in Poland vs. 40/million in Denmark).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 9.1.2. Available therapies

For a detailed description, please see section 2.1 of this document.

Treatment consists of:

Parenteral support via a central venous catheter is the primary and life-saving nutritional therapy for SBS patients, who are unable to adapt physiologically and compensate for their malabsorption by increasing their oral intake.

Surgical interventions aim to increase bowel length, potentially leading to bowel autonomy and weaning from parenteral support.

Symptomatic pharmacological therapy for SBS aims at optimising the remnant colon function, and include:

Antisecretory drugs (H2-receptor antagonists and proton pump inhibitors [PPI's])

Antimotility drugs (e.g. loperamide, codeine, diphenoxylate-atropine and opium)

Antibiotic treatment is recommended for SBS patients who have motility disorders (including those with dilated segments of residual small bowel, blind loop etc.) and who suffer from symptoms of bacterial overgrowth.

Intestinotrophic hormones stimulating intestinal mucosal growth. Two treatments are currently approved for SBS patients: Somatotropin (only approved in US) and the GLP-2 analogue teduglutide (Revestive).

Glepaglutide is a potent, long-acting GLP-2 analogue in a stable, aqueous formulation for subcutaneous (SC) administration. The stability in aqueous solution enables glepaglutide to be prepared as a readyto-use, liquid formulation with a shelf life of 30 months at 2-8°C and in-use storage at room temperature (25°C) for up to 2 weeks. The proposed dose is 10 mg twice weekly (TW), injected SC via a ready-to-use, single-dose auto-injector.

## 9.2. Main clinical studies

For a detailed description of the main clinical studies supporting this application, please refer to section 5.3.2 of this document.

The pivotal study ZP1848-17111 (EASE-1) is a randomised, double-blind, placebo-controlled study in which 106 adult SBS patients with intestinal failure (SBS-IF) were included, randomised and treated with glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly (n= 35), glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly (n= 35), or placebo (n= 36) subcutaneously during a 24-week treatment period after a stabilisation and optimisation of parenteral support treatment. The study was conducted in the EU and North America. The study was designed to demonstrate the superiority of once weekly and twice weekly subcutaneous injections of 10 mg glepaglutide versus placebo in stable SBS patients based on change in actual weekly parenteral support volume from baseline to week 24.

## 9.3. Favourable effects

In pharmacodynamic studies, glepaglutide was shown to decrease the wet weight of ostomy output or diarrhoea, the parenteral support volume and to increase the urine weight, the wet weight absorption, the change in urine weight minus oral intake, absorption of energy, macronutrients and electrolytes, and citrulline levels. These findings support the pharmacodynamic action of glepaglutide in SBS patients.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Pivotal study EASE-1

The primary endpoint change in weekly parenteral support volume from baseline to week 24 was larger for glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly compared to placebo (estimated difference of -2.3 l/week (95% CI: -3.8;-0.7); p=0.0039), but not for glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly compared to placebo (estimated difference of -0.9 l/week (95% CI -2.5; 0.7); p=0.27)(treatment policy estimand). Similar trends were observed in sensitivity analyses with respect to the primary endpoint and another estimand using a hypothetical strategy. No notable subgroup effects were identified in the EASE-1 study.

At least 20% reduction in weekly parenteral support volume from baseline to both weeks 20 and 24 was observed in 65.7%, 45.7%, and 38.9% of study patients, who were treated with respectively glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly, glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly, and placebo. The reported proportion as compared to placebo treatment was higher for glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly (difference 26.6 (95% CI 4.3; 48.9) p= 0.02) but not for glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly (difference 5.5 (95% CI -16.2; 27.1) p= 0.63). The reported proportions appear to be comparable or higher for the same endpoint in SBS patients who were treated with another GLP2-analogue (teduglutide) (teduglutide 0.05 mg/kg/day: 45.7%, teduglutide 0.10 mg/kg/day: 25%, placebo: 6.3%) compared to SBS patients who were treated with glepaglutide.

At least 1 day/week reduction in the days on parenteral support from baseline to week 24 from baseline to both weeks 20 and 24 was observed in 51.4%, 34.3%, and 19.4% of study patients, who were treated with respectively glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly, glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly, and placebo. The reported proportion as compared to placebo treatment was higher for glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly (difference 31.7 (95% CI 11.4; 51.9) p= 0.0043) but comparable for glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly (difference 13.3 (95% CI -5.4; 32.0) p= 0.17).

Weaning off from parenteral support was observed in 14.3%, 11.4% and 0% of study patients who were treated with respectively glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly, glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly, and placebo. The reported proportion as compared to placebo treatment tended to be higher for glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly (difference 14.1% (95% CI 2.5; 25.6); p= 0.02) and glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly (difference 11.2% (95% CI 0.7; 21.6); p= 0.04).

In line with aforementioned trend of a reduction in parenteral support upon glepaglutide treatment in SBS study patients, the mean citrulline concentration (a biomarker for small bowel enterocyte mass (Crenn et al. 2000)) tended to increase in both glepaglutide treatment groups but not for placebo in the EASE-1 study, with the greatest increase observed for the glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly group. With glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly dosing, the increase from baseline was significantly greater vs. placebo (p=0.0025). The dose-dependent increase in citrulline levels provides clinical evidence that glepaglutide is acting via the GLP-2 receptor to increase enterocyte mass, supporting the expected intestinotrophic effect of glepaglutide on the intestinal epithelium. In line with this, in the EASE-2 study, the mean change from baseline in plasma citrulline at month 6 tended to be larger for glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly (11.5 µmol/l) compared to glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly (7.0 µmol/l).

The mean percentage change in body weight from baseline to week 24 was -0.27% for glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly, 0.41% for glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly, and -0.46% for placebo. There were no specific trends across treatment groups with respect to shifts between BMI groups during the treatment period of the study. Similarly, the mean body weight remained comparable to baseline in both glepaglutide arms in the EASE-2 study. Also, in the EASE-3 study, mean body weight remained stable during the study.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In the EASE-1 study, the Patient Global Impression of Change (PGIC) was much/very much improved in 48.6%, 31.4%, and 5.6% of study patients who were treated with respectively glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly, glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly, and placebo. The reported proportion as compared to placebo treatment tended to be higher for glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly (difference 42.9 (95% CI 24.8; 61.0) p&lt; 0.0001) and glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly (difference 25.4 (95% CI 8.3; 42.4) p= 0.006).

In line with the EASE-1 study, the proportion of patients who had achieved a reduction of at least 1 day/week on parenteral support at month 6 was higher in the glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly group (61.7%) than in the glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly group in the EASE-2 study (45.3%) (estimated treatment difference 20.3% (95% CI 2.5; 38.1); p= 0.03).

In a pooled analysis of study data from the EASE-1 and EASE-2 studies, reductions in parenteral support volume compared to baseline are maintained up to 21 months. The clinical effects of glepaglutide 10 mg twice daily are comparable in the EASE-1 and EASE-2 study despite a larger variability in the EASE-2 study relative to the EASE-1 study.

## 9.3.1. Uncertainties and limitations about favourable effects

Limited numbers of SBS patients have been included in the conducted clinical studies and their treatment arms accordingly (ntreated= 106 in the pivotal EASE-1 study). Considering the rarity and heterogeneity of the disease the inclusion of more patients e.g. on defined subgroups cannot reasonably be expected. Therefore, it is difficult to predict what patients will benefit (the most) from treatment with glepaglutide. These limitations are taken into account with respect to the interpretation of the study results.

The evaluation of the glepaglutide dose finding is limited. In the dose finding study, the studied regimens are 0.1, 1.0 and 10.0 mg daily. The results indicate a dose-response (PD) relationship with the highest dose resulting in the highest wet weight output. Despite these results in the pivotal study the evaluated regimens are glepaglutide 10 mg once and twice weekly, with glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly being the proposed dose in the SmPC. There is limited PK data to support the proposed dosing regimen and no robust dose-response relationship was observed based on PK data, so overall, the support for the proposed dose is limited. However, the proposed dose has been studied in the pivotal trial and as the data are considered reliable the proposed posology is accepted.

Limited numbers of SBS patients with intestinal insufficiency (SBS-II) have been included in studies ZP1848-15073 (n=5) and ZP1848-20060 (EASE-4; n=2). Hence, little data are available on the clinical effects of glepaglutide in SBS-II patients, however, the mechanism of action of glepaglutide is the same in SBS-II and SBS-IF patients. Therefore, the results obtained with a population SBS-IF can be extrapolated to patients suffering from SBS-II.

The reductions in parenteral support volume and also improvement with respect to the PGIC at week 24 as compared to baseline in favour of glepaglutide were not accompanied by a clear improvement in the scores for other quality of life parameters such as the SBS-Impact Scale (SBS-I) and the European Quality of Life-5 Dimension 5 Level (EQ-5D-5L). This is not considered to offset the benefit shown on parenteral support volume and PGIC.

Study treatment was injected into the abdomen or one of the thighs during the whole treatment period. In pharmacokinetic analyses, glepaglutide exposure (Cavg en Ctrough) was about 35% higher upon injection into the abdomen as compared to injection in one of the thighs. This difference is explained in part by 3 outliers in the study patients who received placebo treatment in the thigh. Additional sensitivity analyses showed a dose-response association for both injection sites, and the difference between glepaglutide twice weekly and placebo were statistically significant for both injection sites. Therefore, while PK results indicate that glepaglutide exposure is higher with abdomen vs thigh injection, the results are not considered to point toward any substantial or robust impact of injection site on the primary efficacy.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Reductions in parenteral support volume compared to baseline are maintained up to 21 months. Whether this also applies to time points beyond 21 months is unknown due to the limited study data in that time frame. However, long term effect can be expected to be followed up by the treating doctors on and individual patient level.

## 9.4. Unfavourable effects

In the placebo-controlled trial (EASE-1), administration of glepaglutide leads to an increased rate of adverse events (94% of the patients in both TW and OW groups) when compared to treatment with placebo (75% of the patients). Most of these events were mild and moderate in severity, however, 10 (29%) patients in the TW group and 5 (14%) in the OW group experienced at least 1 severe AE compared to 2 (6%) in the placebo group. Also, the frequency of serious adverse events was increased for glepaglutide (&gt;25% in both TW and OW groups) vs. placebo (19%).

The most commonly reported TEAEs and number of patients experiencing these were as follows: Injection site reaction (9 [26%] patients in the TW group, 15 [43%] in the OW group and 1 [3%] in the placebo group), nausea (7 [20%] patients in the TW group, 3 [9%] in the OW group and 1 [3%] in the placebo group), abdominal pain (5 [14%] patients in the TW group, 6 [17%] in the OW group and 2 [6%] in the placebo group), and injection site erythema (9 [26%] patients in the TW group, 15 [43%] in the OW group and 1 [3%] in the placebo group).

Although the frequency of events increased with time, the safety profile of the treatment continuation pool and the long-term treatment pool were largely comparable to the safety profile observed during the placebo-controlled trial. The safety profile of glepaglutide was mainly characterised by injection site reactions, gastrointestinal disorders, intestinal obstruction and stoma complications. This is in line with the pharmacological effects of glepaglutide, its main target organ and mode of administration. In addition, this is in line with the known safety profile of GLP-2 receptor agonists.

During the course of the Phase 3 studies serious adverse events related to the pharmacodynamic effects of glepaglutide occur relatively frequently: SAEs regarding intestinal stenosis/obstruction (11 events in 9 [7%] patients), abdominal pain (2 events in 2 [2%] patients), stoma complications (3 events in 3 [5%] patients), pancreatitis (2 events in 2 [2%] patients), and cholecystitis (2 events in 2 [2%] patients). All side effects are appropriately labelled in the SmPC.

In addition, in total 29 AESIs of neoplasms (benign and malignant) were observed in the clinical database. Of these, 19 events in 16 patients were classified as benign, and 10 events in 9 patients were classified as malignant/unclassified.

Death occurred in 7 patients, of which for one, which occurred in a patient diagnosed with liver metastasis from an adenocarcinoma most likely of colorectal origin, a role of glepaglutide cannot be excluded. Glepaglutide is contraindicated in active and suspected malignancy and the SmPC recommends colonoscopy at the time of starting the treatment and in specific intervals.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Discontinuation of treatment occurred in 17 out of 130 patients (13%) across all phase 3 studies (including EASE-4). The majority of AEs leading to withdrawal from the trial or treatment discontinuation are included as ADR in section 4.8 of the SmPC.

## 9.4.1. Uncertainties and limitations about unfavourable effects

The safety database regarding twice weekly exposure is limited, with only 42 patients being exposed to glepaglutide for at least 1 year. The size of the safety database, with 61 patients attributing to 100.0 PYO in EASE-1 and EASE-2 and only 42 patients having at least 1 year follow-up, is below the size recommended in the ICH E-9 guidelines on chronic treatment. The applicant confirmed that the final CTRs for EASE-2 and EASE-3 are expected in Q1 2027, which will contain additional safety data. The limited database hampers a thorough assessment of the safety profile with regards to the detection of less common adverse drug reactions; however, this can be considered acceptable in this rare disease. The database will be complemented further by long term data post authorisation which is acceptable.

Cholecystitis, cholangitis and cholelithiasis have been reported in the clinical studies of GLP-2 analogues. Accordingly, the SmPC recommends monitoring of liver and gall bladder function. If clinically meaningful changes are seen, further evaluation including imaging of the gallbladder and/or biliary tract is recommended and the need for continued glepaglutide treatment should be reassessed as outlined in 4.4 of the SmPC.

Pancreatitis has been reported in clinical studies with glepaglutide. The SmPC advises in 4.4 that in case of pancreatitis, the need for continued treatment should be reassessed.

Considering glepaglutide is a growth factor inducer and induces epithelial hyperplasia and based on the non-clinical findings it cannot be excluded that glepaglutide may have a tumour-promoting effects in SBS patients who receive long-term treatment with glepaglutide. In total, 29 AESIs of neoplasms (benign and malignant) have been reported across three clinical studies, EASE-1, EASE-2 and EASE-3, with 19 being classified as benign and 10 malignant/unclassified. 1 patient with multiple metastases of possible colorectal origin died during EASE-4. With the current data available, the concern of promotion of benign and/or malignant tumours cannot be fully excluded. To address this uncertainty the SmPC contraindicates glepaglutide in patients with active or suspected malignancy and in patients with a history of malignancies in the gastrointestinal tract, including the hepatobiliary system and pancreas within the last five years. Colonoscopies and upper-intestinal endoscopy or other imaging is recommended at start and during treatment in 4.4 of the SmPC.

In the long-term extension pool a high proportion (87.6%) of the patients developed antiglepaglutide antibodies (ADA) and in vitro glepaglutide neutralizing antibodies (47%). The ADA levels were seen to decrease again over time during continued treatment and no clear impact of ADA development on efficacy or safety was observed. In the proposed SmPC, safety mitigating conditions are described where glepaglutide treatment should be paused (and potentially restarted). Based on the limited data there is no suggestion of immunogenicity concerns following re-initiation of glepaglutide treatment after a pause.

In the EASE-1 study, most study patients had normal hepatic function at baseline. Study patients with hepatic impairment were excluded from enrolment into the EASE-1 study and from direct enrolment into the EASE-2 study. Approximately one-third of the randomized patients had mild impairment, and only 3 patients had moderate hepatic impairment. Therefore, data from this group is interpreted with caution and the SmPC raises this data limitation to the attention of the prescriber.
