## 9.6. Benefit-risk assessment and discussion

## 9.6.1. Importance of favourable and unfavourable effects

The goal of reductions in parenteral nutrition or even weaning off parenteral nutrition in SBS patients is to improve the condition of intestinal failure and thereby to reduce frequency of severe potentially life-threatening complication related to parenteral nutrition.

The study database for efficacy and safety is limited but acceptable due to the rarity of the condition. The observed pharmacodynamic effects of glepaglutide in the phase 2 and pivotal studies support the action of glepaglutide as a GLP-2 analogue.

The primary endpoint in the pivotal studies is the reduction of weekly parenteral support volume. This is a clinically relevant outcome parameter as time spent for patients on parenteral support can be extensive and have a high impact on their daily life. Days without parenteral support would constitute the biggest benefit.

At least 20% reduction (MICD as established in the teduglutide assessment) in weekly parenteral support volume from baseline to both weeks 20 and 24 was observed in 65.7%, 45.7%, and 38.9% of study patients, who were treated with glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly, glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly, and placebo respectively. At least 1 day/week reduction in the days on parenteral support from baseline to week 24 from baseline to both weeks 20 and 24 was observed in 51.4%, 34.3%, and 19.4% of study patients, who were treated with glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly, glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly, and placebo respectively. Weaning off from parenteral support was observed in 14.3%, 11.4% and 0% of study patients who were treated with glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly, glepaglutide 10 mg once weekly, and placebo respectively. The totality of the reported results can be considered clinically relevant.

The safety profile of glepaglutide observed in the clinical development programme was mainly characterised by the adverse events related to the pharmacodynamic action of glepaglutide and mode of administration, leading to injection site reactions, gastrointestinal disorders, intestinal stenosis/obstruction and stoma complications.

Almost all patients (&gt;95%) experienced a treatment-emergent adverse event, with the majority being mild to moderate. Whilst adverse drug reactions related to the pharmacodynamic action of glepaglutide lead to a relatively high burden of serious, and sometimes severe adverse events, including obstructive effects to bowel, biliary, and pancreatic epithelia as well as GI-tract polyps (including few events of gastric and small intestine polyps) this can be considered manageable by the prescriber with the implemented SmPC wordings.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Overall, although the burden of adverse drug reactions is potentially substantial, it is considered manageable, particularly through the mitigating measures described in the SmPC, and acceptable in light of the serious and disabling nature of intestinal failure with a considerable impact on quality of life and for which treatment options remain limited. The safety profile of glepaglutide is in line with its mechanism of action as well as that of prior developed GLP-2 agonists.

## 9.6.2. Balance of benefits and risks

The observed pharmacodynamic effects and clinical effects of glepaglutide in the pivotal study support the action of glepaglutide as a GLP-2 analogue. Reductions in weekly parenteral support volume were observed in favour of the glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly arm. The proportions of study patients who weaned off parenteral support tended to increase with time in the EASE-2 and EASE-3 extension studies.

It can be concluded that a clinically relevant reduction in weekly parenteral support volume in patients who were treated with glepaglutide is reported. At least 1 day/week reduction in the days on parenteral nutrition was reported for patients on glepatuglide 10mg twice weekly. Weaning off from parenteral support was observed in about 15% of the study patients who were treated with glepaglutide 10 mg twice weekly. The totality of the reported results can be considered clinically relevant.

The safety profile seen with glepaglutide is in line with the expectations from the knowledge of the mechanism of action. The safety profile of glepaglutide is characterised by adverse events linked to the mode of administration and pharmacodynamic action of glepaglutide which are generally predictable, manageable, and addressed through the mitigating measures included in the SmPC. This leads to a burden of serious and severe adverse drug reactions, including gastro-intestinal, hepatobiliary- and pancreatic adverse events which is however considered outweighed by the clinical effects reducing the weekly parenteral support and ideally weaning off this vulnerable patient population from parenteral nutrition and its complications such as catheter-related blood stream infections, deep vein thrombosis, intestinal failure associated liver disease (IFALD) as well as impaired quality of life (QoL).

## 9.7. Benefit-risk conclusions

The benefit / risk balance of Zeydovio (glepaglutide) in the treatment of adults with Short Bowel Syndrome (SBS) is positive. Patients should be stable following a period of intestinal adaptation after surgery as stated in the indication.
